The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2888. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2887. Bengals News: Should Jets RB Breece Hall be trade target for Cincinnati?

The Cincinnati Bengals have been linked through insiders to be checking in on a possible trade for running back Jonathan Taylor from the Indianapolis Colts, but another running back may be just as good of a target. The New York Jets may be looking to see what they can get for running back Breece Hall as he gets healthy from a quad injury.

Jason Buechler from Sports Illustrated also brought up a good point for Hall being a decent target for teams:

Hall is in the first year of a three-year contract, so this is more than a rental. Hall could slot into the Bengals' offense and take over the backfield immediately. With the Ohio team holding all its 2027 draft picks, including an extra third-rounder, New York could push for the Bengals' second-round pick.

While it is true that Hall and Taylor both have two years left on their contracts after this season (Hall has a couple void years attached to his), Hall's overall cap hit and age are much closer to what Cincinnati would prefer to take on. Not to mention the team could cut ties very easily before the 2028 season and save roughly $12 million in cap space, according to Over The Cap. Taylor's deal has a similar out, but his caph hit exceeds $20 million starting next season.

The other thing here to consider is the cost. A third round pick could get this pick done for a player who is still well within their prime while also costing less money. Taylor is a wonderful player, but considering the Colts would like a multiple pick return which would seemingly need to include a second round pick, it makes that a hard pill to swallow.

We are of course playing in the hypothetical space where the team had to choose one. I think a vast majority of fans would agree that adding a couple of players to the back seven would do worlds more to improve this team overall than a running back.

#2884. Team effort sees Nets beat Hornets, winning for first time at home, 114-104

One of the few silver linings to the Brooklyn Nets' 1-6 start was the supposedly easier stretch of opponents awaiting them after it. As Jayson Tatum game-winners, injuries, and second half collapses have all hacked away at early season morale, that was the only thing left to hold onto.

Like a jagged ledge in a damp cave, it was a ballsy thing to reach for. After all, the Hornets had beaten Brooklyn badly at home just two weeks ago.

Well, you know what they say. Audentes fortuna iuvat.

Brooklyn began this one with a starting five featuring Nolan Traoré, Chaney Johnson, Michael Porter Jr., Julius Randle, and Day'Ron Sharpe for a fourth straight game. Their first minutes brought mixed results. Day'Ron Sharpe picked up his first foul of the contest just six seconds into it, and then his second with us less than two minutes deep. That gave us a premature introduction to Mo Wagner.

But even if you discount the foul trouble, Brooklyn's early defense looked flimsy. Either due to slow rotations or off offensive rebounds, the Nets gave up warm-up threes for Charlotte during pre-game shoot around sessions.

Luckily for Brooklyn, even in those shoot around sessions, nobody makes 'em all. Charlotte missed all of 'em, beginning the game 0-of-7 from downtown. The Nets, benefitting from another assertive start at the other end from Nolan Traoré and more efficient one from Julius Randle, led until the 3:47 mark of the first. The Hornets went ahead on a four point play, after Naz Reid hit a three as Wagner got caught with his elbow in Grayson Allen's chest, which warranted a flagrant foul penalty. Don't you hate when that happens?

Had Mikel Brown Jr. not come in and convert an and-one finish and then a step-back three in Moussa Diabaté's face in back-to-back possessions, the Nets might've been down double digits after the opening frame. Instead, they trailed by just three.

MBJ kept the showing going in the subsequent quarter, hitting a turnaround jumper, dropping two assists, and taking a charge at the defensive end all within the span of three minutes. One of the dimes, a no-look, behind the back dish while soaring through the air. If you can't even conceptualize what that would look like, I don't blame you…

At the break, Brown Jr. had nine points, three assists, a steal, and a block in 13 minutes. He would finish with a career-high 18 points on 6-of-11 shooting along with three assists. For Brooklyn, he trailed only Randle at the halfway point, who had 14 points, three boards, and an assists. Michael Porter Jr. got hot late in the second, adding nine points and 3-of-8 shooting.

However, the Hornets maintained a seven point lead going into the break, even with three extra turnovers and after shooting 41% from the field against a Brooklyn defense that'd begun playing with an extra layer of physicality. As the Nets did better to shoot and take care of the ball better, Charlotte manufactured a seven extra points at the free throw line.

Apparently running New York's playbook from Monday night, the Hornets also hit an abundance of corner threes down the stretch of the second. Grayson Allen, who led the Hornets with 20 points at the break, got two of his five made threes in the half from that areas.

During the break, the Nets announced that Noah Clowney would sit out the remainder of the game with right knee soreness. Chaney Johnson still started the third with the original group, but that gave us our first non-garbage time minutes of the season for Danny Wolf, who came in for Johnson less than three minutes deep in the frame.

The new lineup quickly made a case for itself as a mainstay in Jordi Fernández's rotation. Capped off by a triple from Wolf himself, that group went on an 11-0 run in three minutes, giving the Nets a lead for the first time since the 5:42 point of the second.

"Danny coming in, you know, and contributing right away like that, that takes a lot of a lot of readiness and a lot of discipline to not play for so many games and come in in the second half and to contribute the way he did," Porter Jr. said. "I mean, I always thought Danny was a special player, and I've said that since he got here. I think he can do so many things at the basketball court, and I'm really happy that he got the opportunity."

"Obviously in the NBA, there's gonna be times, especially early in your career, that you might not be in the rotation, and that was the case when we started the year," Wolf said. "Everyone emphasizes and preaches that you have to be ready, and you hear it over and over, just make sure you're doing all your work and doing all of the things to make sure that when your name gets called, you're ready. And I tried taking advantage of all the time I had, and when the first called my name in the second half, I was ready, and I was looking for that opportunity and tried to do anything I could to help the team win."

The latter half of the third period then gave us some fun back-and-forth action as neither team managed to separate by more than a bucket or two. We even got a surprise cameo from Terance Mann, who's either played early or not at all so far this year, coming in with around three and a half to go in the frame. Like the quarter before, Brown Jr. also finished it scoring with a three he carved up for himself in isolation, which things 80-80 entering the fourth.

There, Brooklyn began the scoring with triples from Randle, Wolf, and Brown Jr. to extend a six point lead.

A few moments after his triple, Brown Jr. turned it over trying to sneak an interior feed in for Sharpe, and then left his assignment trying to steal the ball back, leading to an open dunk for Charlotte and a Fernández timeout. The shot-making was there, but so were the headaches, as they often are with the league's younger teams.

To win, the Nets would need proceed with one of those things and ditch other latter. Michael Porter Jr. got them off to a solid start in the right direction.

After Fernández's timeout, MPJ got in on the action with three of his own makes from deep across Brooklyn's next five possessions. He didn't do anything fancy to get them — and didn't need to — just ducking hard around screens and rising up to fire without hesitation. That was enough anything to keep it a multi-possession lead even as Reid put in nine points in the period's first five minutes.

"I just told the guys like at the beginning of fourth quarter, like let's be a team that learns from our mistakes, tries to not make the same mistake twice," Porter Jr. said. "We were in the same position a couple nights ago in New York and fell apart in the fourth quarter. So I mean, that's what good teams do, rowing teams do, is they learn from their failure and they try to not do that same thing again. So I was proud of everybody."

The Nets went up 10 with 3:49 to go after Sharpe got up for a rejection that sprung Wolf on the break, who finished and-one through a back-pedaling Sion James. That rejection might as well have been an assist for Sharpe, but he got eight of those tonight anyway — a career high to go along with his six points and five rebounds.

"I think the biggest staple of our team, especially our offensive identity, is you're supposed to be playing fast," Wolf said. "We use the terminology 'pace and space,' and I feel like there's times when we do it great, and I think there's times with my play style that I can really help us push the ball up the floor, make simple passes, to start the break. I think you get those, and you get momentum, and momentum is what helps win a basketball game."

Without Kon Knueppel, who exited due to left leg soreness, and Brandon Miller shooting 1-of-12 from the field, the Hornets had no choice but to settle for Naz Reid isolation possessions. And the Nets, conscious by that point of the Charlotte's intention to hunt switches with their big man, were ahead, shading and dropping in defense in a timely fashion to limit their opponent's scoring.

Of course, Miller hit his first three of the game after a timeout a few sequences, but Traoré just kept on driving, and scoring, adding six points in the fourth. Fernández also won a big challenge to give Porter Jr. free throws that extended the lead to 12 with under two to go when we otherwise would have been Charlotte ball. He also rolled the dice closing with a lineup featuring Traoré, Mann, Porter Jr., Wolf, and Sharpe, as Randle only logged three fourth period minutes. Fernández credited the three-time All-Star for being "selfless" postgame.

"I called him in to sub in, and then they took it to 10 or 11, and Julius the one just like letting them roll," Fernández said. "So you see, every single instance of great things that have to happen in the game, and even a bit of being Julius, who is and how important it is for us, I think that that was huge."

Randle still finished with 22 points, four assists, and three rebounds while shooting 7-13 from the field. Porter Jr. led with 26 points while shooting 8-of-17 from the field. He also grabbed 10 boards. After Brown Jr., Traoré came next with 16 points on 7-of-11 shooting along with two assists and three steals. Wolf added 12 points, dished out three assists, and grabbed five boards while shooting 4-of-11 from the field.

That closing group also looked nothing like the uncoordinated and out of place team we saw last time out, running the clock down like a methodical NFL offense.

With all the active players logging minutes, almost all of them making an impact at one point or another, and Fernández pressing all the right buttons along the way, it was as good of a team effort as you've seen from this group in some time. It also got the Nets their first home win of the season.

Final: Brooklyn Nets 114, Charlotte Hornets 104

After the game, Coach Chris Brown, Mikel's father, had a message for fans: it's going to get better…

Day'Ron Sharpe had recorded a career-high eight assists in tonight's game vs. Charlotte.

Rookie Mikel Brown Jr. had a career-best 18 points on 3-of-4 from deep with four assists off the bench.

Michael Porter Jr. led Brooklyn with 26 points and 10 rebounds in tonight's win vs. Charlotte for his second double-double of the season. It was his 26th career game with 25+ PTS and 10+ REB after a career-high eight such games last season.

As mentioned, the Nets announced at halftime that Noah Clowney would be out for the remainder of the game with right knee soreness. We'll provide updates on him as they're made available.

Brooklyn begins its first legitimate road trip of the season next with a trip down south to face the Atlanta Hawks. At the time of writing, Atlanta is 4-4 with wins over the Pacers, Cavaliers, Hornets, and Magic, and losses against the Pelicans, Heat, and two to the Rockets.

The Nets have lost five straight to the Hawks. Their last win came in March 2025 in a game where Keon Johnson, Tyrese Martin, and Jalen Wilson started. This next one tips off on Saturday at 7:30 p.m. EST.

#2880. What Republicans' huge loss means for Trump

Democrats just put President Donald Trump in a bind.

The party is projected to win commanding majorities in the House and Senate after thrashing Republicans in key races up and down the ballot across the country. The blue tsunami will knock Washington on its head and crush the Republican congressional bulwark that both supported and shielded Trump through the first two years of his term.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The midterm result snuffs out any chance Trump had of passing partisan legislation in the second half of his presidency. And many of the interests he has been able to pursue without Congress' input — including tariffs, the Iran war and an array of Washington-area construction projects — are sure to face sudden pushback from newly empowered Democratic majorities.

The paradigm shift hardly leaves Trump helpless, however. As president at a time of executive power expansion, Trump wields singular authorities that could stymie Democrats' efforts to pursue their own agendas.

But with his grip on the Capitol shriveling and his approval ratings scraping record lows in numerous polls, it's unclear how much support he'll be able to muster to fight the coming political battles.

Even before Election Day, analysts and pundits had contemplated a Democratic rout so decisive that it would force a reckoning for the GOP, and potentially even pose an existential threat to Trump's "Make America Great Again" movement.

Marc Short, chairman of Advancing American Freedom and onetime chief of staff to former Vice President Mike Pence, told CNBC on Tuesday afternoon that the GOP has moved away from its conservative foundations.

"We need a refresh right now," Short said in an interview. "We've lost our way."

Trump has been vague about what he hopes to achieve with the rest of his term. But he has shared a vision of how he might coexist with a Democratic majority, and it involves heavy use of the veto pen.

"It's one of the stronger powers that a president has," Trump said in a Fox News interview overnight. "They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them."

"And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together," he said, adding, "I have a lot of friends that are Democrats."

Trump has previously stated his willingness to veto reams of legislation that Democrats send across his desk. In a New York Post podcast interview released Monday, Trump said that if Republicans don't win, "I veto everything."

Pressed to detail his plans for the next two years, Trump touted his record and said, "The most important thing is to keep it going. It's going to get better and better and better, unless people come in that hate our country."

If that happens, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," Trump said.

He may soon get his chance: House Democrats, poised to be led by current Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned.

The legislative push, first reported by The New York Times, aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to introduce bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law earlier this year.

Democrats also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the war with Iran, or seek congressional authorization to keep it going.

But without Trump's approval, those bills may end up as little more than symbolic gestures, as Democrats will lack the two-thirds majorities in both chambers that are required to override a presidential veto without GOP help.

And there's no guarantee the bills will even make it out of Congress: Despite their landslide victories, Senate Democrats will still end up shy of the 60 votes required to bypass the chamber's filibuster rules for most legislation, giving Republicans key leverage in the minority.

Democrats also could feasibly benefit from Trump's vetoes in the long term. Forcing the president to shoot down numerous popular bills could give them fodder in the 2028 election.

It's far from clear whether Trump believes he can find compromise with Democrats on any of their initial legislative priorities.

But any potential for a working relationship with the opposition could be poisoned early on, as Democrats in both chambers are aggressively signaling their plans to launch politically charged investigations against Trump, his political and corporate allies and even his family members.

Winning control of House committees gives Democrats the ability to issue subpoenas under the current rules.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is likely to chair the House Oversight Committee in the next Congress, said his panel will target the Trump family's business dealings, corporate corruption and alleged abuses by federal immigration agencies. He also plans to return focus to the Trump administration's handling of files related to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, Garcia summed up his plans in a two-word statement: "Lawyer up."

It's not just Garcia. Other Democrats have already demanded records from the Trump administration on a range of issues. Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., for instance, last week accused Trump of using tariff exemptions to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

Senate Democrats have sent similar signals.

"They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing," Sen. Ed Markey, D-Mass., said in an X post late Tuesday in response to a post, appearing to refer to tech executives.

The investigations are widely expected to consume much of the rest of Trump's term.

Trump has ample experience being targeted by Democrats, who impeached him twice in the House during his first presidential term.

But with his base of support appearing to erode inside and outside of Washington, Trump may have to face his political foes with a smaller arsenal than he's used to.

"I'm not expecting Republicans to revolt" against Trump following the midterm losses, AAF's Short told CNBC. "But I'm expecting some of them to quietly distance themselves."

#2876. Blood banks targeted in latest European fridge cyberattacks

French blood banks were on Monday affected by the recent wave of cybersecurity attacks on European refrigeration systems, the French Blood Establishment confirmed on Wednesday.

The organization said that while it wasn't the direct target of the attack, cold storage units operated by external providers containing the EFS' blood products were compromised across three of its sites, with hackers cranking up temperatures.

"Specifically, temperature and defrost thresholds were set to excessively high levels," the EFS said in a statement translated from French to English by CNBC.

"These cold storage units—located at EFS preparation and distribution sites—are used to store blood products prior to their delivery to healthcare facilities, as well as samples intended for EFS biobanks."

They also stored frozen plasma used for transfusions the manufacturing of medicinal products.

The EFS said that its surveillance systems were triggered rapidly, and depending on the situation, affected equipment was either brought under control with no impact on the products or samples, or they were taken offline. No samples or blood products were lost during the attack, with samples quickly transferred to other facilities, the EFS added.

The EFS is now coordinating with the French National Cybersecurity Agency (ANSSI) and the Ministry of Health and has increased monitoring of its equipment

Hundreds of supermarkets across France and other European countries, including Belgium, Sweden and Finland, had their fridges remotely hacked and temperatures raised, resulting in potentially millions of euros of spoiled food.

"Several regional facilities of the French Blood ​Establishment (EFS) were affected by a cybersecurity incident ​that impacted certain cold storage room monitoring and control ‌systems,"

The situation is now under control and is being closely monitored, the French Health Ministry said in a statement, according to Reuters. They added that the safety of the blood supplies was ​preserved "thanks ​to the ⁠facilities' monitoring and alarm systems, as well as precautionary measures immediately ​implemented."

#2872. Broncos focused on taking care of business against Panthers

The Denver Broncos have gone through their fair share of ups and downs to start their 2026 gridiron campaign. Nevertheless, the team touts a 6-2 record and has continued its penchant for gutting out close games and embarking on miraculous fourth quarter comebacks.

#2871. From tanks to drones: South Korea’s defense firms face a new battlefield test

South Korean defense stocks rode a wave of global rearmament over the past two years.

Russia’s invasion of Ukraine accelerated military spending across Europe. South Korean companies drew buyers with weapons that were competitively priced and could be delivered relatively quickly, including tanks, artillery and missile systems.

But that rally has given way to a sharp sell-off, with shares of several major South Korean defense companies plunging this year.

The combined market value of the top four Korean defense companies — Hanwha Aerospace, LIG D&A, Hyundai Rotem, and Korea Aerospace — had plunged to about 62 trillion won ($46.18 billion) as of end-October from their 52-week peak of 170 trillion won, according to South Korean broadcaster SBS.

Are South Korean companies, known for exporting tanks and artillery systems, well-placed for a modern battlefield that has seen drones and counter-drone systems proliferate in Ukraine and Iran?

The war in Ukraine has shown how relatively inexpensive drones can threaten expensive military equipment, and drone strikes from Iran have depleted stocks of costly air defense missiles of the U.S. and its Gulf allies.

"Modern warfare is being fought with less traditional weaponry (tanks, cannons, armored vehicles, etc.), which Koreans are positioned for. Even on this front, drones, for example, are dominated by China, pricing out other potential competitors from Europe, the U.S., and Asia," said Peter Kim, senior managing director at KB Securities.

KJ Hwang, Korea autos & industrials analyst, at Bank of America agreed that procurement priorities were evolving, with more focus on drones, counter-drone systems and air defense.

The reversal in defense stocks is not all down to new warfighting platforms.

The declines come as investors weigh several concerns: disappointing earnings, a stronger won and delayed export orders.

Fund flows into the semiconductor sector and high valuations built on expectations of strong earnings growth have also weighed on Korean defense shares, said KB Securities' Kim, adding that governments have moved slower than expected to convert defense spending pledges into approved budgets and orders.

Financial results have amplified those concerns. Hanwha Aerospace’s third-quarter operating profit fell over 46% from a year earlier, with the company citing delivery-schedule adjustments and one-off costs. Its shares are down more than 28% so far this year.

Hyundai Rotem, which reported a more than 11.1% decline in operating profit, has seen its stock drop almost 60%.

Korea Aerospace Industries’ third-quarter sales and operating profit improved about 52% and 32% respectively, limiting the decline in its shares to just over 8% this year.

Experts, however, are still positive on the sector, especially over the long-term.

"South Korean defense companies have continued to expand cooperation with partners in Western Europe and the Middle East, including in air-defense systems, while additional K9 howitzer orders suggest that supply constraints and the continued importance of deterrence remain supportive of demand for conventional platforms," BofA's Hwang said.

Some deals made this year include K9 orders to Spain from Hanwha Aerospace, valued at about 546 million euros, as well as a $922 million order for K239 rocket systems from Norway.

LIG D&A has made a $94 million deal with Malaysia for its K-SAAM naval surface-to-air guided missile system, the first export deal of the system ever made.

Countries still rely heavily on conventional weapons to bolster their defense capabilities, with KB Securities' Kim saying that the Korean defense sector "remains attractive, given the long-term growth outlook is still in the teens."

David Roche, strategist at Quantum Strategy, told CNBC that there is a "greater emphasis" on AI and drone technology in military orders as major powers learn the lessons of Ukraine, however, “drones cannot capture and hold terrain ... So, in any mobile battlefield, infantry, tanks, armor etc, are essential."

South Korean defense companies are also actively investing in drones and unmanned systems, and while meaningful exports will take time as overseas buyers require a proven operational track record, Kim expects more tangible results potentially emerging in the near future.

#2870. Russia, China may be more aggressive if they feel Trump is on 'his way out': Brookings

Russia and China could become more aggressive over the next two years if they conclude that President Donald Trump is politically weakened and on "his way out" after Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress, Brookings Institution senior fellow Robert Kagan told CNBC.

Speaking on "Squawk Box Asia," Kagan said the war with Iran had also exposed how Washington has "proved itself rather incapable militarily" in terms of capacity, which would be critical in Asia.

"Everybody can see how much our stocks are depleted in terms of interceptors and missiles, which are of vital interest in the Asian theater," he said.

Congressional Budget Office reports have estimated that the conflict depleted as much as two-thirds of U.S. missile-defense interceptors since June 2025, largely because of efforts to defend Israel against Iranian attacks. This could leave the U.S. with constrained inventories for several years.

Kagan said the deployment of three U.S. aircraft carriers to the Middle East had reduced the U.S. naval presence available in Asia, potentially giving China more room to act assertively in the region.

"I think Trump's only interest right now is in cutting economic deals with China. I don't think he has any real interest in confronting China, but that is going to lead China to be even more aggressive," he said.

In December, Trump approved an $11 billion arms package for Taiwan, but later held back on a second package worth about $14 billion, calling it a "bargaining chip" in talks with China.

China claims self-governing Taiwan as its territory. While Washington does not have a mutual defense treaty with the island, U.S. law requires it to provide Taiwan with the means to defend itself.

Former CIA Director William Burns said in 2023 that Chinese President Xi Jinping had instructed the military to be ready to invade Taiwan by 2027, while stressing that this did not mean Xi had decided to launch an invasion that year or at all.

Kagan's comments come as U.S., China and Russia discuss a possible trilateral summit between Trump, Xi and President Vladimir Putin at the APEC Leaders' meeting in Shenzhen later this month.

Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Mao Ning said Wednesday that Beijing was "in communication with relevant parties regarding a meeting between the three heads of state," although no agenda has been announced.

Putin had reportedly floated the idea of a three-way summit with Trump and Xi in October to discuss ending the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

"If it's clear that Donald Trump is on his way out, and the United States may be returning to something like normal, I don't want to overstate that, but they may feel both of them, both Russia and China, may feel that the time to move is in the next two years," Kagan said.

#2868. Snap shares fall over 10% on third-quarter revenue miss

Snap shares sank over 10% when the Snapchat parent missed on revenue and daily active users in its third-quarter earnings report Wednesday.

Here's how the company did compared with analysts' expectations:

Sales in Snap's third quarter jumped 14% year-over-year while net income came in at $56 million, compared to the $104 million net loss it logged the prior year.

Snap's third-quarter adjusted earnings came in at $383 million, topping the $337 million that analysts were projecting, according to StreetAccount.

The Snapchat parent said fourth-quarter revenue should come in between $1.95 billion and $1.99 billion. The midpoint of that figure is higher than Wall Street's estimates of $1.95 billion.

Fourth-quarter adjusted earnings are expected to come in between $490 million and $525 million. Analysts were expecting that figure to be $498 million.

Snap executives said in an investor letter that the company plans for globally monthly active users to become its "primary audience metric" starting in its first quarter for 2027. The company also said it has 977 MAU for the quarter.

"Daily engagement has historically been stable as a percentage of monthly engagement, but we do not intend to optimize Snapchat around daily use at the expense of long-term value for our community," the investor letter said. "A longer-term horizon better aligns our reported metrics with the product and safety decisions that strengthen relationships, create lasting utility, and support broader, more durable audience growth."

Snap chief commercial officer Ronan Harris told analysts during an earnings call that lower spending by unspecified consumer packaged goods, or CPG, companies impacted third-quarter sales. Harris attributed the curbed CPG spending to "macro pressures in the U.S. and also the Middle East.""As we look ahead, we're seeing very positive signs of growth from diversified verticals like financial services in the U.S.," Harris said. "So part of our strategy here is to look outside of the pressures in CPG and find traction in these other verticals."

#2867. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2865. Inside the chip talent war: Trade secrets and rising Chinese rivals

A former Samsung Electronics employee who later joined Chinese memory chipmaker ChangXin Memory Technologies, or CXMT, was reportedly sentenced by the Seoul High Court on Oct. 22 to five years in prison for leaking Samsung's technology.

The sentence was reduced from seven years at his first trial in April. The former employee had joined CXMT in 2016.

The case highlights an intensifying battle over semiconductor know-how as Chinese chipmakers narrow the gap with established rivals, putting the race for top engineering talent increasingly at odds with efforts to protect trade secrets.

In 2023, a South Korean court granted Samsung's request to block a former DRAM researcher from joining U.S. rival Micron. It ruled that the two-year non-compete was not excessive and was necessary to protect Samsung's technology and business information, JoongAng Ilbo reported.

A decade ago, Chinese memory chipmakers were not Samsung's biggest concern when it came to losing experienced engineers.

Instead, the South Korean company was more focused on employees leaving for established rivals such as SK Hynix and Micron, according to a former employee who asked not to be identified because the information was private.

Samsung has been monitoring where certain departing employees went — to signal to remaining staff that moving to competitors would be difficult — according to a former employee.

The company also used non-compete agreements restricting employees from joining rivals for two years, as part of their employment contracts, with senior employees holding valuable technical expertise likely facing closer scrutiny.

But Samsung's concern extended beyond individual departures.

"It wasn't just about one employee leaving. The company wanted to send a message to those who stayed that moving to a competitor wouldn't be easy," the former employee said.

Meanwhile, Chinese semiconductor companies have sought to close the technology gap by recruiting experienced overseas engineers with high salaries, stock options, housing and family support, according to Jeongku Choi, a senior analyst at Counterpoint Research.

Their expertise has helped Chinese chipmakers overcome technical bottlenecks, particularly as U.S. export restrictions have limited China's access to advanced chipmaking equipment, Choi said.

Once viewed as unlikely to catch up with established players, Chinese manufacturers are now seen as "meaningful competitors," she added.

Counterpoint's Memory Tracker for the second quarter of 2026 projected CXMT's share of global DRAM revenue would reach 10%.

Established chipmakers are strengthening legal protections and internal security measures while adjusting compensation packages to retain key engineers, according to Choi.

South Korea has also designated certain semiconductor manufacturing technologies as "National Core Technologies," raising the penalties for unauthorized leaks of those technologies, Choi said.

The legal battle could intensify as Chinese memory companies expand beyond their home market.

"I believe that this management of trade secrets will expand its scope even further to IP and legal areas once Chinese memory companies move beyond the Chinese market and begin to expand their exports," Choi said.

The growing competition is putting greater focus on how semiconductor companies protect the knowledge accumulated by engineers working on complex manufacturing processes.

In Taiwan, home to the world's largest contract chipmaker TSMC, protections for trade secrets have evolved alongside the semiconductor industry’s growing strategic importance.

Jamie J. Yang, a partner specializing in antitrust and intellectual property at Innovatus Law, said Taiwan limits post-employment non-compete agreements to two years and requires employers to compensate former employees at least half their average monthly salary during the restricted period.

Yang added that the complexity of semiconductor manufacturing makes it difficult for any single engineer to possess enough knowledge to replicate an entire production line elsewhere.

"There is just very little chance that one person ... will know enough to replicate that line in any of the other companies," Yang said.

Moves by engineers between rival semiconductor companies could increase modestly as competition for top talent intensifies, while trade secret litigation is also likely to rise as firms seek to protect their technological edge, she added.

In Singapore, Zechariah Chan, a partner at law firm Lee & Lee, said protecting trade secrets can be particularly difficult in the semiconductor industry, where proprietary knowledge often involves intangible manufacturing techniques and methods.

Proving that a competitor has used proprietary manufacturing techniques can also be difficult, as outsiders generally see only the final product, according to Chan.

"There is no bright line between an engineer's expertise and proprietary information," he said. General skills and knowledge stay with the engineer, while trade secrets remain with the company.

In Singapore, non-compete clauses are enforceable only if they protect legitimate business interests and are reasonable in scope, geographical area and duration.

In Hong Kong, courts generally take a restrictive approach to non-compete clauses, enforcing them only when they protect legitimate business interests and go no further than necessary, according to Jezamine Fewins, head of litigation at Lewis Silkin in Hong Kong.

Courts are more willing to protect trade secrets through court injunctions, Fewins said.

"The difficulty is that often the trade secret is carried in the employee's head," she added. Information is more likely to qualify as a trade secret if its disclosure to a competitor could cause significant harm to the former employer and the company has taken steps to restrict access.

For chipmakers, Fewins said the key is to clearly define what constitutes a trade secret, make employees aware of that information and put safeguards in place to limit access.

Samsung and CXMT did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

#2864. Patriots set for measuring-stick opportunity against Packers

The New England Patriots have a big opportunity ahead of themselves against one of the NFC's top teams this Sunday when they welcome the Green Bay Packers to Gillette Stadium.

At 5-3, both teams enter Sunday looking to keep pace in competitive divisions while strengthening their respective position in the playoff picture. For the Patriots, it also presents a chance to prove they can compete against a quality opponent from the other conference.

"This is a big game for us. This is a huge game," captain Kevin Byard said. "To be real, we haven't beat an NFC team just yet. This is going to be a huge challenge for us as a team. I almost want to say a measuring stick game."

The similarities between the two teams extend beyond their records, making Sunday's matchup a significant test for a Patriots team looking to establish itself as a legitimate playoff contender.

"Both these teams are trying to keep pace with the other teams in the division," Byard said. "So, I think both coaches preaching the same thing. This is, I'm not going to call it a must win, but we need to get this win at home and we've been really good at home at Gillette, so we got to keep that going."

Quarterback Drake Maye sees it similarly.

"For sure. It's a playoff team from last year. A playoff team with a great defense, a really good offense, a good quarterback," Maye said. "Then, for us, it's another NFC North opponent that's been someone that we don't play very often, but has been historically, in the past couple years, a playoff football team. They're good, they've beaten good teams, and they continue to show up every year. We've definitely got a challenge and a chance to see how we look."

Green Bay presents a difficult challenge on both sides of the ball. Despite an offense that ranks in the bottom 10 in points per game (21.4), the Packers have shown an ability to generate explosive plays through the air. They lead the league in pass plays of 20 or more yards, with quarterback Jordan Love continuing to push the ball downfield to explosive pass catchers in Christian Watson and Matthew Golden.

Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel then noted that Green Bay's defense has generated the second-most negative plays in the league, while a league-high 17% of its drives have ended in a turnover.

While Byard stopped short of calling the game a must-win, outside linebacker Dre'Mont Jones made it clear that the Patriots understand the importance of continuing to stack victories. With 10 AFC teams currently holding at least four wins, there is little room for the Patriots to let opportunities slip away.

"We need to win," Jones said. "I mean, we got to win to stack up W's in that column so we can figure out where we're going to be when it comes January and February, hopefully."

#2863. Jim Cramer warns investors may be reading too much into Wall Street's post-election rally

CNBC's Jim Cramer warned Wednesday that Wall Street may be getting ahead of itself following the midterm elections.

Stocks rallied after Democrats scored a decisive victory in the midterms, but the gains masked a divide within technology. Hyperscalers surged as investors anticipated that greater opposition to data center development could slow the AI buildout and ease spending pressures. Meanwhile, semiconductor stocks and data center developers came under pressure. Cramer cautioned that investors may be drawing premature conclusions about the election's economic implications.

"The midterm results controlled today's trading, but by Friday we will be talking the Labor Department's nonfarm payrolls report, and we could see a repeal of so much of this move that I hesitate to guess where we go next," the "Mad Money" host said.

"But I do like the word guess," he added. "Because that sums up what I saw throughout today's session and it's not the stuff I want to base investment decisions on, even as it's terrific for a one-day trade for the ages."

Apple shares hit an all-time high Wednesday as investors bet that a slowdown in data center construction could ease memory chip prices, which have been pressuring the company's margins. Microsoft and Amazon also rallied on expectations that a slower AI buildout could reduce data center costs and rein in their massive capital spending.

Conversely, investors sold shares of companies whose growth depends on continued AI infrastructure investment, including chipmakers Nvidia and AMD and data center developers CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren.

But Cramer questioned whether a single election could fundamentally alter the forces driving the market. "I want you to understand that, as important as a midterm election can be, and this one is important, the elections did not upend the business cycle," Cramer said.

Cramer noted that he's aligned with JPMorgan strategist Michael Cembalest, who publishes the closely followed Eye on the Market commentary.

In his latest piece Wednesday, Cembalest wrote, "The business cycle drives markets, and the important components are things that midterms do not affect: 10 yr interest rates, commodity prices, Fed policy, corporate profits, budget deficits, defense spending, the pace of AI investment over the next 12 months, the ferocious borrowing needs of AI companies and the impact on credit spreads, etc."

Cramer said it's important that investors understand that short-term market moves may not always align with the actual fundamentals. "Never confuse what happens in the stock market with what happens in reality," he said.

Cramer's Charitable Trust, the portfolio run by CNBC's Investing Club, owns shares of AAPL, AMZN, MSFT, and NVDA.

#2862. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2861. Amazon’s ‘Terrell & T.O.’ examines Terrell Owens: a man and a showman

The Prime Video documentary Terrell & T.O. is unlikely to change anyone's opinions on Terrell Owens.

If you're entrenched in your belief that Terrell Owens is a narcissistic ego monster who wrecked team chemistry, that will remain your viewpoint. If you're adamant that he is a misunderstood Hall of Famer who loves to have fun, that will remain your assessment.

After watching this documentary, the fairest and most accurate depiction may lie in the middle. Terrell & T.O., which is available to stream on Nov. 5, revisits one of the most polarizing figures in NFL history. Owens didn't merely see himself as a football player, but as an entertainer as well. He coined the phrase "Get your popcorn ready" because he was a showman. He was the epitome of the diva wide receiver: highly productive and high maintenance.

As difficult as Owens could be, his greatness is undeniable. When his career ended after the 2010 season, Owens ranked second all time in receiving yards (15,934), behind only his former San Francisco 49ers teammate Jerry Rice (22,895). He was surpassed for second place by Larry Fitzgerald (17,492). Owens is also third in receiving touchdowns (153), trailing Rice (197) and Randy Moss (156).

And yet, the controversies surrounding Owens are what most people think of first. This is even though he never got into any criminal trouble and was readily accessible to the media. To be fair, Owens brought a lot of negative publicity on himself with some of the things he said and did. But the media also treated him unfairly at times, particularly Skip Bayless. Owens waited three years before NFL voters finally selected him for the Hall of Fame Class of 2018. That's a joke.

In Terrell & T.O., Owens goes into more detail about why he decided not to attend the Hall of Fame ceremony in Canton. Instead, he made his acceptance speech at his alma mater, UT Chattanooga. Owens was a third-round pick from a small school who reached the heights of his profession. The T.O. we know today is not who Terrell Owens was growing up.

Director Alexandria Stapleton shows viewers a different side of the Owens story that some may not know. Growing up in tiny Alexander City, Tennessee, he was raised by his grandmother and mother. There were family secrets Owens originally wasn't aware of. Those likely impacted his development. In his formative years, Owens was described as introverted. A sharp contrast to what he would eventually become.

An armchair psychologist could see how his upbringing contributed to his personality explosion as he rose to fame. Owens grew to love the attention that scoring touchdowns brought.

But his insatiable hunger for the limelight also caused problems for him, his teammates, and coaches. His turbulent 2-year stint with the Philadelphia Eagles defined his career. He was lauded for playing in the Super Bowl with a broken leg. He also clashed with Donovan McNabb and Andy Reid. He was suspended for insubordination in 2005 while seeking a contract extension. The video of Owens doing shirtless sit-ups in his driveway in front of the media will live on forever in Philly sports lore.

Owens played for the 49ers, Eagles, Dallas Cowboys, Buffalo Bills, and Cincinnati Bengals. He excelled on the field throughout his career. But his greatness was always overshadowed by his antics and his reputation. In Terrell & T.O., it says a lot that two of his former quarterbacks, Steve Young and Tony Romo, speak highly of him.

"From my perspective, he was a great teammate," Romo said.

Terrell & T.O. will be available to stream starting on Nov. 5 on Amazon Prime Video.

#2860. Qualcomm stock drops 7% on weak earnings forecast as memory shortage weighs on smartphone sales

Qualcomm reported quarterly results on Wednesday that topped Wall Street estimates, but the chipmaker's earnings forecast came in lighter than expected. The stock slid 7% in extended trading.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates:

For the current quarter, Qualcomm said it sees earnings per share of between $2.15 and $2.35 on revenue of $10.4 billion to $11.2 billion. Analysts expected earnings per share of $2.27 on revenue of $10.48 billion.

Most of Qualcomm's chip sales come from system-on-chips and modems for smartphones. It's a market that's currently in turmoil as rising memory costs force handset makers to raise prices, hurting demand. Adjusted company revenue fell 7% during from a year earlier.

Qualcomm raised prices in September. Still, handset chip sales during the quarter fell 26% to $5.14 billion, trailing a StreetAccount estimate of $5.21 billion. Qualcomm said it's starting to see sequential growth in the category, and that Android shipments in China have bottomed, even though the company predicts the handset market will be flat in 2027.

Under CEO Cristiano Amon, Qualcomm has tried to turn investor attention to other kinds of chips. The company is looking to enter the fast-growing market for AI data center infrastructure. It had $650 million in data center revenue during the quarter, mostly from customizing and connectivity work, Amon said.

Amon said Qualcomm expects $5 billion in data center revenue in fiscal 2027. He said the company recently signed a customer for optical connectivity and had engaged a global hyperscaler.

"We don't have a demand issue; it's a supply issue," Amon said. "If I had more supply, I would probably be doing better."

Qualcomm also sells chips for cars and other automotive uses. Revenue in that division soared 89% on an annual basis to $1.99 billion, beating StreetAccount expectations of $1.66 billion.

Internet of things revenue includes low-power chips, smart glasses SoCs, laptop chips, as well as the company's bid to provide cellular infrastructure. Earlier on Wednesday, Qualcomm announced a deal to provide technology to SpaceX's cellular buidlout. IoT revenue increased 4% from a year earlier to $1.88 billion, slightly higher than estimates.

Qualcomm's QTL division includes revenue from licensing its intellectual property for 5G and other technologies to other companies. Qualcomm said during the quarter that it had renewed licensing agreements with Apple, Huawei, and Transsion.

"QTL will maintain its scale going forward," Amon said.

QTL revenue sank 7% to $1.3 billion, in line with a StreetAccount estimate.

#2858. White House panel delays hearing on Fed's Lisa Cook to Dec. 14

A White House committee of inquiry postponed from Thursday to Dec. 14 a hearing with Federal Reserve Governor Lisa Cook for an investigation of whether allegations of false statements in mortgage applications warrant her firing by President Donald Trump, a White House official said Wednesday evening.

But it was unclear if Cook, whom Trump unsuccessfully tried to fire in August 2025, would agree to attend.

The official said the postponement came at the request of Cook.

Her lawyer Abbe Lowell last month said questions about the committee's fairness and objectivity would have to be addressed before the hearing could occur.

Lowell also said the hearing could not take place until December, at the earliest, because she "will be executing her official duties as Governor at financial and monetary policy meetings in Europe."

The New York Times first reported the hearing's rescheduling.

Trump in early October established the committee to investigate allegations that Cook made false statements related to "one or more mortgage instruments."

"The Committee shall investigate the allegations against Governor Cook and report to me whether there is 'cause' for Governor Cook's removal within the meaning of" a federal law that was cited in a Supreme Court ruling in June that temporarily blocked her firing.

The committee is comprised of Kevin Hassett, assistant to the president for economic policy, Andrea Lucas, the chair of the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, and Office of Government Ethics Director Keith Sonderling.

In a lawsuit challenging her firing, she argued that Trump's claim about the mortgage statements is pretextual, and that his real reason for trying to oust her is her refusal to agree to his demands that she vote for lower interest rates.

Trump created the panel more than three months after the Supreme Court ruled that the president, for now, could not fire Cook from the central bank's board.

The ruling kept in place a lower court's order preventing Cook from being terminated while the suit challenging her dismissal proceeds.

The decision did not determine whether Trump ultimately has the power to fire a Fed board member. But it said she could remain on the board as her suit was pending because she was not given due process to challenge her firing.

The committee of inquiry Trump set up may be designed to show the Supreme Court that Cook has now received due process.

#2857. Rapid Recap: Celtics 109, Bucks 102

Despite being down by 21 at halftime, the Milwaukee Bucks were able to battle back and outscore the Boston Celtics in the second half, 61-47, but ultimately fell short and could not secure their first road win of the year, losing 109-102. Tyler Herro led all Bucks scorers with 25 points, while Paul George led the Cs with 25 as well.

The game began with the Bucks scoring just five points—all from Jaime Jaquez Jr.—in the first four minutes while turning it over three times. The most optimistic fans (me) could watch and think, "Once they settle in, these shots will fall," but nothing was further from the truth. After Myles Turner hit his first three to make it 12-8, the Bucks didn't score for almost six minutes as the Celtics went on a 15-0 run; Nate Ament ended the drought with a corner three. I mean, Brayden Burries secured a couple of nice offensive rebounds, but it was otherwise a quarter to forget. The visitors looked out of sync and lacked any identity, down 28-14 after one.

The second quarter started at a much better pace, with Milwaukee going mostly blow for blow to keep the deficit at 14 through the first half of the quarter. Once Boston's starting lineup was back on the floor, though, they took the pace over again, and the Bucks looked handcuffed. By the end of the second quarter, the Bucks had committed 10 turnovers for 12 Celtics points, and the Bucks were lucky it wasn't more. Milwaukee was also totally ineffective in transition, with a points-per-possession of -7.4; ironically, their transition play has been one of their few strengths this year, but over two quarters, they couldn't find any rhythm there. Milwaukee also didn't attack the rim, with just five total shot attempts at the rim, converting only one. The lone silver lining in the first half was Myles Turner, who was really having a game: nine points, five rebounds, two assists, two steals, and a block. Still, the Bucks were down 62-41 at the half.

The second half started with a much faster pace, with a 12-5 run pulling the Bucks back within 14 and forcing Boston to take a timeout. Most of the scoring and pace came from Jaquez, who stormed out of the locker room and put up eight points in the first few minutes. The pace continued, led mostly by Herro, and at one point the Bucks made eight straight buckets from the field, including a sick Burries dunk that forced another Boston timeout with the visitors down nine, 74-65. Milwaukee looked like a totally refreshed team, crashing the boards and moving the ball exceptionally well, all while finally protecting the ball (only one turnover for the quarter). Milwaukee shot a white-hot 14/21 in the third, down 87-79 entering the final frame.

The Bucks kept fighting to start the fourth, largely thanks to Nate Ament. Ament looked impressive, playing incredibly effectively off-ball and helping his teammates find opportunities to score, while also hitting some big-time shots himself; he hit a triple from the top of the key and then a layup to tie it up at 91. Burries subbed in at the six-minute mark and helped recapture the lead with a sweet 14-foot floater as the game turned into a sludge fest, with the lead changing on what felt like every possession. Make no mistake, the Bucks had multiple chances to take the game, but could not execute when it mattered, unlike the Celtics. Boston was just way better at making the crucial play, converting just enough of their opportunities to stave off the Bucks' comeback.

Four: the number of turnovers the Bucks committed in the whole second half. Milwaukee looked like a different team, and their ability to move the ball efficiently in the second half was the biggest reason.

I've seen enough: Start Nate Ament at the four. I made a similar call in the preseason after Dieng had a solid outing, so this is becoming something of a habit for me, but Ament played with infectious energy tonight that helped keep the Bucks in it despite being down 21 at halftime. On the flip side: Kyle Kuzma is arguably more responsible for the last two losses than anyone else. Low effort, high turnovers, and not good enough at anything to justify starting him or closing games. Let Ament start and let him get battle-tested now.

#2856. Democrats flip dozens of GOP seats. Republicans haven't flipped a single one

Seven Senate seats. At least 24 House districts. Five governorships. Twelve state legislative chambers. By Wednesday afternoon, that's how far Democrats' historic midterm rout had reached.

Republicans, meanwhile, had yet to flip a single Democratic-held congressional seat, governorship or state legislative chamber, according to Associated Press data. Their remaining opportunities for congressional pickups are largely confined to two newly gerrymandered House districts that weren't even supposed to be competitive in the first place.

The lopsided results underscored the breadth of the GOP's shellacking, with Democrats breaking decades of Republican dominance in reliably red states and reshaping the balance of power from Washington to state capitols.

As of late Wednesday, nine House seats remained uncalled by the AP along with two Senate seats and two governors races. The one seat Republicans may have a chance of flipping that hasn't been redistricted ahead of this election is Maine's 2nd District, FiftyPlusOne head of research Mary Radcliffe said by email.

In Kansas, voters elected their first Democratic U.S. senator since 1932, ending a 94-year losing streak for the party. In Texas, Democrats toppled three-term Republican Gov. Greg Abbott and captured a Senate seat that had eluded the party since 1988. Republican-held Senate seats also fell in Iowa, South Carolina and Ohio, along with seats in Maine and North Carolina.

The same wave swept through governors' mansions. Democrats scored upsets in Georgia, Iowa, Ohio and Nevada — all states President Donald Trump won in 2024 — giving the party a majority of the nation's governorships for the first time since 2010.

Upheaval even reached state capitols, where Republican legislative majorities that had endured for decades fell like dominoes.

Democrats seized control of the Texas House for the first time in more than two decades, flipped both chambers of the Wisconsin Legislature and wrested the North Carolina House from Republicans after 16 years in the minority.

In Pennsylvania, Democrats captured the state Senate for the first time since before 1994, giving the party complete control of state government. They also reclaimed lower chambers in Michigan and Minnesota, restoring full Democratic control in both states. In Arizona, unofficial results pointed toward Democrats taking both legislative chambers for the first time in roughly 60 years.

Even where Republicans held on to legislative majorities, Democrats stripped away veto-proof supermajorities in Florida, Indiana, Kansas and Missouri. Not since the aftermath of Watergate in 1974 had the GOP faced a statehouse reckoning of this magnitude, according to the Democratic Legislative Campaign Committee.

The scale of the losses is all the more remarkable given how aggressively Republicans had tried to protect their power. At Trump's urging, GOP lawmakers spent months pursuing an extraordinary mid-decade redistricting campaign, redrawing congressional boundaries in Texas, Florida and North Carolina to tilt the electoral map in their favor.

Yet the gerrymandering push did little to blunt the Democratic wave. And the damage might not be over. Republicans were still fighting to hold Senate seats in Alaska and Mississippi, with several House contests also yet to be called Wednesday.

— Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2855. Zac Taylor gives first impression of Madrid ahead of Bengals practice

MADRID, Spain – The Cincinnati Bengals (5-2) have arrived in Spain and are preparing to face the Atlanta Falcons (3-5) on Nov. 8 at the world-famous Santiago Bernabéu Stadium.

Zac Taylor is in his eighth season as the Bengals head coach and has only taken his team overseas one other time back in 2019 when Cincinnati played the Los Angeles Rams in London at Wembley Stadium and lost.

Taylor spoke head of practice at the Real Federación Española de Fútbol (RFEF) and offered his first impressions of Madrid.

"We've been working a lot," Taylor said. "I think the players have probably gotten out a little bit more. But we came over here yesterday and just walked around the facility. Pretty neat, beautiful city from what I've seen so far. Look forward to experiencing it a little bit more over the next couple days."

The RFEF training facility serves as the home for the Spanish national soccer team. After he arrived in Spain, Taylor came over to the practice facility a day before practice to learn his new whereabouts.

Taylor was hoping to see the FIFA World Cup trophy at the RFEF as Spain is the reigning World Cup champions, but learned the trophy is working its way around Spain currently.

"I was here yesterday looking for it," Taylor said. "It's not here. It's traveling around Spain. I learned that. It's on a whole tour of Spain, but I did my best to find it. I know it's a big soccer country. That's their number one sport. I'm not quite sure how much they know about our game yet. Hopefully, they'll learn a lot over the course of this week. It's a fun one."

The Bengals traveled through the night on Nov. 3 and arrived in Spain in the morning on Nov. 4. Taylor gave his players the day off when they arrived, with practice scheduled for Nov. 5 and 6. Select players, including Joe Burrow, Tee Higgins, Ja'Marr Chase, Dexter Lawrence, Chase Brown, Ted Karras and Jonathan Allen will all speak to the media over the course of the next two days in Madrid.

Taylor says the team is back on schedule now that they're here. It was important to Taylor to get his team to Spain early enough in the week so that their bodies could adjust to the time difference. Spain is six hours ahead of Cincinnati time wise.

"I think this is the best compromise we found to make sure that we can get acclimated by Friday, get a really good practice where we feel better, and then play our best on Sunday," Taylor said. "We've all embraced that mindset, and I think it's working well for us so far."

The Bengals will hold a walk-through at Santiago Bernabéu Stadium on Nov. 7, the day before the game against the Falcons. Bernabéu is the second-largest stadium (83,186 seats) in Europe and is the home stadium of Real Madrid.

"I know the tradition of soccer here is unbelievable," Taylor said. "People who follow it are very passionate. I think to be able to be in a stadium where there's so much history with the team is cool for our guys to have a chance to see a stadium like that with the tradition of soccer has. We'll embrace and enjoy it."

#2854. Sean Payton says J.K. Dobbins remains "a huge part of what we're doing"

It's been a somewhat strange second year in Denver for veteran running back J.K. Dobbins. As coach Sean Payton tells it, however, everything is going as planned.

"He's a huge part of what we're doing," Payton told reporters on Wednesday regarding whether the team is managing Dobbins's workload. "So each week we look at — a lot of times who's the back that's in there with the hot hand. But he's been fantastic. So to answer your question: No, that's not a hot button topic with us here. It might be with you guys, but not with us. So we're good. We're good with the rotation."

Payton is generally using a three-back rotation, with Dobbins joining R.J. Harvey and rookie Jonah Coleman. Still, with Harvey on injured reserve for Sunday's showdown against the Chiefs, Dobbins got fewer chances than Coleman.

Coleman, who was on the field for 41 offensive plays, had 11 carries for 41 yards and four catches for 63. Dobbins played 13 snaps, getting seven carries for 13 yards and no targets in the passing game.

"Look, it's always easy to work two [running backs] and it becomes more challenging when you're trying to work three, but he's doing a great job," Payton said of Dobbins.

For the year, Dobbins has 94 carries for 306 yards in eight games, with eight catches for 27 yards. That's 333 yards from scrimmage — an average of 41.6 per game.

In 10 games last year, before he suffered a season-ending foot injury during a Thursday night game against the Raiders, Dobbins was averaging nearly twice that amount, at 80.9 yards from scrimmage per game.

Through it all, the Broncos have been linked to a potential trade for Colts running back Jonathan Taylor. While the prospect of acquiring a workhorse would cut against Payton's longstanding approach to use multiple running backs, Taylor's arrival would surely result in even fewer chances for Dobbins.
