#2853. Nets find way to finish this time for payback win over Hornets

Monday in the Garden, the Nets let a sorry fourth quarter spoil three solid ones.

But two nights later, they closed the deal.

This time the Nets held their poise and held on for a comfortable 114-104 victory over visiting Charlotte.

Michael Porter Jr. (left) and Julius Randle high-five during the Nets' 114-104 win over the Hornets on Nov. 4, 2026 at Barclays Center. NBAE via Getty Images

It was payback for a humbling season-opening loss against these same Hornets. And after a win in Indiana — and taking a lead into the fourth quarter on the road at the reigning NBA champion Knicks — there are signs of the Nets making strides.

"We were in the same position a couple nights ago in New York and fell apart in the fourth," Michael Porter Jr. said. "So, that's what good teams do, growing teams do, is they learn from their failure and try to not do that same thing again. So I was proud of everybody."

Porter had 26 points and 10 rebounds, taking over late. Julius Randle scored 22 while they got solid play from the young point guard tandem of starter Nolan Traore (16 points, three steals, plus-13) and rookie Mikel Brown Jr. (career-high 18 points, four assists).

But most auspicious was the Nets' fourth quarter offense. After imploding two nights earlier — when they coughed up a 25-5 run to open the final period, and shot 3-of-16 with seven turnovers — this time they looked like a cohesive unit.

The Nets (2-6) outscored the Hornets 34-24 in the fourth, shooting 51.2 percent and 6-of-11 from deep. And Porter — who had been scoreless in the fourth against the Knicks — had 13 points on 4-of-4 from behind the arc.

"Fourth quarter is winning time," Porter said. "You can play a good three quarters of basketball; the fourth quarter's a completely different game. You have to be more intentional with your cuts, your setups, the speed in which you play, your defensive intensity.

Mikel Brown Jr. celebrates after hitting a 3-pointer during the Nets' win over the Hornets. Corey Sipkin for the NY POST

"In the fourth quarter, you've got to be better at communicating; you've got to be more disciplined; be more alert. Offensively, we were a lot more connected, and we had a lot of intention and speed with how we played. That's what it takes in the fourth quarter in a close game. So if we can build off of that, that'll be great."

The Nets' defense was stout from the start, forcing six straight misses and a turnover to open the game.

A Randle 3-pointer gave the Nets a 40-37 edge with 5:42 left in the half.

They surrendered it to an extended 20-7 Hornets run, falling behind by 10 on a Grayson Allen 3-pointer with a minute left in the first half.

The Nets were still down 59-50 a minute into the second.

But they promptly reeled off 11 unanswered to take the momentum and the lead.

Danny Wolf (right) celebrates with Terance Mann during the second half of the Nets' win over the Hornets. Getty Images

Jordi Fernández switched up his rotation, partly because of need. With Noah Clowney forced out by right knee soreness, Fernández went to second-year forward Danny Wolf, and also replaced 6-8, 235 pound Chaney Johnson with guard Terance Mann. The extra ballhandling paid dividends.

Wolf (12 points, plus-17) capped the run on a 3-pointer off a Randle feed, giving the Nets a 61-59 edge.

Even after they coughed it up to fall behind 78-72, they seized it back with a 17-5 run that spanned the third and fourth quarters. Randle found Brown for a 3 that put the Nets ahead 89-83.

"We just came together in the fourth quarter, and we understood the assignment," Brown said. "This has been the theme of we haven't been finishing games and now we finished it."

This time — unlike two nights earlier in the Garden — they held their poise. And held on for the victory.

"It shows that we definitely went back to the film. … [The coaches] told us how to win a game and we went out there and executed it," Mann said. "This game could've very likely went how the game in the Garden went. We were in the same situation and we flipped the script."

#2852. Bank of England hikes interest rate for the first time since Iran war, warns inflation will near 5%

The Bank of England announced its first interest rate hike in over three years on Thursday, raising its key rate by 25 basis points to 4%.

The widely anticipated move marked the bank's first hike since Aug. 2023, and made it the last major central bank to embark on a tightening cycle since the U.S. and Israel went to war with Iran eight months ago.

The majority of the bank's Monetary Policy Committee voted for the quarter-point hike, with the motion passing by 8 votes to 1. The dissenting member voted to raise the bank's key interest rate by 50 basis points to 4.25%.

"The conflict in the Middle East is now in its ninth month," the MPC said in a statement Thursday. "Global energy prices have continued to rise from an already elevated level."

Policymakers said they now expected U.K. inflation to rise to "about 5%" by the middle of 2027.

"It is then projected to fall as the impact of rising energy prices on 12-month inflation rates fades, and still restrictive monetary policy bears down on inflation," they said.

Ahead of the decision being announced, markets had been pricing in more than a 99% chance of a rate hike, according to LSEG data. Traders were also pricing in a one-in-four chance of a further hike in December.

Bank of England Governor Andrew Bailey said in the November Monetary Policy Report that without "real progress towards settling the conflict in the Middle East... risks to energy prices, and so to inflation, are to the upside."

"Without news which softens the risks to inflationary pressures, I can see the argument for a further increase in rates and thereafter taking a more conditional approach, dependent on the evidence on energy prices and inflation dynamics," he said.

Data released earlier this month showed that the U.K.'s annual inflation rate jumped to 3.5% in September, driven by the surging cost of motor fuels.

As a net energy importer, the U.K. is particularly vulnerable to external energy shocks — such as the one caused this year by the Iran war.

The MPC's most hawkish member, Catherine L Mann, said she had voted for greater tightening than her peers because research pointed to "non-linear inflation risks."

"My recent votes to increase Bank Rate by 25 basis points represented a 'down payment' to lean against second-round effects, reinforce credibility, and uphold 'good policy'," she said in comments published in the MPC's policy statement.

"The economy has crossed the estimated statistical threshold into a high inflation-tight labour market regime where the asymmetric behavioural responses of households and firms support more robust second-round effects," Mann added. "A different research approach estimates that the economy may well have breached the policy credibility threshold, which worsens the policy trade-off... From a risk management and credibility perspective, a 50-basis point hike in Bank Rate is 'good policy'."

Yields on U.K. government bonds, known as gilts, had been rising before the policy announcement and continued to edge higher after the Bank of England's update. The yield on the benchmark 10-year gilt was last seen 2 basis points higher at 5.1546%, while the 2-year gilt yield rose by 4 basis points to trade at 4.782%.

The British pound, which hit a post-Brexit high on Wednesday, was flat against both the U.S. dollar and the euro on Thursday afternoon.

Scott Gardner, investment strategist at JPMorgan Personal Investing, said in a note on Thursday that the November hike was "hugely symbolic."

"This is a 180-degree turn from the start of the year when many believed interest rates would come down as UK inflation had been mostly tamed," he said.

"The path ahead remains uncertain with much depending not only on how oil trades but also on natural gas prices which have risen sharply over the past few months. Worryingly, there are tentative signs of second round inflation effects in the UK economy as we have seen an acceleration in goods and services inflation. The Bank will be wary of this but also conscious of being too aggressive with rate hikes which could stunt the positive UK economic growth we've seen of late."

#2851. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2850. Rheinmetall shares plunge 17% after outlook cut as military contracts stall

German defense giant Rheinmetall cut its full-year guidance late Wednesday, citing delays to military contracts and ammunition deliveries.

It marks the company's second outlook cut since August, when Germany scrapped plans to build warships in what would have been Rheinmetall's biggest order since World War II. The defense company now expects sales to rise to around 13.2 billion euros ($14.9 billion) in 2026, down from a prior outlook range of between 13.7 billion and 14.2 billion euros. It is expected to have an operating margin of around 18.5%, down from the previously expected 19%.

Rheinmetall said it revised its 2026 guidance due to delays in the German military's Arminius project, a major German military procurement program for Boxer armored vehicles, and setbacks in artillery ammunition acceptance testing.

The Arminius contract, now expected to be commissioned next year, involves vehicles manufactured by ARTEC, a joint venture between Rheinmetall and the Franco-German defense group KNDS.

Rheinmetall shares fell over 17% in early trading, bringing year-to-date losses to about 46%.

It follows an average annual gain of 119% over the prior four years as it became one of the main beneficiaries of ramped-up military spending by European governments amid Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.

RBC analysts called the announcement "clearly negative," citing concerns over the Boxer program's timing, scope and impact on medium-term guidance. They warned the news could fuel broader investor concerns about traditional defense technology.

"We suspect bears will see this negatively as part of the ongoing 'new-tech vs old-tech' sector debate," they said.

Rheinmetall said delays to the Boxer contract would also push expected advance payments into 2027, affecting its previous cash conversion target of over 40%, although it still expects positive operating free cash flow this year.

The company also flagged delays in expected fourth-quarter revenue from new business activities, particularly its partnership with Dutch defense technology firm Destinus.

The guidance cut came as the German company posted improving results, with third-quarter sales increasing 32% year-on-year to 3.15 billion euros and operating profit jumping 62% to 553 million euros.

New order intake was 2.89 billion euros, bringing its backlog to 78 billion euros by the end of the third quarter.

Despite being overshadowed by the outlook cut, Citi analysts called the third-quarter print "a strong set of Q3 results."

#2849. Glacier spikers get past Butte in 5 sets, make State

It's not about how you start, but how you finish.

Glacier had both going its way in a Western AA volleyball playoff victory over Butte, but the third and fourth sets left the Wolfpack with work to do in the 5-set victory at the Wolf Den Wednesday evening.

"I don't know why we have to go to five with Butte every single time," Glacier coach Jaimie Nelson said. "I am proud of my girls, there were a couple moments where they could have just let it slip away, but they decided to fight instead."

After splitting the first four sets, Glacier commanded the fifth set to take the victory and secure its spot at the State AA Volleyball tournament in Bozeman Nov. 12-14.

Allie Krueger opened the final set with a kill after the Pack scrambled to get the ball over the net earlier in the rally.

Then, Glacier added four kills split between Krueger and Kyla Gully to go up 6-0.

Butte earned two points on long serves from Glacier, but the Wolfpack had an instant answer each time. Madi Stevens served up back-to-back aces to help Glacier lead 14-2.

Cadence Graham sparked a little life into the Bulldogs with four straight points, but Glacier closed out the match with a 15-6 win in the fifth set.

Nelson said this may have been the most dominant set she has seen this team play.

"I don't remember one off the top of my head," Nelson said. "I think that was pretty much them saying, 'We are here to go and do what we have to do to get to our goal.' "

The start was just what Glacier was looking for also as the Pack sparked a run off of a Stevens ace to go up 16-13 and take set one 25-19.

That momentum continued into set two where Glacier jumped out to a quick 9-2 lead after Audrey Mitchell served an ace. Butte closed to 14-9 later in the set but got no closer as the Wolfpack took set two 25-17.

Set three is where Butte found its footing.

The Pack led 11-7 in the set when Elli Graham stepped up to serve. She guided the Bulldogs to 11 straight points and an 18-12 lead. They continued the run to win the set 25-14 and force set four.

Glacier had more early momentum in the fourth set, but again Butte had a rally. Stevens put Glacier up 15-8, then the Bulldogs scored four of the next five points.

Butte eventually tied the set at 19 and then used a Cadence Graham kill to take a 22-21 lead.

Glacier wasn't done though as they battled back from 24-21 down to extend the set thanks to a kill from Krueger.

The teams went back and forth before Butte sealed the win 30-28 with a kill from Avery Barsness tie the match at two and set up the deciding set.

"I felt like we could have done it a little cleaner and taken advantage where we could have," Nelson said. "But to take the momentum into the fifth with them and close it out, I was proud of that."

Glacier moves to 11-8 on the season with the win. Butte finishes the year 8-12.

Butte's statistics were not made available.

Glacier def. Butte 25-19, 25-17, 14-25, 28-30, 15-6

Aces — Glacier 10 (Madi Stevens 3, Audrey Mitchell 3). Kills — Glacier 67 (Allie Krueger 20, Kyla Gully 16). Assists — Glacier 53 (Stevens 51, Addison Thramer 2). Blocks — Glacier 7 (Chloe Farrell 4). Digs — Glacier 85 (Thramer 22, Aria Stewart 19).

#2847. Eagles’ new QB taught teammate a hard lesson: ‘Don’t tell (him) I said that’

PHILADELPHIA — Eagles wide receiver Darius Cooper knows from now on that he must wear gloves when catching passes from new starting quarterback Joe Milton.

Milton throws harder than most NFL quarterbacks, and Cooper wants to protect his hands.

During the 2024 NFL Combine, Milton tied Bills quarterback Josh Allen's combine record for the fastest throw ever recorded at 62 miles per hour.

"I would say I have pretty rough, sturdy hands," Cooper said on Wednesday. "But today during walkthrough, I ain't had no gloves on. Stung a little bit. Just a little bit. Don't tell [Joe] I said that."

Milton will become the third Eagles quarterback to start a game this season. Jalen Hurts started the first five games before being placed on injured reserve after blood clots were discovered in his lungs. Fourth-year backup Tanner McKee replaced Hurts as the starter for three games.

On Wednesday, three weeks after signing Milton off the Cowboys' practice squad, the Eagles named him their new starting quarterback. They benched McKee after he struggled in his first three starts, completing 72 of 114 passes for 638 yards, two touchdowns and two interceptions.

With the Eagles having lost six in a row, Milton is unlikely to save their season. He might not even be an upgrade over McKee, but the Eagles are making this change because they are desperate for a spark.

"Have you seen the guy's arm? It is a f—ing cannon," Eagles offensive tackle Jordan Mailata said. "Oh, my god. Pardon my language. You see it on tv, you hear about it when he was going through the draft, but then when you see it, it's a work of art, man."

The Eagles dropped to 2-6 after a 27-10 loss to the Washington Commanders last Sunday. McKee struggled for the third consecutive week to handle pressure and to make plays out of structure. The Commanders sacked McKee five times for a loss of 35 yards and intercepted one of his passes.

With Philadelphia's offensive line struggling in pass protection, the Eagles need Milton to extend plays with his legs and make creative throws outside the pocket to beat the New York Giants on Sunday.

"That's beyond me," Eagles tight end Dallas Goedert said about the team's decision to switch quarterbacks. "Obviously feel for Tanner in this situation. I don't think he played terrible, but I think they're trying to do anything to find a spark.

"It'd be nice if it wasn't out there and we could surprise them late in the week and maybe they panic a little bit. With his skill set, he brings something different to the table than Tanner did with his mobility. So we'll see how that works on Sunday."

A sixth-round pick of the New England Patriots in 2024, Milton has a starter-caliber arm, but his accuracy and decision-making are inconsistent.

The Patriots traded him to the Dallas Cowboys in 2025 for a fifth-round pick. One year later, Dallas waived him before the start of the 2026 season.

Milton will make his first NFL start against the Giants, but he has played in five games over the last two years. In those games, he completed 37 of his 53 passes for 424 yards and two touchdowns, and he recorded 66 rushing yards and one rushing touchdown.

"Joe is a really good player," Mailata said. "He's able to just plug and play. So there may be a couple changes to [the offense], sprinkle of change in there. But for the most part, Joe has pretty much picked up the playbook, and he's done a really good job last two weeks of diving head first. He actually does a really good job of communicating things. ... Even in the last two weeks, just trying to get caught up to speed with the lingo of things and how we communicate things up front."

It's been a dark season for the Eagles, but Milton is giving them their last bit of hope.

"With everything going on, he brings a good light to it," Mailata said when describing Milton's upbeat personality.

LeBron James controlled the tempo in spurts of the game. He was playing like that OG at the Y who's keeping up with the youngins on pure know-how. The Pistons did not have their sharpest defensive night, and Philly still only cracked 112. It was just one of the games. Detroit shifts their focus to another superstar in Luka Dončić. #2844. Full Count: Valkyries want to take the next leap

In Ariel Putnam's fourth season as Bigfork's volleyball coach, the Valkyries reached a landmark: The 2022 State B tournament. It was their first state appearance in 17 years.

Year 8 could bring another milestone. After reaching the State A in 2024, the Vals want to return this month and stay a couple days at Bozeman's Brick Breeden Fieldhouse.

State is always the goal, but building confidence in tough situations became a renewed priority this fall.

"Last year we had a very talented team, but every time we got into these very tough situations, we didn't know how to handle them," Putnam said. "We've really worked on it every day, putting ourselves in a tough situation in practice. We reward effort, and we're OK with an error."

Bigfork's junior-laden squad won 28 matches in 2025 before faltering at the Western A Divisional. Now senior-laden, the Vals went 11-1 in Northwest A play for a second straight season and are playing Corvallis to start this year's divisional on Thursday.

"The last two years we've had a couple key players here and there that we've lost," Putnam said. "But for three of my senior hitters, this is their third year on varsity."

Outside hitters Sofia Lowell, a junior, and Raegen Waller, a senior, never leave the rotation. Seniors Somer Campos and Lillie Suffia are the middle blockers.

Junior Payton Despain is the Vals' 6-foot setter; junior Kenley Wheeler plays libero. Seniors Olivia Carson and Emily Wisman are the defensive specialist and right-side hitter, respectively.

The squad cruised until Butte's Blocktober Tournament, finishing second in its pool before going 1-2 in bracket play with losses to Columbia Falls and Hamilton — a callback to 2025.

"Last year we were in the same situation," Putnam said. "We played really tough volleyball, and played well, but we lacked this confidence. They were playing a little behind the pace of play."

So the Valkyries had to re-regroup — and did, winning their next four matches without dropping a set. But there were tests.

In a key match at Columbia Falls on Oct. 27, Bigfork trailed 25-24 in the first set and 24-22 in the third. The Vals won both 27-25, with a 25-17 win in between.

"We've had the talent; what we've been working on is fostering an environment where the girls controlling their game," Putnam said. "How are we handling the huddle; how are we using our breath and how are we holding our heads high for our teammates?"

Campos had 10 digs at C-Falls; Lowell had eight. Campos hits a .334 percentage.

Add it up and the Valkyries have a No. 1 seed going to the Western A.

"I don't have one girl that I don't have full trust in get the job done," she said. "I feel very fortunate. They're very professional, they show up and they're very serious about volleyball."

#2843. Figma sinks after earnings beat as company prioritizes long-term growth with AI changes

Figma shares tumbled 7% in extended trading after the company reported third-quarter results and guidance that surpassed Wall Street's predictions.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Figma's revenue grew about 43% year over year in the quarter, according to a statement. Net loss narrowed to $81.3 million, or 15 cents per share, compared with a loss of $1.10 billion, or $2.72 per share, a year earlier.

The company's 12% adjusted operating margin exceeded the 6.1% consensus among analysts polled by StreetAccount.

Executives lifted the full-year forecast. Figma now anticipates an 11% operating margin, up from 9% as of August, and $1.497 billion to $1.499 billion in revenue, compared with $1.463 billion to $1.467 billion before. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $1.471 billion in 2026 revenue.

The company sees fourth-quarter revenue growth slowing to about 33%. Analysts polled by LSEG had predicted 28% growth.

Figma's announcement comes a day after highly valued design software startup Canva said it would cut 22% of its employees.

Shares have come down about 41% so far in 2026, while the S&P 500 index has gained 12%. Earlier this year, investors became concerned that artificial intelligence models could pose a competitive risk to Figma. Across much of software, AI panic has largely subsided as incumbents have posted persistent growth.

Figma wants to benefit from the rising adoption of AI to build websites and apps. During the quarter, the company started giving more AI credits to clients while maintaining prices.

"What that means is you're potentially trading off near-term revenue, investing in your customers, and then allowing yourself to then grow durably over the long term," Figma's finance chief, Praveer Melwani, told CNBC in an interview on Wednesday.

The number of weekly users working at organizations that spend over $10,000 on Figma who used AI credits increased by almost 50% during the quarter, Figma said in the statement. The share of work the company's own AI model performs doubled, and the rate of people undoing AI-generated work has fallen.

"If there's certain actions that we can power for our customers at lower latency, lower cost, or similar quality to third party, we're open to that as well," Melwani said.

Figma is increasing its focus on cybersecurity, said Dylan Field, Figma's co-founder and CEO. In recent months, AI agents have infiltrated systems at Hugging Face and other companies.

Clients aren't asking Figma to become a security vendor, but the company must scrutinize "the security of all of our vendors right now," Field said.

#2842. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2841. Democrats take Congress in a blue wave. Here's what it means for your money

With Democrats flipping control of Congress in the midterm election, the shift in power on Capitol Hill could reshape household budgets — from student loan bills and health insurance premiums to housing costs and taxes.

Experts say results show that rising costs were top of mind for voters.

"I see this election as a repudiation of the anti-affordability agenda from the Trump administration," said Daniel Bunn, president and CEO of the Tax Foundation, a nonpartisan, nonprofit research organization.

"Whether that's the conflict in the Middle East or the tariffs, just top to bottom, it's clear to me that was a real concern of the electorate," he said.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton said in an emailed statement that the Trump administration has delivered "tangible wins to make life more affordable for American families." Officials "will continue to put hardworking American's first with or without support from Democrats," he wrote.

Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and Senate, which could make it easier for the party to advance legislation. They can also use hearings and investigations to press federal agencies and companies to answer for policies and practices that impact consumers, and exert leverage over confirmation of President Donald Trump's nominees.

But turning campaign promises into changes people feel in their finances won't be easy. Trump can veto bills a Democrat-controlled Congress passes, limiting how much of their agenda could become law. Based on projections, Democrats will lack the numbers needed to override a presidential veto.

Any changes will also take time: Newly elected members of Congress won't take office until January.

Here are eight ways Democrats’ new congressional majority could affect your money.

"Not surprisingly, the Dems are going to be keyed in on affordability with respect to healthcare," said Zach Gaumer, vice president of health policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonpartisan think tank.

OBBBA made Trump's 2017 tax cuts permanent and added temporary tax breaks. These included new deductions for tip income, overtime earnings, seniors and auto loan interest, which will expire after 2028 without an extension.

Democrats may support extending these provisions. But as the Democrat-controlled Congress maps out its agenda, lawmakers will have to address the federal budget deficit. For fiscal year 2026, the budget deficit ballooned to nearly $2 trillion, the Treasury announced in October.

Newly elected Democratic senators James Talarico of Texas, Troy Jackson of Maine and Annie Andrews of South Carolina all campaigned on expanding the child tax credit. Earlier this year, Senate Democrats also floated ideas to expand the standard deduction and create income tax exemptions for lower-earning Americans — which are costly proposals, according to Bunn from the Tax Foundation.

"I've called these termites in the tax code," he said. "You cannot consistently narrow your base, especially for the income tax, and expect your spending priorities to be well-funded."

With Democrats in control of Congress, they are likely to hold hearings and subpoena Trump officials over the administration's student loan policies, said higher education expert Mark Kantrowitz.

One priority may be pressing the Education Department to fix the popular Public Service Loan Forgiveness program, which cancels the remaining federal student debt of eligible government and nonprofit workers after a decade. Over the summer, Trump officials reassessed repayment timelines, delaying some borrowers' forgiveness date by years.

Broad student loan forgiveness is unlikely: Trump could veto such a bill, and the Supreme Court blocked the Biden administration's plan. More attainable changes could include restoring tax-free treatment of forgiven student debt, raising the new borrowing caps and expanding repayment options — measures that may attract bipartisan support, Kantrowitz said.

Trump has also said he may support legislation capping federal student loan interest rates, a proposal that could give Democrats common ground in their push to ease borrowers’ costs.

Housing affordability was a key election issue, and Democrats will likely explore ways to deliver relief, such as introducing measures to help first-time homebuyers, according to an Oct. 30 analysis by Desjardins, a Canadian financial services company.

However, lawmakers generally have little direct impact on home prices, which are based on market conditions. Mortgage rates, too, are influenced by forces generally beyond congressional control. The yield on the 10-year Treasury, which remains higher than it was before the Iran war — due at least partly to concerns about inflation eroding the value of those bonds — influences mortgage rates.

"Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run," plus more housing supply and a "more dynamic labor market in the long run," said Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com.

The bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which automatically became law July 11 without the president's signature due to a constitutional provision, is "a model for what can pass in a divided Washington," Krimmel said. It includes measures to encourage housing construction, reduce regulatory barriers, expand financing options and limit purchases of single-family homes by large institutional investors.

The incoming class of senators will have to address Social Security insolvency.

The program's trustees project that the trust fund Social Security relies on to help pay retirement benefits, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, may be depleted in the fourth quarter of 2032. Without action from Congress before then, benefits could face an across-the-board cut of 22%.

A number of Democratic lawmakers and some Republicans have called for increased payroll taxes on high earners to help shore up Social Security.

A potential SECURE 3.0 bill — a follow-up to bipartisan retirement-savings laws passed in 2019 and 2022 — could help expand workers' access to workplace retirement plans like 401(k)s, for example, among a roster of other reforms, experts said.

"Trump promised to cap credit card rates at 10%," said Aaron Klein, a senior fellow in economic studies at the Brookings Institution, a think tank. "A Democratic Congress might be willing to do that."

Any such cap is likely to be closer to 36% than to the 10% cap Trump has sought, Jaret Seiberg, a financial services policy analyst at TD Cowen, an investment bank, wrote in a research note Wednesday. Lawmakers in the current Congress have proposed legislation with these thresholds.

A cap would likely be welcome news for low- and middle-income Americans, Klein said. They are more likely than wealthy households to use debt to finance their lives and to carry a credit card balance — and therefore owe interest on their charges, he said.

Cardholders might see other impacts of such a policy, such as lower credit limits and a clawback of certain card rewards, he said.

Democrats' takeover of Congress could stall efforts to put crypto-friendly regulations into federal law, but everyday investors shouldn't expect much to change immediately.

"Platforms will keep offering the products they offer today, and they still have friendly regulators at the SEC and CFTC who are approving an ever-wider range of crypto products," said Lee Reiners, a cryptocurrency regulation expert and lecturing fellow of economics at Duke University.

The election results could threaten the Clarity Act, which would give the Commodity Futures Trading Commission greater authority over cryptocurrency trading while reducing the Securities and Exchange Commission's role. The bill passed the House in July 2025 by a 294-134 vote, but failed to advance in the Senate in September on a 49-50 procedural vote. "CLARITY is dead," said Reiners, adding that he sees little chance of the bill passing during the lame-duck session before Democrats take control in January.

Any future legislation aimed at creating a regulatory framework for digital assets would likely need to address Democrats' concerns about investor protections and conflicts of interest involving Trump's crypto businesses, said Martin Mulyadi, an accounting professor at Shenandoah University.

The SEC and CFTC can also continue developing crypto-friendly rules without Congress. But those rules are easier for a future administration to reverse than legislation, said Marcel Miu, a certified financial planner and founder of Simplify Wealth Planning based in Austin, Texas. "I'd tell investors to size their crypto investments as if the rules could change repeatedly over the coming years," Miu said.

The Iran war has led oil prices — and those of gasoline, jet fuel, heating oil, diesel and other fuels refined from oil — to surge. That has trickled into higher prices across the U.S. economy, experts said.

Oil prices fell on Wednesday on hopes that the election outcome will push Trump to make a deal to end the war.

"Ending the war in Iran would be the biggest policy lever to reducing inflation," the Brookings Institution's Klein said.

A Democrat-led Congress could try to limit Trump's ability to continue the war, perhaps by cutting funding.

"Congress' biggest power is the power of the purse," Klein said. "Wars are expensive."

— CNBC's Lorie Konish and Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting.

#2840. Is Jacoby Brissett playing today for Ravens? QB’s status for 'Thursday Night Football'

Jacoby Brissett's debut with the Baltimore Ravens will have to wait until next week.

In the minutes after news broke that the Ravens were finalizing a trade to acquire the veteran quarterback from the Arizona Cardinals, further reporting indicated that Brissett wouldn't be around for Baltimore's Week 9 game. According to multiple reports, the Ravens and Cardinals will not officially complete their trade until the weekend.

Baltimore head coach Jesse Minter announced Tuesday, Nov. 3 that undrafted free agent rookie Joe Fagnano would make his first career start against the Jacksonville Jaguars on "Thursday Night Football." Since Brissett won't officially join the Ravens until after the game, Fagnano is poised to remain the starter despite news of the pending transaction.

JACOBY BRISSETT: Baltimore Ravens make trade for quarterback after Lamar Jackson injury

Arizona and Baltimore were perfect trade partners for Brissett on paper.

The Cardinals benched the veteran midway through his eighth start this year after he underperformed against a poor Cowboys defense. Backup Gardner Minshew led Arizona to a comeback victory in overtime after entering the Week 8 game.

Meanwhile, the Ravens' quarterback situation was in dire straits. After Lamar Jackson sustained a thumb injury in Week 8 that would land him on injured reserve, backup Tyler "Snoop" Huntley suffered a season-ending toe injury in the same game.

Now Brissett, 33, is set to join the seventh different team of his career when the trade becomes official. Before his Cardinals stint, the veteran previously spent time with the New England Patriots, Indianapolis Colts, Miami Dolphins, Cleveland Browns and Washington Commanders.

Here's what to know about Brissett's Week 9 status ahead of "Thursday Night Football":

Brissett is not yet officially on the Ravens' roster, so he is ineligible to play for the team on "Thursday Night Football."

Baltimore will not finalize its trade for the veteran quarterback until the weekend, according to multiple reports. Until the transaction becomes official, Brissett will remain on the Cardinals' roster.

As a result, the Ravens are sticking with Fagnano, their undrafted rookie quarterback, for the start against Jacksonville on Thursday, Nov. 5. In his first career game last week, Fagnano was 10-of-21 (47.6%) passing for 91 yards and an interception against the Bills. He also rushed three times for three yards.

Brissett was having a decent start to his 2026 season before struggling against the Cowboys in Week 8. Here's how his year has looked by the numbers:

Rushing: 29 attempts for 87 yards (3.0 yards per attempt)

Baltimore has two active quarterbacks on its roster ahead of Week 9. Fagnano is the only one who has been with the Ravens since the start of the season – Slovis signed with the team on Tuesday, Nov. 3, following the injuries to Jackson and Huntley.

Both Jackson and Huntley – the Ravens' top two quarterbacks just last week – are expected to land on injured reserve in the coming days. Jackson's thumb injury requires surgery and will keep him out for at least four games. Huntley is reportedly out for the season with his turf toe injury. Skylar Thompson, whom the Ravens signed earlier this year, is also on injured reserve with an undisclosed injury.

Outside of the quarterbacks listed, Baltimore also has Austin Reed on its practice squad. Reed, who played in the UFL in the spring, was on the Ravens' training camp roster before his release ahead of the regular season. He re-signed with the Ravens' practice squad on Monday, Nov. 2.

#2837. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2836. NFL Week 9: How to watch every football game this week during the 2026 season

If you buy something through a link in this article, we may earn commission.

Week 9 of the 2026-27 NFL season is here, featuring matchups between the Jacksonville Jaguars and Baltimore Ravens, the Buffalo Bills vs. Minnesota Vikings and and the full slate of Sunday games.

Are you ready to watch some football? This season, NFL games will air across 10+ channels and streaming platforms. If you're worried about the challenge of following your favorite team through the upcoming season, from the NFL season opener to the 2027 Super Bowl, Yahoo Sports has you covered. Here's your playbook for how to watch the 2026-27 NFL season.

TV channels: ESPN, NBC, CBS, FOX, ABC and NFL Network

Streaming: Prime Video, Peacock, ESPN+, Paramount+, NFL+, Netflix

Jacksonville Jaguars vs. Baltimore Ravens: 8:15 p.m. (Prime Video)

Cincinnati Bengals vs. Atlanta Falcons: 9:30 a.m. (NFL Network)

You could also spring for the uber-expensive NFL Sunday Ticket package to get every out-of-market Sunday game of the season.

When it comes to nationally broadcast games, the NFL typically airs on ESPN, NBC, CBS, FOX, ABC, and NFL Network. Thursday Night Football games stream exclusively on Amazon Prime Video; select football games stream exclusively on Peacock; games on CBS stream live on Paramount+ Premium; and select games stream on ESPN's App this season. For those keeping count, that's six channels and four streaming platforms to keep up with this season. Plus, Netflix is once again hosting at least two Christmas Day matches, a playoff game, and the first-ever Thanksgiving Eve match, so don't forget to add that subscription into the mix.

Confused? You're not alone. Here's a breakdown of the platforms we recommend checking out ahead of the 2026 NFL season, so that come game time, tuning into your favorite team's games will be as easy as simply turning on the TV.

To recap, here are all the ways you can watch NFL games in 2026.

YouTube TV gets you nearly every channel you need to watch the NFL, including Fox, NBC, ABC, CBS, ESPN and NFL Network. Plus, YouTube TV is the home of NFL Sunday Ticket. The Sunday Ticket package, while pricey on its own, gets you ALL the Sunday out-of-market games (160+ games per season).

Current pricing for new users for the 2026 Sunday Ticket is $30/month for eight months. Factor in the cost of YouTube TV's Sports and News plan at $49.99/month for your first five months, then $71.99/month thereafter.

That's $79.99/month for five months of YouTube TV + NFL Sunday Ticket, and $101.99/month for the following three months. You can also opt, with or without a YouTube TV subscription, to pay for the rest of the season of Sunday Ticket upfront for just $240.

You can find more details on the discount here.

Fubo's Sports+ News package gives you access to ESPN, NFL Network, Fox, ABC, CBS, NBC, and 20+ more live channels. Starting at $54.99/month for your first month, the live TV streaming service is definitely pricey (though certainly not the most expensive option on this list), but it offers most channels you'll need to watch both the NFL preseason and regular season.

DirecTV's Choice tier (currently $104.98/month after fees for your first three months) gets you access to nearly every channel you need to watch NFL games throughout the year: NFL Network, ESPN, NBC, CBS, Fox, SEC Network, and an abundance of local RSNs. DirecTV Choice (and most other DirecTV plans) also come with free ESPN Unlimited, which gets you access to every ESPN channel, an amazing value and a must for watching tons of college and pro sports all year round.

Amazon Prime Video is home to the NFL's Thursday Night Football. On top of Amazon Prime Video, an Amazon Prime subscription includes free shipping, exclusive deals, access to the Prime Day 2026 sale event, Amazon Music, a year of free GrubHub+ and more.

A standard Amazon Prime subscription is $15 monthly or $139 annually, but discounts are available for students and those on qualified government assistance. You can try Amazon Prime free for 30 days.

NFL+ Premium, while a little pricier at $14.99 per month (or $80 annually), also includes access to NFL RedZone, plus full and condensed replays of every game across supported devices. NFL+ also offers a 7-day free trial.

#2835. Chasing the Final 4: Wildcats, Bulldogs in quarterfinals

Kelly Houle knew what last name to look out for before turning on Hardin's film.

In the late '90s, Houle coached against the Bulldogs' quarterback Cale Nedens' dad Chris in wrestling. Now he's watching Cale slip out of the hands of tacklers and wondering how many Wildcats it'll take to contain him.

Nedens is the reigning Class A wrestling champion at 150 pounds; he brings the Bulldogs (8-2) to Columbia Falls (7-1) for a quarterfinal matchup at 7 p.m. on Friday. The Wildcats earned a first-round bye with their second straight Northwest A title. Hardin beat Lewistown 22-14 on the road for its first playoff win in 14 years.

Houle and his staff charted 61 runs in 70 plays in Hardin's victory over Lewistown. By Houle's count, Nedens carried the ball 42 times for about 300 yards.

"They just found something they liked," Houle said. "They kept doing it and doing it, and they were successful."

Houle likened Hardin's attack to a Wildcat offense. Nedens takes the snap and looks for a crease, and a play that seems bottled up can turn into a long run. He can throw it, too. In a 30-22 win over Dawson County, Nedens passed for 111 yards to go with 245 rushing yards and three scores on the ground.

"He has a tendency to get in there, bounce around, all of a sudden he squeaks out the other side," said Houle.

Staying in their gaps will be a priority for linebacker Trip Ross and the Wildcats. When Columbia Falls has the ball, Ross gives Hardin a runner to worry about, too.

Ross ran 11 times for 114 yards, including a 51-yard touchdown, in a 42-0 win over Libby to close the regular season. He also caught one of Banyan Johnston's four touchdown passes. Johnston threw for 212 yards and ran for a score, and Jory Hill caught five passes for 118 yards and two touchdowns.

Houle sees room for Hill and the other slot receivers between Hardin's linebackers and safeties. He also wants to use Johnston's legs more in the playoffs, giving the Wildcats another option alongside Ross.

"We're just going to take advantage of the green grass that they give us," he said..

Columbia Falls spent most of its bye week cleaning up its own play. Houle liked what he saw enough to give the Wildcats an unofficial record through five sessions.

Friday's winner will face the winner of Frenchtown at Sidney in the semifinals. Frenchtown handed Columbia Falls its only loss, 28-13 on Sept. 4.

Brett Bollweg watched the East Helena Vigilantes' quarterback run and their receivers stretch the field. He recognized a few things from his own sideline.

"It feels a little bit like us," said Bollweg.

The Bulldogs (9-1) visit East Helena (7-1) at 7 p.m. on Friday. Both teams took their only loss by three points this season against Columbia Falls.

The winner of Friday's matchup will face either Billings Central or Hamilton in the Class A semifinals.

The Vigilantes ran for 419 yards in their 34-14 win over Lewistown to clinch the Central A title. Running back Leo Longcake carried the ball 36 times for 215 yards, and quarterback Bearek Shuman ran for three touchdowns, including a 60-yard score. Bollweg also singled out receivers Conor Carpenter and Parker Taylor for what they can do downfield.

"They run the ball well," Bollweg said. "They're good up front."

Whitefish leaned on its own front in last week's 36-12 win over Dillon, running for 248 yards and finishing with 449 yards of offense. Luke Dalen completed 17 of 23 passes for 201 yards and a touchdown and ran 15 times for 108 yards and three scores. Vance Schwaiger added 104 yards on 19 carries, including a 23-yard touchdown. Keenan Curtiss caught three passes for 56 yards, including the screen that put Whitefish ahead for good.

"Up front is where we won the game tonight," said Bollweg after the Dillon win.

This week, Bollweg sees an East Helena defense whose linebackers play downhill. He said Whitefish can use some of that aggression to its advantage with plays already in the book.

Dillon led 12-7 early in the second quarter before Whitefish scored the final 29 points.

If Friday's game is close late, Bollweg likes how his team has handled those moments this season. He pointed to tight games against Hamilton and Columbia Falls and the way the Bulldogs responded when they fell behind Dillon last week.

"There was never a moment where our guys panicked," Bollweg said. "There was never a moment where our guys flinched."

Reporter Luke Schmit can be reached at 406-758-4441 or [email protected]. If you value local journalism, pledge your support at dailyinterlake.com/support.

#2834. Knicks 135, Bulls 105: Scenes from the World Champs looking like it

After 21 years at our Binghamton mansion, we're moving. That means Mrs. Russell has countless extra chores for your intrepid blogger, including the retrieval of a loveseat in a sketchy neighborhood just before tip-off of tonight's game between the Knicks (4-4) and the Bulls (1-7). Planning to watch the game after delivering the furniture to the new mansion (we are fabulously wealthy), I tried to ignore the alerts blowing up my phone. An impossible task. My eye caught "Jalen Brunson is on track to score 76 points" and "84 ties a franchise first-half record," and all the surprise of tonight's blowout was spoiled.

What did I see when I finally returned home? Brunson blazin', OG Anunoby gorging at the free-throw line, KAT recording his eighth straight double-double, a very promising Dalton Knecht debut (10 PTS, 7 RBS, 16 MIN), solid minutes from Diawara, and a 44-point lead with 43% shooting from deep. We'll need to burn sage around that loveseat to rid the stench of resentment. Final score: 135-105.

Landry Shamet started in place of Josh Hart (sore ankle) and scored the team's first four points. From there, the Knicks went straight into high gear, turning a 6-6 tie into a 17-8 lead by attacking the paint and shooting with stunning efficiency. Who was the commander of the first-quarter onslaught? Captain Jalen Brunson, scoring his first 14 points on seven shots and finishing the frame with 22. That wasn't his highest first-quarter total—he dropped 23 on Milwaukee in January 2025. Nonetheless, stunning. Cap had all but six of the Knicks' first 19 points. Not to be kept in the corner, OG Anunoby contributed seven, including four from the stripe.

Mohamed Diawara checked in around the middle of the quarter, and this time he looked good! The sophomore delivered some of his best minutes yet this season, with a three and a block on Wilson's layup. Fellow bench buddy Jordan Clarkson added five points, including a sweet transition three (dimed by Brunson). In a flash, the lead reached 22. Karl-Anthony Towns contributed just two points, starting slowly for his second straight game, but he helped in other ways, notching four boards, a steal, and an assist.

What's your camp: "I Stand with Mikal" or "Mikal's Got Me Sweating"? The struggling wing did little in the first quarter to calm our nerves about his recent play. He scored no points but dished two dimes and grabbed two boards.

For Chicago, Normal Norman Powell managed four points, while Tre Jones added four off the bench. Chicago got little from its young players, though. Rookie Caleb Wilson must've been star-struck in his MSG debut, missing all four of his shots, and Matas Bazookas went scoreless. At the break, the Knicks were sitting on a comfortable 43-21.

Q2: More of the same. Our heroes outscored Chicago 10-5 over the first three minutes to extend their lead to 53-26. The unrelenting Anunoby was still in beast mode, scoring six points and forcing two Josh Giddey turnovers. New York's 3&D specialist scored 11 points in the quarter to reach 18 by halftime, while his three steals helped keep Chicago twitching and glitching.

With Brunson resting, Tyler Kolek ran the offense effectively, collecting four assists with sweet setups for Bridges, Clarkson, and Shamet. What a wonderful security blanket a skilled playmaker off the bench is. With Kolek dishing, Bridges racked up seven points. Diawara got into the action, too, scoring his first points of the season and finishing with seven first-half points.

Chicago managed a paltry little run early in the period, but New York responded with a 9-4 run that pushed its lead to 62-36—and eventually 78-41 with 1:47 left. Brunson added four points after returning, reaching 26 by halftime, while Towns remained quiet, adding just three more points in the second. You can count on KAT to crash the glass, though, and he did so for two more rebounds, giving him six at halftime. And newest 'Bocker Dalton Knecht made a fine first impression with a putback dunk and a step-back three-pointer for five points.

Chicago's Josh Giddey had a particularly rough quarter, committing three turnovers in the opening three minutes, although he recovered to finish the half with seven points and four assists. Bahzelis finally got going late, scoring all six of his first-half points in the final 1:06, including a three-pointer with six seconds remaining. Yee-awn. Wilson managed five points in the quarter, while Powell added just two, and Chicago found itself bereft of consistent scoring threats or prayers for winning this contest. Halftime score: 84-52. That tied New York's franchise record for first-half points.

The Knicks had scorched the nets with 63% shooting from the floor and 50% from deep. The Bulls had shot 40% and a grotesque 19% from deep (5-of-26). The good guys also won the rebounding battle, 23-17, despite surrendering eight offensive rebounds, and committed just five turnovers to Chicago's eight. Weirdly, the Bulls actually outscored the Knicks in the paint, 30-26, and attempted 13 more shots, yet trailed by 32 at halftime. Math was not my strong subject, but that doesn't compute. Anyhoo, on to the second half!

Yet again, KAT pepped it up at halftime and came out firing. He drilled consecutive three-pointers in the opening minute as the home team goosed its lead to 36. Towns scored 12 points in the first five minutes of the quarter, including a putback layup, and reached another double-double with 17 points and 12 rebounds before heading to the bench.

Brunson added seven points, including a pull-up three, to reach 33 and call it a night.

The Bulls continued to fumble and bumble. Wilson missed his first three shots of the quarter and committed another turnover. Boozelis scored six points in the period, but the Bulls couldn't make much headway against New York's enormous lead. Meanwhile, Deuce McBride provided a spark off the Knicks' bench, picking off passes from Norman Powell and Wilson before hitting a transition three-pointer that extended New York's lead to 42. McBride later converted a four-point play, giving him seven points in the quarter, while Mohamed Diawara contributed another blocked shot and continued to provide promising minutes on both ends. Brother, imagine if the Knicks could play Chicago every night. . . .

Tre Jones was Chicago's most productive player late in the quarter, scoring eight points over the final 2:19, including a three-pointer and three free throws. His unit closed the period on a 12-2 run (the Knicks got a little sloppy), but New York still ended the quarter up 113-79.

Hey! Check out this Knecht kid, blocking Buddy Hield's layup attempt in the closing seconds. It's gonna be fun to watch him go on heaters in Westchester.

With the Knicks leading by 34 entering the fourth, the reserves got the run of the joint. Andre Drummond opened the quarter with an alley-oop, while Clarkson and Knecht knocked down threes. James Wiseman made the most of his 10 minutes, finishing with eight points, eight rebounds, and three blocks, including rejections of Giddey, Bewzelis, and Patrick Williams. More of that, please! Pacôme Dadiet added four points. The Bulls briefly cut the deficit to 32, but Wiseman answered with a putback dunk and Knecht followed with a jumper. New York's offense slowed with the end-of-bench guys on the floor, and Chicago won the quarter 26-22, but who's complaining?

Mo's knitting a cozy recap to keep you warm. New York travels to Cleveland for a clash with the Cavs. Safe travels, Knickerbockers.

#2833. OpenAI pledges to halt NDAs in new data center policy proposal after election rebuke

OpenAI reaffirmed its promise to pay the full cost of powering data centers as part of a series of commitments the company published Thursday, two days after voters at the polls roundly rejected the rapid expansion of AI.

The artificial intelligence company said in its commitments that for data centers it owns and operates, it will solicit community input, publish construction plans and stay away from using non-disclosure agreements with public officials. Additionally, in a policy blueprint, OpenAI encouraged policymakers to enact similar commitments as laws.

"What we're doing with this blueprint is really laying out the best lessons that we have learned and committing to applying them as they go forward," OpenAI Chief Global Affairs Officer Chris Lehane told reporters during a Wednesday briefing. "This will help ensure that there is a very high floor in terms of how people move forward."

Other tech companies, including Meta, Microsoft, Google and Anthropic, have outlined several similar commitments.

On Tuesday, Americans issued a stinging rebuke of data centers at the polls. Out of 41 local ballot measures across 10 states, voters backed restrictions or rejected pro-development measures for all but one, according to an analysis by Ballotpedia and additional results gathered by CNBC on Wednesday.

Opposition to AI data centers mounted swiftly over the summer across both sides of the political aisle. Consumers grappling with inflation, rising grocery prices and stubbornly high prices at the pump grew worried that the facilities could exacerbate environmental harms, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss. For many, they became a physical manifestation of the widespread antipathy and anxiety around AI.

AI companies like OpenAI depend on AI data centers to serve customers and develop new models, and they're seeking to build out as rapidly as possible to meet demand. In February, OpenAI told investors that it's targeting $600 billion in total compute spend by 2030, and the company has since announced massive data center projects in Georgia and Ohio.

OpenAI has repeatedly pledged to protect ratepayers and invest in local communities this year, and Lehane said the company's latest commitments build on many of its previous initiatives. He said the policy blueprint has been in the works since August, and that he met with dozens of lawmakers and candidates since then.

"Having these conversations now actually helps put more energy into the momentum, so that when people come back in late January, new Congress is in in session, a lot of the work is being done in advance," Lehane said.

#2832. What the Democratic midterms sweep changes for U.S.-China relations

BEIJING — Democrats taking power from Republicans in Congress raises risks for U.S.-China tensions around Taiwan, while hardening tech competition, analysts said.

The Democrats swept this week's midterm elections, taking majorities in the House and Senate and flipping governors' offices in places like Texas, Nevada and Iowa. Most of the new election winners, however, won't begin their terms until January.

Before then, U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping will likely maintain their fragile truce by meeting twice: in Shenzhen this month, and in Miami, Florida, next month.

"As a lame duck who is increasingly focused on his personal legacy rather than the Republican Party's success, Trump may resist increased pressure for confrontation with China and continue his pursuit of 'strategic stability,'" Gabriel Wildau, managing director at Teneo, said in an email.

High on the Democrats' agenda is removing Trump's tariffs, as part of a broader focus on addressing domestic inflation, CNBC has learned.

"On balance as a result of the midterms the Democrats may nudge the U.S.'s China policy in a more hawkish direction through hearings and legislation, particularly on economic security issues and Taiwan," said Scott Kennedy, senior advisor and trustee chair in Chinese business and economics at the Center for Strategic and International Studies.

But any efforts will "be constrained by the executive branch's wide range of latitude and China's growing power and leverage, which can't be easily undone," he said.

Beijing has hosted a flurry of global leaders this year, while major economies have struggled to slow a surge of imports from China. Chinese dominance in rare earths has given Beijing leverage in trade talks with the U.S. and the EU.

"Taiwan remains the most dangerous issue in U.S.-China relations and, in my view, the area where a Democratic Congress is most likely to challenge Beijing," wrote George Chen, partner and co-chair of digital practice at consultancy The Asia Group.

"Congress has repeatedly shown a willingness to pursue Taiwan initiatives even when administrations are more cautious," he said, noting Trump's balancing act will likely become more difficult.

Nancy Pelosi's visit to Taiwan while House Speaker in 2022 angered Beijing, which considers the democratically self-governed island part of its territory. The U.S. maintains unofficial relations with Taiwan under a "One China" policy.

Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.), who is poised to become the first Black Speaker of the House in U.S. history, met in 2023 in New York with Tsai Ing-wen, who was then president of Taiwan.

During Trump's trip to Beijing in May, Xi warned that mishandling Taiwan would put U.S.-China relations in "great jeopardy."

Maintaining stability over Taiwan has grown more critical in the artificial intelligence race as the island is home to semiconductor giant TSMC, which manufactures many of Nvidia's chips.

Over the last several years, the U.S. government has tightened restrictions on the ability of Chinese companies to buy advanced chips from Nvidia, spurring Beijing's efforts to develop its own version of the technology.

U.S. stocks surged Wednesday after Trump accepted the Democrats' election wins, and oil prices fell following indications Washington would do more to end the Iran war. Apple and most "Magnificent Seven" tech stocks surged, despite declines in Nvidia.

But there's an "emergence of a broader consensus in Washington that America's technological lead should not be used to strengthen China's military or strategic capabilities," Chen said, noting a Democratic Congress may be motivated to be more aggressive.

"As a result, Beijing may discover that technology becomes a growing source of friction even if Trump and Xi continue to maintain relatively cordial summit diplomacy," he said.

The tech outlook ultimately depends on bigger questions about AI.

Chinese tech stocks climbed Thursday, extending a recent rebound from a sharp sell-off last month.

Beijing's latest regulatory intervention has helped support the market, said WisdomTree's Liqian Ren. But she said Chinese tech stock performance is ultimately tied more to global investor sentiment on AI.

"There's a lot of uncertainty on how much AI can deliver for U.S., or for China," she said.

—CNBC's Justin Papp and Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.
