#2831. Fantasy football Start ‘Em, Sit ‘Em: 30 players to start or sit in Week 9

Week 9 of the 2026 NFL season has already brought with it plenty of chaos, as several teams are making notable moves at the quarterback position.

A microcosm of the league's greater movement can be seen with the Baltimore Ravens. The Ravens lost Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley to long-term injuries and will start undrafted rookie Joe Fagnano on "Thursday Night Football." However, they are also planning to trade for Arizona Cardinals quarterback Jacoby Brissett over the weekend, so their quarterback room is in flux.

The same is true with the Seattle Seahawks, where Drew Lock will replace an injured Sam Darnold, and with the Cleveland Browns, where Shedeur Sanders may start with Deshaun Watson in concussion protocol. And that's not to mention the Philadelphia Eagles' decision to bench Tanner McKee to see what they have in the potential-packed Joe Milton.

That swath of quarterback changes will leave many fantasy managers with difficult start 'em, sit 'em decisions. Those quandaries will also exist at other positions like running back, where fantasy footballers will have to decide whether to trust Ashton Jeanty or Tyler Allgeier, among others.

Who can you start and sit in fantasy football for Week 9 of the 2026 NFL season? USA TODAY Sports outlines 30 players to start or sit.

Malik Willis, Miami Dolphins (vs. Detroit Lions)

Willis is starting to round into form for the Dolphins and has scored multiple touchdowns in three consecutive games. During that span, he has averaged 37.7 rushing yards per game, which will raise his floor in most matchups. The Lions have allowed the most fantasy points per game (FPPG) to quarterbacks this season, so Willis should be positioned to have a strong showing in what figures to be a favorable, high-scoring matchup.

Drew Lock, Seattle Seahawks (vs. Arizona Cardinals)

The Cardinals are one of just three NFL teams to allow 18 passing touchdowns to quarterbacks this season, along with the Lions and the Las Vegas Raiders. Unsurprisingly, Arizona has allowed a league-high 29.8 fantasy points per game to the position because of that, so Lock should be positioned for a nice outing in which he could toss multiple touchdowns.

Joe Milton, Philadelphia Eagles (vs. New York Giants)

If you're looking for a fun dart throw, Milton could be worth considering. He's set to start in place of Tanner McKee and has the cannon arm and elite athleticism needed to blossom into a fantasy asset. The Giants have allowed the seventh-most FPPG to quarterbacks, so Milton has a chance to emerge as a decent streamer.

Quinshon Judkins, Cleveland Browns (at New Orleans Saints)

Judkins has been boom-or-bust as a touchdown-dependent fantasy option, but he should be poised for a good outing against the Saints. New Orleans has allowed the most FPPG to running backs this season, including a league-high 11 rushing touchdowns to the position. With that in mind, Judkins should be able to find his way into the end zone.

Jonah Coleman, Denver Broncos (at Carolina Panthers)

Coleman took on a lion's share of Denver's backfield touches in Week 9, posting 104 total yards and a touchdown on 15 touches (11 carries, four receptions). The rookie should continue in that role and has a great matchup against a Panthers defense that has allowed the second-most FPPG to running backs this season.

Tyler Allgeier, Arizona Cardinals (at Seattle Seahawks)

Seattle is a tough matchup for any running back, but Allgeier figures to get great volume with Jeremiyah Love (ankle) on IR and Bam Knight dealing with a concussion. That could be enough to make him a touchdown-dependent flex play after he popped off for 191 total yards and two touchdowns against the Cowboys.

Jadarian Price, Seattle Seahawks (vs. Arizona Cardinals)

Price and Zach Charbonnet split carries for the Seahawks against the Bears. Neither was particularly effective but each gets a much easier matchup in Week 9 against a Cardinals defense that has been middle-of-the-pack against running backs this season. Price has some explosiveness, so don't be surprised if this is his breakout game.

Caleb Douglas, Miami Dolphins (vs. Detroit Lions)

As mentioned, the Lions have been weak against the pass this year. If we're recommending to start Willis, then Douglas should get a nod too, as the rookie has notched six receptions for 113 yards and two touchdowns in two games since returning from an early-season ankle injury.

Ladd McConkey, Los Angeles Chargers (vs. Houston Texans)

Trusting McConkey may seem risky, given that Justin Herbert has struggled under pressure this season. That said, the Texans are allowing the second-most FPPG to receivers this season and McConkey is fresh off a four-catch, 86-yard outing that was one of his best of the season. He should be in line for a solid showing against the Texans.

Josh Downs, Indianapolis Colts (vs. Dallas Cowboys)

Could this be a showcase game for Downs ahead of the 2026 NFL trade deadline? It sure feels like it. Dallas has allowed the fifth-most FPPG to receivers this season, and the Colts may be eager to boost Downs' trade value by targeting him early and often in this spot.

Brian Thomas Jr., Jacksonville Jaguars (at Baltimore Ravens)

The same is true of Thomas, who could be traded as he sees his role shrink in Jacksonville. He caught a touchdown last week and could be in line for ample playing time if the Jaguars can build a big lead against a Joe Fagnano-led Ravens team on "Thursday Night Football."

Mike Gesicki, Cincinnati Bengals (at Atlanta Falcons)

Gesicki has scored in five consecutive games and six of the seven he has played this season. Joe Burrow has thrown 13 touchdown passes over his last three games, so Gesicki should continue to have high touchdown upside regardless of his opponent.

Greg Dulcich, Miami Dolphins (vs. Detroit Lions)

Dulcich has posted at least four catches and 37 yards in four of his last five games. Now, he gets to face a Lions defense that is allowing the most receptions (61), receiving yards (684) and receiving touchdowns (11) to tight ends this season. He could emerge as a surprise top-10 tight end this week.

Jacksonville Jaguars (at Baltimore Ravens)

The Jaguars are playing the Ravens on a short week. Baltimore is expected to start third-string quarterback Joe Fagnano, an undrafted rookie, after injuries to Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley. Fagnano completed 10 of 21 passes for 91 yards and an interception while being sacked three times against the Bills. The Jaguars should pressure him into numerous mistakes while their top-ranked run defense should be able to bottle up Derrick Henry.

The Lions will likely give up some yardage and points to the Dolphins, but they should also generate plenty of sacks. Detroit has 24 sacks this season, third-most in the NFL, while Malik Willis has been sacked 21 times through seven starts.

Bryce Young, Carolina Panthers (vs. Denver Broncos)

Young has regressed over the last two weeks after a torrid start to the season, completing just 54.4% of his passes for 372 yards, two touchdowns and three interceptions during Carolina's two-game losing skid. The main issue? Young has been pressured a lot in those contests, and he has just a 64 passer rating when pressured compared to 106.3 when kept clean, per Pro Football Focus. The Broncos rank fourth in pressure rate league-wide, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats, so they should be able to limit Young in this favorable spot.

Bo Nix, Denver Broncos (at Carolina Panthers)

The Panthers have a banged-up secondary, but it still won't be easy to justify starting Nix. The Broncos quarterback has looked better recently, throwing for exactly 222 yards in back-to-back games and scoring twice in each. Still, the Panthers are typically more susceptible against the run, so Nix has a lower ceiling than most streaming options this week.

Daniel Jones, Indianapolis Colts (vs. Dallas Cowboys)

Normally, the quarterback playing the Cowboys is a solid streaming option. However, with Jones facing down the potential for a benching and having failed to throw multiple touchdown passes in a game this season, it's best to steer clear of him.

Ashton Jeanty, Las Vegas Raiders (at San Francisco 49ers)

Can you really trust Jeanty after the Raiders gave him just two carries against the Jets while giving Mike Washington Jr. a whopping 25? Jeanty will remain productive as a receiver, but his uncertain between-the-tackles workload makes it hard to trust the former first-round pick, especially against a 49ers defense allowing just 3.6 yards per carry to running backs this season. It's better to wait and see whether Jeanty's decrease in carries was punishment for trash-talking the Jets before Week 8 or a sign of what's to come in Klint Kubiak's running back rotation.

Breece Hall, New York Jets (at Kansas City Chiefs)

Hall seems likely to return from a nagging quad injury this week, but he gets a tough matchup against a Chiefs defense that has largely bottled up opposing running backs this season. Hall may also be splitting carries with Braelon Allen, who has thrived in his stead, so this doesn't seem like an ideal spot to trust him.

Aaron Jones and Jordan Mason, Minnesota Vikings (at Buffalo Bills)

We'll do two for the price of one here. The Vikings have a bottom-six rushing offense this season in terms of EPA per play, per the NFL's Next Gen Stats. Jones and Mason are also stuck in a frustrating time share, so neither seems like a particularly viable play against a Bills defense that has allowed just 97.4 rushing yards per game this season.

Michael Wilson, Arizona Cardinals (at Seattle Seahawks)

Wilson finally had a breakout game for the first time since Marvin Harrison Jr. was traded, generating eight catches for 111 yards and a touchdown against the Cowboys. He'll have a much tougher matchup against a Seahawks team that has been excellent against receivers this year, especially after Wilson suffered a couple of broken ribs on his touchdown catch.

Adonai Mitchell, New York Jets (at Kansas City Chiefs)

Mitchell just logged seven catches for 142 yards and a touchdown against the Raiders in Week 8. He gets a much tougher matchup in Week 9 against a Chiefs defense that has allowed the second-fewest FPPG to receivers this season. Add in that Omar Cooper Jr. could also siphon targets from him and Mitchell may be a hit-or-miss play each week even if Garrett Wilson (concussion) is unable to play.

Jalen Coker, Carolina Panthers (vs. Denver Broncos)

The Broncos get a lot of pressure, so Young won't have much time to throw in that matchup. That could limit Coker's deep-ball upside, and while he could rack up yards after the catch, it's hard to trust him to do so against a quality defense.

Khalil Shakir, Buffalo Bills (at Minnesota Vikings)

Shakir started well in the Bills' win over the Ravens but did little outside the first drive. DJ Moore seems likely to continue getting most of the attention from Josh Allen, so don't be surprised if Shakir struggles against a Vikings defense that has allowed the third-fewest FPPG to receivers this season.

Dalton Kincaid, Buffalo Bills (at Minnesota Vikings)

Kincaid scored a touchdown last week for the first time since Week 2, but he will be hard-pressed to repeat that feat against the Vikings. Minnesota has yet to allow a receiving touchdown to tight ends this season, so Kincaid figures to be limited.

T.J. Hockenson, Minnesota Vikings (vs. Buffalo Bills)

The Bills have only allowed one touchdown to tight ends this season. It also isn't clear whether Kyler Murray (hamstring) will play in this contest, so Hockenson might have a lower ceiling in this matchup than most.

No NFL team has allowed fewer points per game than the Vikings (13.3). No NFL team has scored more points per game than the Bills (33.3). Something will give in this matchup, but if there's one quarterback that's built to beat Brian Flores' pressure-happy scheme, it's Josh Allen.

San Francisco 49ers (vs. Las Vegas Raiders)

The 49ers are favored to beat the Raiders, but this could end up being a shootout given how Kirk Cousins and Co. have been playing. Las Vegas is averaging 27.6 points per game, so even if Nick Bosa returns, this is still a low-floor spot for San Francisco.

#2829. LIV Golf faces liquidation after BC Partners scraps rescue plan

LIV Golf could be headed for liquidation after BC Partners Credit reportedly dropped plans to finance a relaunch of the breakaway league.

The investment firm had pledged up to $300 million for a revamped version of LIV, known as LIV 2.0, but withdrew the plan after LIV Golf failed to secure enough player commitments, according to the Associated Press.

LIV is scheduled to appear in U.S. bankruptcy court Thursday, with the hearing now expected to focus on liquidation of its assets. The league's ultimate fate remains unclear.

LIV Golf and BC Partners did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

LIV filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in September after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund said it would stop funding the league after the 2026 season.

PIF had invested more than $5 billion since LIV launched in 2022, according to its Chapter 11 filing.

BC Partners' proposed relaunch would have made players majority owners of the reorganized league and featured a scaled-back schedule.

But LIV has lost several prominent players, including Jon Rahm and Sergio Garcia, as it sought commitments for the new venture.

Rahm said Tuesday that uncertainty over the league's future and proposed changes to its format influenced his decision to leave.

"There were too many unknowns at the time, and there's still quite a few," he told reporters ahead of the Abu Dhabi Championship. Rahm added that the revamped competition appeared to be moving away from the team format that initially attracted him.

Garcia, who secured a release from his LIV contract in October, has outlined plans to return to the DP World Tour and explore a possible return to the PGA Tour.

Only four players had publicly committed to LIV 2.0: Richard Bland, Cameron Tringale, Brendan Steele and Ben Campbell, according to Golf Digest.

LIV launched in 2022 as a challenger to the PGA Tour, recruiting prominent players including Phil Mickelson, Dustin Johnson and Bryson DeChambeau with lucrative contracts.

Its arrival split men's professional golf, with the PGA Tour suspending members who competed in LIV events. The disputes later spilled into the courts, with players challenging suspensions and the rival organizations pursuing competing legal claims.

The PGA Tour denied the allegations, while LIV dismissed the counterclaims.

A June 2023 framework agreement between the PGA Tour, DP World Tour and PIF ended their litigation and proposed combining their commercial businesses, although the deal was never completed.

#2828. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2827. Knicks' Jalen Brunson finds his groove in win over Bulls

So far this season, Knicks star point guard Jalen Brunson hasn't looked like the MVP-caliber player fans saw in the NBA Finals this past summer.

But one week after helping New York erase a 19-point deficit in Chicago, Brunson seemingly officially found his groove Wednesday night against the Bulls. However, instead of showing up as basketball's best closer as he so often does, he hit the ground running early, racking up 22 points in 12-straight first-frame minutes and outscoring the entire Bulls roster by one.

He played just 17 more minutes across the remaining three quarters of the game but continued to find his way to the basket, increasing his total to a season-best 33 points. No other player tallied more than 18 (Karl-Anthony Towns). He hit five of his 10 three-point attempts, eight of his nine foul shots, and 10 of his 17 from the field.

"It's part of the game," he said of his effort. "Obviously, when you have it going, it feels good. Just trying to play off that and use your gravity to make plays."

Odds are, the reigning NBA Finals MVP would have kept scoring had he been given more time on the floor. At the rate he was going, he may have hit 80 points.

"Our analytics guys [chart] how guys play in two-minute increments," head coach Mike Brown explained after the game. "Some guys, after six minutes, their three-point field-goal percentage drops. They also can track their movement — how quickly they're still moving out on the floor, if that slows down or not. Jalen's just seems to go up every two minutes."

But Brown said his desire to protect Brunson's health significantly outweighs his curiosity regarding just how much Brunson could produce if he were left unrestrained. In fact, he said, keeping Brunson in the game just for the sake of it never even crossed his mind.

"He's about as durable as anybody I've been around. He's mentally tough, physically tough, and he can push himself to go," Brown said. "... But at the end of the day, it's going to impact him, in my opinion, come playoff time, so I have to be conscious of not going to the well too much, especially at this point in the season."

When asked if he would ever request to keep playing in a game like tonight, Brunson was hesitant.

"Possibly, but it has to be the right moment, right time," he said. "I don't want to say no because you never know when you're in this situation, but I just want to respect the game as much as possible."

#2826. J.J. Watt, Cris Collinsworth want Bengals to trade for Jonathan Taylor

The Cincinnati Bengals have been floated in a couple of trade rumors ahead of November 10th's trade deadline. A team that is 5-2 with Super Bowl aspirations could be aggressive ahead of the deadline if they think there is a player or players who could put them over the top.

Per NFL insider Jordan Schultz, the Bengals are one of a few teams "aggressively pursuing" Indianapolis Colts running back Jonathan Taylor, although no deal to any team seems imminent. The Cincinnati offense has been lighting it up in the passing game, but the running game has left much to be desired so far in 2026.

With those rumors out there, former NFL great and current CBS broadcaster J.J. Watt is urging the Bengals to make the move.

"I really do believe the Cincinnati Bengals make sense for a guy like him because of what their offense has in terms of the passing game and they just haven't been able to get going on the ground," Watt said during an appearance on CBS' "Set Hut" show.

As the Colts have started a fire sale following the firing of General Manager Chris Ballard, Taylor is widely expected to be moved before the deadline, but the Colts appear firm on getting top-tier value for him. Aside from the Dexter Lawrence II trade this offseason, the Bengals aren't known for trading away draft capital. With the clear urgency in the locker room, though, the Bengals could surprise us once again.

Former Bengal Cris Collinsworth also believes the Bengals should make the aggressive move.

"Now with the defense and Dexter Lawrence comes in and Boye Mafe comes in, and the Bengals make moves that they've just never made, it's right now, it's this year, so go do it," Collinsworth said on the PFF NFL Show.

#2825. What Wall Street billionaires are saying about Democrats' midterm sweep

America's billionaires woke up Wednesday to a political reckoning — and a Wall Street rally.

As Democrats celebrated a sweeping midterm victory, some of the country's wealthiest donors traded barbs over record election spending. Others warned the results could threaten the artificial intelligence boom.

Wall Street, however, cheered the outcome. The Dow Jones Industrial Average soared 1,569 points, or 3%, while the S&P 500 gained 1.6% and the Nasdaq Composite rose 1.4%, as investors welcomed political gridlock, reduced uncertainty and President Donald Trump's willingness to accept the results.

Virgin Group founder Richard Branson took aim at the billions spent influencing the election.

"I have felt embarrassed by the billions that tech entrepreneurs and hedge fund managers have spent," Branson wrote Wednesday on LinkedIn, calling for campaign finance reform. Branson did not give donations that met the reporting threshold in the 2026 election cycle.

His comments came as Elon Musk, the election's largest individual donor, watched his preferred Republicans lose the Texas Senate and governor races despite pouring $224 million into the state — nearly half his record $474 million in political giving nationwide, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records.

Early Wednesday, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO suggested on X that data centers in space were looking increasingly appealing before joking that his "election-interfering space lasers" had failed.

LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, another major political donor, took a sharper tone.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote late Tuesday on X, accusing Musk of spending "outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power" while defending his own contributions to Democrats.

Other Democratic megadonors celebrated. "The world changed yesterday," Alex Soros wrote Wednesday on X, hailing the results as a generational victory after his family contributed roughly $103 million to Democratic political efforts.

But beyond the political sparring, some of the country's richest investors were focused on a potentially bigger consequence of the Democratic sweep: the future of the AI boom.

Even before polls closed Tuesday, Pershing Square founder Bill Ackman warned on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that slowing AI development could jeopardize America's technological edge. "Practically speaking, we can't slow down," he said.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, billionaire investor Chamath Palihapitiya sounded an even starker warning.

Palihapitiya initially questioned whether the early results would hold, but warned that if they did, the implications for the AI trade could be severe.

"And the only way to sink the AI trade is to sink the data centers," Palihapitiya said during an election night livestream of the "All-In" podcast.

Texas offered an early test of those fears. Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, who has proposed a moratorium on new data center construction until stronger safeguards are established, defeated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

During the same livestream, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner warned the Democratic wave could mean at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of expected AI data center capacity would not come online in 2027. Wall Street analyst Dan Ives estimated Wednesday that 10% of planned U.S. data centers would no longer go ahead.

Advanced Micro Devices CEO Lisa Su acknowledged the growing resistance to AI's expansion, urging technology companies to demonstrate the benefits of data centers to local communities.

"At the end of the day, what we need to do is ensure that everybody wins from AI," Su told CNBC on Wednesday.

Yet Wall Street may have found opportunity in the same slowdown that worried AI's biggest backers.

Fewer new data centers could increase the value of existing facilities while easing the enormous spending burden on technology giants. Big Tech stocks surged Wednesday, adding more than $750 billion in market value at one point, even as smaller, debt-dependent data center operators tumbled.

#2824. The WNBA spent 30 years just trying to prove it belonged. Now it’s time to build daringly

The wildest of the WNBA's 30 seasons ended in defiant triumph. It ended with evidence that the league is greater than the labor battles, culture wars and institutional missteps. It ended with a gnarly defensive finals lasting seven games and holding interest all the way to a record-setting television audience for Game 7.

It ended with the Golden State Valkyries, a newbie full of substance, capturing the title in their second year. And it ended with coach Natalie Nakase, a 5-foot-2 former UCLA walk-on, standing atop women's basketball and delivering a message about her team that resonated with the entire sport.

"This is for all the people that have been underrated, misunderstood," she said. "This is for all y'all, because this is proof: If you put your heart and all your soul, you can (expletive) win championships!"

After a taxing season, there couldn't have been a more perfect final scene: confetti and F-bombs bursting in air.

Two nights before that cinematic ending, the Valkyries-Atlanta Dream series played Game 6 at 12:30 p.m. on a Thursday because of drama and suspect planning over arena availability. Nobody wanted a lunch-hour conclusion in Atlanta, and fortunately, it wasn't the final impression this season. Despite the confusion and criticism, that penultimate moment still managed to be compelling.

Now, let's put both images side by side: Game 7 averaged 3.3 million viewers, the most-watched WNBA playoff game, and Game 6 averaged 1.5 million, a robust number even though office workers needed to sneak glances on their phones. That exemplified the WNBA in 2026. Two extremes stirred endless debate. Yet the sport won anyway.

That shouldn't be viewed as a crisis. It's a growth spurt. The league is wearing clothes that no longer fit. Now that another consequential offseason has arrived, with a vacant commissioner job and a handful of leadership jobs open at high-profile franchises, there's time to revamp the wardrobe.

For the past few years, as leaguewide popularity has risen, critics have pointed to various controversies as proof of an unserious league: the ugly moments of a labor fight that dragged into March before resulting in a landmark new collective bargaining agreement; the players' denunciation of the leadership style of retiring commissioner Cathy Engelbert; the ongoing perception of jealousy over Caitlin Clark's popularity; the feud between newcomers who worship Clark and longtime loyalists with a broader view of the league; a manufactured culture war over transgender players (even though the league has none) that turned courtside seats into stages for provocateurs.

Those critics have some evidence, for sure, but their conclusion is off the mark. Unserious leagues don't matter this much to people. Much of the drama wasn't a sign of a clown show. It was an indicator of growth the league wanted but never fully prepared to handle.

For a long time, the league survived by doubting itself, by hedging its bets. From now on, it will thrive by believing.

Not surface-level confidence. Complete self-assurance. Ambition to maximize progress.

Look at what's no longer in question. The salary cap went from $1.5 million to $7 million. Ratings and attendance numbers are surging. The Golden State franchise is worth $1 billion two seasons into its launch. Clark hasn't arrived as a savior. She has been an amplifier. The survival question that has hung over the WNBA since 1997 doesn't need to be asked anymore.

The greatest fix the WNBA can make isn't really a fix. It's a shift in mentality. The league doesn't need repair anymore. Maybe it never did.

For three decades, the product has been better than its reputation. The basketball was good when crowds were sparse and TV execs were offering scraps. Skeptics, many of whom never watched and lazily repeated the assumption that women's sports could only survive as charity, mistook neglect for a judgment. The WNBA absorbed that doubt and built a safe space for protection. It had to be careful, cost-conscious and grateful for the tiniest bit of acknowledgment. That approach kept the league alive. Now, those remnants hold it back.

It's noticeable in the hedging. The WNBA took too many trepidatious stances on major issues, whether it was trans eligibility, policing nasty fan behavior or ensuring finals-worthy buildings were available. When the WNBA had less to lose, it took bolder positions, especially during the pandemic-era days of heavy collaboration with the players. Lately, it has been afraid to establish a clear identity, mostly because a diverse audience is contributing to this boom. But every time the league tiptoed, someone else filled the gap and co-opted the story. And those people never have the sport's best interest at heart.

Golden State represents the sleekest new model of what it means to meet this moment. The Valkyries haven't hedged. They went big from the start: turning the Chase Center into Ballhalla, sellouts and season ticket holders galore, a culture deliberately built around a coach and a roster that plays like a team rather than a collection of parts around a marquee superstar. They functioned like a major-league franchise before anyone permitted them. Now, they're champions. Already, they're champions. But that trophy didn't make them successful. It was the culmination of their breakthrough. They're the trendy avatar for what belief looks like.

The Valkyries aren't alone. Franchises such as the Las Vegas Aces, New York Liberty and Seattle Storm have spent years outpacing the league in ambition, investing in facilities, staff and continuity and becoming model franchises. Under new ownership, the Phoenix Mercury are getting to that level, too. No operation is more stable than the Minnesota Lynx. The Indiana Fever, who endured a long stretch as one of the league's worst-run organizations, are finally rising structurally to Clark's level of transcendence. With a team president and general manager to hire, they could complete their transformation. The Dream, a franchise once known more for turmoil than basketball, ended this season with the best kind of problem: too much demand for its building to hold. The Dallas Wings look like they could be next. Or maybe the Washington Mystics, another good organization that won the title in 2019 and is currently stocked with enviable talent, are a budding dynasty.

A couple of franchises are struggling, but the general message throughout the league appears to be the same. Ambition isn't as risky as it used to be. The teams that bet big on the WNBA are winning the bet. The league office should go all in, too.

That's the task for Engelbert's successor. The league doesn't need a caretaker or a crisis manager. It doesn't need a CEO above all other skills. It needs someone who believes fully and builds daringly. No need to be reckless, but this is now a maximizer's job. Assume sellouts rather than hope for them. Draft and articulate a league-wide code of conduct so that the protection of players and fans isn't dependent on which arena hosts the game. Box out the provocateurs by taking proactive measures to eliminate blind spots.

And this one is for Adam Silver and the NBA owners he serves: Empower a commissioner with greater autonomy to make decisions in the WNBA's interests, even when they don't align perfectly with the NBA's.

None of this is radical. It just requires the league to stop acting as if one wrong investment will make it disappear.

Believing at this level carries risk. But the WNBA has spent 30 seasons managing risk. The bigger danger now is not being ambitious enough.

Engelbert deserves credit for navigating all of this and putting the league in a strong position, including an aggressive plan to expand to 18 teams by 2030 that has yielded incredible early returns. Her successor must be asked to do more than maintain. The opportunity exists to lift the WNBA out of the niche tier permanently.

The WNBA has spent three decades proving it was underrated. That part is over.

It's time to shop for a new look, one fitting a league that knew it was always destined to be fashionable.

#2823. Nintendo Switch 2 sales plunge 41% as price hikes and memory crunch bite

Sales of Nintendo's flagship Switch 2 plunged in its fiscal second quarter after the Japanese gaming giant rolled out price hikes amid soaring memory costs.

Nintendo said it sold 2.67 million units of the Switch 2 in the three months ended Sept. 30, down 41% from the same period last year. That was also lower than the 3.82 million units sold in the prior quarter.

During the quarter, Nintendo rolled out price increases for the Switch 2 in the U.S., Canada and Japan after hiking the cost of the device in Japan earlier in the year.

Memory, a core component of Nintendo's consoles, continues to face a supply shortage and huge demand, which is driving an unprecedented price surge for the chips.

Still, the Japanese gaming giant stuck to its previous Switch 2 sales forecast of 16.5 million units in the current fiscal year, which ends in March 2027.

Here's how Nintendo did in its fiscal second quarter, compared with LSEG average estimates:

While the Kyoto-headquartered firm has had some strong games this year, including "Tomodachi Life: Living the Dream" and "Pokémon Pokopia", which became a surprise viral hit, there are no major marquee titles.

Investors hope that "The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time," a remake of one of Nintendo's most iconic games, could boost Switch 2 sales in the coming weeks. The game was released on Thursday.

"Nintendo's only real blockbuster this fiscal year is the Ocarina Of Time remake. It's not enough to make investors happy, especially after the device is now as expensive as it is," Serkan Toto, CEO of Kantan Games, told CNBC.

"It's getting harder and harder to sell video game hardware these days, and only hits help. Nintendo must step on the gas and deliver more big games next fiscal year to make sure the Switch 2 momentum continues."

Nintendo also has new Pokémon games slated for release in 2027.

#2822. Victor Wembanyama stats tonight must be seen to be believed: Spurs star's incredible box score

Victor Wembanyama continues to do things never seen on a basketball court.

The Spurs phenom absolutely torched San Antonio's in-state rivals, the Dallas Mavericks, Wednesday, Nov. 4 in a 112-111 victory, setting a new career high in points and rebounds and nearly recording a triple-double with blocks.

Wembanyama's historic night started in the first half. Overall, the Spurs dropped 68 points, but Wembanyama recorded 42.6% of those all by himself. He was also dominant on the glass; while the Mavericks scooped up 31 boards in the first half, Wembanyama collected nearly half of that.

"Dominant," Spurs guard Dylan Harper said of Wembanyama. "When he plays like that, you see the result."

Wembanyama was an absolute force down low. All seven of his converted field goals in the first half came within the restricted area. He also made all 15 of his free throw attempts in the half.

How dominant was he? Wembanyama became the first player since 1997-98 to reach at least 25 points, 10 rebounds and 5 blocks in a half.

The Spurs took a 68-51 lead into intermission.

His stellar play continued, especially as the Mavericks closed their deficit. Wembanyama did struggle from deep and only hit one of his 3-point tries, but he was pivotal down the stretch, getting to the paint and drawing contact.

By the end of the night, Wembanyama became only the second player since blocks began to be tracked in 1974 to record at least 50 points, 20 rebounds and 5 blocks.

"I can do more," Wembanyama told reporters after the game. "I can do more if my shots were falling. This was the worst 3-point shooting of my career."

#2821. World's largest olive oil company plummets 20% after rivals agree to takeover deal

Shares of Deoleo, the world's largest olive oil company, plummeted Thursday after two rivals announced plans to acquire the company, a deal that would further consolidate Spain's dominance in the sector.

Spanish olive oil firms Dcoop and Acesur will each hold a 50% stake in the company, resulting in a new olive oil giant in the region.

The deal is intended to boost olive oil consumption worldwide and support farmers in creating value through the supply chain, Dcoop said in an announcement.

Deoleo was last trading down 19.9%. However the stock is up around 55% since the beginning of the year as it's benefited from a moderation in olive oil prices that has helped lift U.S. sales.

CNBC reached out to Deoleo for comment and is awaiting a response.

The news comes after an intensifying takeover battle between Dcoop and multinational firms including those from Italy, France, and Australia. The stock gained over 20% on Aug. 19 when news of the takeover battle emerged. Dcoop reportedly previously offered 470 million euros ($545 million) for Deoleo.

Spain is one of the world's leading olive oil producers and is often used as a global reference for prices.

Prices for the commodity have been volatile over the past few years as supplies swung dramatically one season to the next, with climate change, water scarcity, and pest and disease playing a role.

Deoleo CEO Cristóbal Valdés told CNBC in July that the period between 2022 and 2024 was one of the most challenging periods for the sector, but that prices are now stabilizing due to favourable rain trends.

"The highly complex market cycle experienced between 2022 and 2024, which had a severe yet temporary impact on the industry, is now definitively behind us," Valdés said at the time.

#2820. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2819. Why isn't Lamar Jackson playing today? Injury update, return timeline for Ravens QB

The Baltimore Ravens are taking on the Jacksonville Jaguars in a "Thursday Night Football" game that could prove crucial for their chances of staying in the AFC North race.

The Ravens will have to navigate that game, and the foreseeable future, without the services of their starting quarterback, Lamar Jackson.

Jackson suffered a thumb injury that is expected to sideline him long-term. This marks the second time this season the veteran quarterback is facing a multi-game absence, after missing three games leading up to Baltimore's Week 8 game against the Buffalo Bills with an ankle injury.

Here's everything you need to know about Jackson's thumb injury and when he might return to the field.

Jackson suffered a thumb injury in the Ravens' Week 8 loss to the Buffalo Bills.

The injury occurred on Jackson's first passing attempt of the game, when he hit his throwing hand on the arm of Bills pass rusher Gregory Rousseau. Jackson immediately reached for his hand in pain and was seen clutching it while running toward the sideline.

Jackson was quickly deemed out for the remainder of the game. He was later spotted wearing a cast on his injured hand after he returned to the field clad in street clothes during the second half of the contest.

Before the game against Buffalo, Jackson had missed the previous three games because of an ankle injury. He has appeared in five of the Ravens' eight games this season but has only finished three.

The Ravens have not provided a definitive timeline for Jackson's return, but coach Jesse Minter confirmed the star quarterback will be placed on IR because of his thumb injury.

"That'll be a surgery deal," Minter said when discussing Jackson's injury during a postgame news conference on Sunday, Nov. 1. "He'll go to IR. That's a tough deal."

Players placed on IR must miss a minimum of four games, per NFL rules. That would guarantee Jackson misses the Ravens' Weeks 9-12 games before becoming eligible to return for their Week 14 contest against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers, which comes after their Week 13 bye.

But Minter did acknowledge that Jackson's injury could "potentially" be season-ending. He also declined to provide updates about the quarterback's thumb when asked during a Monday, Nov. 2 news conference.

"I'm not going to get into the specifics of the injury," Minter told reporters.

Minter did reveal Jackson had not yet had surgery to repair his thumb malady and that the Ravens would likely have a more concrete update about his status "maybe sometime this week."

The Ravens have not yet clarified whether Jackson will be able to return during the 2026 NFL season. Minter simply said the injury was "potentially" season-ending but did not offer a concrete timeline for the veteran quarterback's return.

Normally, veteran Tyler Huntley would take on that mantle in Jackson's absence. However, he is dealing with a turf toe injury and is expected to miss the rest of the season because of it.

That has left third-string quarterback Joe Fagnano to serve as the starter for "Thursday Night Football" in Week 9. The undrafted rookie spent the final three seasons of his college career at UConn and completed 68% of his passes for 3,448 yards, 28 touchdowns and one interception during his sixth and final college season.

Fagnano (6-3, 230 pounds) completed 10 of 21 passes for 91 yards and an interception in relief of Jackson and Huntley against the Bills.

That said, Fagnano isn't expected to be the Ravens' long-term starter. The team has agreed to acquire veteran quarterback Jacoby Brissett in a trade with the Arizona Cardinals, and Brissett will assume the starting mantle beginning in Week 10, if all goes well.

Brissett has previous experience working with Ravens quarterback coach Israel Woolfork during their time together with the Arizona Cardinals.

Baltimore's quarterback depth chart remains in flux with Jackson and Huntley out and Brissett not yet officially acquired.

Brissett is expected to top the Ravens' quarterback depth chart once he officially joins the team, but below is a look at the pecking order within the group for Thursday's game.

Reed signed to the Ravens' practice squad on Monday, Nov. 2, just after Jackson and Huntley's injury. The 2024 undrafted free agent previously played for the Chicago Bears but has never appeared in NFL regular-season game. The Western Kentucky product thrived for the Dallas Renegades during the 2026 UFL season, completing 56.3% of his passes for 1,923 yards, a league-high 21 touchdowns and nine interceptions.

Slovis is another player who went undrafted in 2024. The USC, Pitt and BYU product spent time with four NFL teams before joining the Ravens from the Packers' practice squad. He dressed for two games with the Arizona Cardinals last season and completed 1 of 2 passes for negative-2 yards.

Because Slovis was on the Packers' practice squad, the Ravens needed to sign him to their 53-man roster to obtain him ahead of Thursday's game. That said, the 25-year-old will likely remain behind Reed given that the latter spent time with Baltimore during the preseason – though Slovis, like Brissett, previously played for Woolfork in Arizona.

#2818. Banks are turning cautious on lending, according to the Fed. What it means for the economy

U.S. banks are starting to tighten their lending standards amid growing concern over the underlying health of the economy, according to a new Federal Reserve survey.

The Fed's latest Senior Loan Officer Opinion Survey on Bank Lending Practices for October, published Nov. 2, showed an uneven credit backdrop in the third quarter, with smaller businesses, riskier households and commercial property in particular facing a lending squeeze.

The latest quarterly SLOOS study for October — which pulled together responses from 61 domestic banks and 18 U.S. branches of foreign lenders — covered changes in lending standards, terms and demand during the third quarter.

The survey tracks banks' reported lending standards, terms and demand, not actual loan volumes, offering a snapshot of evolving credit conditions and sentiment.

Commercial and industrial lending to large and middle-market firms was broadly unchanged from the previous survey — but banks tightened lending to smaller businesses.

"Banks reported tightening standards for premiums charged on riskier loans for firms of all sizes," the report noted. Lenders pointed to a less favorable or more uncertain economic outlook, and a worsening of industry-specific problems as the key reason for the shift.

Commercial real estate is also emerging as a pressure point, with banks reporting tightened standards across the board on construction and land development, nonfarm nonresidential properties, and multifamily properties, and weakening demand for loans in all CRE categories.

U.S. banks are also tightening credit standards for nonbank financial firms such as private equity funds and business and mortgage credit intermediaries. The pullback comes amid growing demand for liquidity from private equity funds and business credit intermediaries, with lenders again highlighting prevailing economic uncertainty and concerns over the deteriorating financial strength among NDFI borrowers.

Meanwhile, household credit sentiment appears mixed.

While banks have left mortgage standards broadly unchanged, there has been some easing for jumbo mortgages — but tighter rules for subprime loans — as banks reported weaker demand, on balance, across all residential real estate loan categories.

"Banks reported being more likely to approve credit card applications to super-prime and prime borrowers and less likely to approve applications to subprime borrowers, with small banks driving the trend for subprime borrowers," the report added.

The squeeze comes as borrowing costs moved sharply higher during the third quarter. The 10-year Treasury note yield — the main benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — hit an intraday high of 5.349% on Oct. 5, its highest level since April 2002, amid concerns over rising U.S. debt.

Tiffany Wilding, managing director and economist at PIMCO, said small businesses, housing and debt-laden firms face tighter credit and higher borrowing costs as the AI investment boom squeezes other parts of the U.S. economy.

"Higher government bond yields have catalyzed a broader repricing of credit risk, especially for lower-quality borrowers," Wilding said in a note Oct. 28.

Signals of credit stress among riskier households chime with a recent CNBC and SurveyMonkey poll, which found that one in five Americans who rent or have a mortgage spend at least half of their pretax income on housing, while 49% spend between 25% and 49%. Lender guidance recommends consumers spend 28% to 30% of their income on housing.

A separate Federal Reserve report indicated that U.S. families' ability to address their debts has worsened over the past three years. The Fed's latest Survey of Consumer Finances, released Oct. 9, found that the percentage of families behind on loan payments at the end of 2025 reached almost 20%, up from about 12% in the previous survey in 2022.

#2817. Voters rejected AI data centers over resource concerns. Here's why measuring water usage is hard

Voters made it clear this week that they want to see limits put on data centers for artificial intelligence, passing 40 of 41 proposed ballot measures that would place restrictions on some of the more than 2,000 new facilities planned for communities around the nation.

Water and power top the list of ratepayers' concerns. But calculating how much each data center draws from local resources is an elusive task, with operators typically reporting usage figures companywide instead of site by site.

Giant 1-gigawatt AI data centers from the likes of Meta, Google, Amazon and SpaceX can consume up to 10 million gallons of water per day, according to Shaolei Ren, a data center researcher and professor at University of California, Riverside.

The exact number varies greatly depending on what type of hardware is inside a data center, where it's built, and what method is used to cool the servers and the building as a whole.

The complex ways data centers use water have left room for misinformation from both the builders and those fighting against them. CNBC talked to experts to get a clearer picture.

Data centers need serious internal cooling methods to keep chips and servers from overheating.

Legacy data centers, filled with lower-power servers used for things like networking or storage, are often air-cooled. Large fans move hot air away from the servers and outside the building.

But modern AI data centers are filled with high-power, denser graphics processing units, or GPUs, which Ren said can run so hot that the servers can "boil your eggs."

Water is about 3,000 times more efficient for cooling down these GPUs, according to Equinix sustainability head Christopher Wellise.

GPUs "run at such a high wattage, a high temperature, you would need nearly hurricane-force winds to actually cool them down if you were just using air," Wellise told CNBC in an interview.

That's why the latest rack-scale AI systems, Nvidia's Vera Rubin and Advanced Micro Devices' Helios, rely on liquid cooling. Most large AI data centers use a closed-loop liquid cooling system that uses very little water on a daily basis.

At an Equinix data center in San Jose, California, CNBC got an up-close look at the closed-loop liquid cooling system used to keep temperatures down in a row of eight Nvidia Grace Blackwell racks owned by AI marketing startup Alembic.

Equinix builds colocation data centers, where companies like Alembic rent space to run their own server racks, while Equinix supplies the power, network connections, interconnectivity and cooling.

Like many data center operators, Equinix doesn't disclose water consumption at individual sites. In 2025, Equinix operations as a whole consumed 1.2 billion gallons of water, up 10% from a year prior.

Despite closed-loop internal cooling like what Equinix uses in San Jose, total water consumption is far more dependent on an additional cooling process that happens outside the data center.

"How do we reject the heat from the closed loop to the ambient environment?" Ren said. "That's where the water usage comes in."

In Lebanon, Indiana, for example, Meta is building a 1GW data center that it says uses a "closed-loop, liquid-cooled system that recirculates the same water and will use zero water for a majority of the year."

Water allocation for the site, however, could ramp up to 8 million gallons per day by 2031, according to the company's law firm. Meta says that figure represents peak usage on the hottest days. "We anticipate rarely reaching this maximum usage level, currently projected as only a few days per year," Meta told CNBC in a statement.

That's because the hottest days of the year will require more water for that external cooling process, removing the heat from that separate internal closed-loop system.

There are two primary methods for this external cooling process at a data center: dry cooling and evaporative cooling. While dry cooling uses far less water, it can require far more power, especially in hotter climates where many of these AI data centers are being built.

"There's no sort of silver bullet," Wellise said. "At some point you need to eject that heat out to the atmosphere. And that's where those trade-offs occur."

Dry cooling relies on fans to blow air across heat exchangers, using the ambient air to cool it down. Those fans are less effective when the outside temperature is high, so data centers turn on refrigeration units to cool down the air first.

"That's a very power-intensive process, and that's exactly why we have power shortages in the summer," Ren said. "Because everybody else turns on their AC."

Evaporative cooling, meanwhile, uses very little power. The hot water circulates through cooling towers or over wet pads that are exposed to air, and the portion that evaporates carries heat away with it. Large amounts of water can be lost through that evaporation on the hottest days.

In between these two extremes, hybrid methods are common as companies try to find a balance.

Some data centers, like Equinix's in San Jose, use dry cooling in the colder months and evaporative cooling when it's hot outside. In Indiana, Meta relies on dry cooling and keeps power consumption down in hotter months by spraying a mist to cool the air instead of relying solely on refrigeration units.

Near some data center projects, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft and others are building water treatment plants to help treat and recycle the necessary water. Meta, for example, spent $100 million on a new water and wastewater system near its data center in Kuna, Idaho.

Outside Memphis, Tennessee, Elon Musk pledged $80 million to build a water recycling plant that's meant to turn treated municipal wastewater into cooling water for SpaceX's Colossus data centers. The first data center there, Colossus 1, came online rapidly in 2024, but construction on the water treatment plant was put on hold in April of this year.

Colossus 1 has a water allocation of up to 1.28 million gallons per day, although it hasn't yet reached that threshold, according to the local utility, Memphis Light, Gas and Water.

Tech firms are also building their own power plants on some data center campuses in an effort to secure enough supply, but those also consume a large amount of water. Colossus gets energy from gas-powered turbines it installed on-site, which have been the subject of multiple lawsuits over alleged Clean Air Act violations and noise pollution.

Meanwhile, some chip companies are directly working on ways to bring the heat of a chip down so less cooling will be needed to begin with.

South Korean startup DeepX makes an AI processor it says runs up to 100°F cooler than competitors. Hyundai Motor will use the DeepX chip in robotics offerings.

SK Hynix, the world's leading maker of high-bandwidth memory, told CNBC in July that it's working on a low-heat memory technology called iHBM that can reduce thermal resistance by 30%.

At Equinix, Wellise said each new site undergoes a water risk analysis to determine which external cooling method to deploy in areas with scarce water.

"Communities are beginning to ask the right questions. They're concerned about water consumption," Wellise said. "We take resource consumption, resource use very seriously."

#2816. Stanford's Jay Leng Jr. breaks new ground with WWT sponsorship

Jay Leng Jr. has gone through plenty of changes in the past three months.

The junior at Stanford won the Western Amateur and then finished runner-up at the U.S. Amateur. He later represented the red, white and blue on the U.S. Walker Cup team in Ireland. Oh, and he made his PGA Tour debut.

Now, he's breaking barriers in the Name, Image and Likeness space.

World Wide Technology, which is sponsoring this week's PGA Tour event at El Cardonal in Cabo San Lucas, Mexico, has partnered with Leng on an NIL deal that will see him wear the WWT logo on his apparel at all PGA Tour and non-collegiate sanctioned events. Leng is the eighth golfer to don the WWT logo but first amateur to do so.

"I'm really excited to be their first NIL ambassador," Leng said. "I know it's not easy to trust a 21-year-old to be an ambassador, but I am glad they see a future for me and I'm grateful for the opportunities."

Other WWT golf ambassadors include Billy Andrade, Ben Griffin, Luke Donald, Austin Eckroat, Keith Mitchell, Taylor Pendrith and Justin Suh. Griffin is the defending champion at the WWT Championship.

Matt Horner, the Executive Vice President of WWT, told Golfweek the way Leng carried himself and the opportunities he presented with a partnership were too good to pass up.

Leng will wear the logo at the Masters Tournament and the U.S. Open at Pebble Beach next year. That, in addition to whatever amateur accolades he may collect in the time being, was a perfect opportunity for partnership.

More: These 6 amateurs earned invites to the 2027 Masters

"I think it's great for us to be able to partner with an athlete like this early on," Horner said. "We're excited to see Jay blossom and continue to grow."

Leng is making his second PGA Tour start this week at El Cardonal looking for his first made cut. He'll do so with a new sponsor on his clothing.

#2815. Sixers Bell Ringer: Brown 30-bomb, Embiid, LeBron double-doubles lead Sixers over Pistons

Bell Ringer Standings:Tyrese Maxey – 3.5VJ Edgecombe – 1Joel Embiid – 1LeBron James – 1Anfernee Simons – 1Jaylen Brown – 0.5

The Philadelphia 76ers are 8-1 to start the season after defeating the Detroit Pistons on the road Wednesday night 112-106.

The Sixers took an early advantage in this one and quickly built a lead into the double-digits thanks to the scoring of Jaylen Brown and the playmaking of LeBron James. Though they wavered at times and let the Pistons inch dangerously close toward the end, the Sixers were able to hold on to secure the win in dramatic fashion. Always gotta make it interesting.

If you're looking for a full game recap, you can find that here! The bottom line is that the Sixers get revenge on the Pistons for their lone loss of the season so far and are now 8-1. Their road trip continues on Friday night with a visit to the Cleveland Cavaliers.

Until then, let's get to the Bell Ringer.

Jaylen Brown: 30 points, 7 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 steal, 1 block

(Photo by Brian Sevald/NBAE via Getty Images)

I've been writing in recent Bell Ringers how Jaylen Brown has a way of scoring an absolute ton of points without you ever even noticing he's slowly adding them up. Tonight, it was a bit more noticeable.

Brown led the floor after one with 10 points on 4-for-6 field goal shooting thanks in part to a buzzer-beating long triple to end the frame. He continued to pile it on in the second, putting up another 13 points in the second. At halftime, he comfortably led the floor with 23 points shooting 8-for-14 from the floor and 3-for-5 from long range.

It felt like no matter who the Pistons had on him in isolation, Brown was able to dance his way to just enough space to pull up and nail a jumper. There are times the offensive scheme of "one guy in iso, everyone else stands still" really doesn't work for the Sixers… but tonight it worked pretty damn well with Brown. He dropped 30 points tonight, his highest mark as a Sixer — all in addition to his usual stellar defense and aggressive rebounding.

He ended up 11-for-19 from the floor and 3-for-5 from three-point range. Brown finished with 30 points, seven rebounds, one assist, a steal and an absolutely massive block on a Cade Cunningham three attempt with under a minute to play and the Pistons trailing by just four.

LeBron James: 13 points, 10 rebounds, 8 assists, 1 steal, 1 block

(Photo by Brian Sevald/NBAE via Getty Images)

It feels weird writing things like this about LeBron James, because they almost seem obvious. Like, of course LeBron James can take over games, that's kind of what he's done for over 20 years now, right? But as someone who is watching him truly night in and night out for the first time in their life, it's just incredible to watch how effortlessly he seems to do it. Tonight, early in the second frame, LeBron locked in and started absolutely carving up the Pistons defense with precisely perfect passes. He assisted three buckets, scored another two field goals himself and came up with a block down the other end all in the first five minutes of the period, helping the Sixers push their lead to double-digits. He had a similar sort of quick takeover sequence in the fourth, but LeBron just overall was impacting every moment he was on the floor tonight.

The King finished tonight with 13 points, 10 rebounds, eight assists, a steal and a block. Still not that first triple-double as a Sixer… but inching ever closer.

Side note fun fact: LeBron James' second field goal of the night was his 16,000th of his career. He is the first player to ever reach that mark in the NBA.

Joel Embiid: 26 points, 15 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal

(Photo by Chris Schwegler/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

The shots weren't all falling from the floor for Joel Embiid early on so he started his night getting going by getting to the foul line and by working the two-man game with LeBron James to get some easier buckets. After one, Embiid already had eight points and five rebounds, even with some of the struggles.

That was the key with Embiid tonight. It wasn't his best game especially early on. It was far from perfect — I thought he was a bit lackadaisical with the ball at times and settled for some bad jumpers when he didn't need to. And yet, he still has so much to contribute even with all of those factors being present. He can hit some incredibly difficult buckets through contact in the paint for and-1 opportunities or make excellent passes to cutting teammates to set them up for easy points. He can set screens that create a ton of space for them. He can still pull in a ton of boards (on both ends) and put up some solid defense. He can knock down crucial free throws at the end of a close game. Embiid can still do a hell of a lot with a mixed bag night.

Embiid finished the night with 26 points, 15 boards (four offensive), two assists and a steal.

#2814. Chinese AI lab Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC

Moonshot AI, a Chinese artificial intelligence lab that the White House accused of accessing Nvidia advanced chips remotely in East Asia, has sought access to the chips through data centers in Europe, sources have told CNBC.

Deals have been discussed this year for Moonshot to rent capacity from neoclouds hosting Nvidia graphics processing units, or GPUs, at facilities in Europe, four people familiar with the matter said. They asked not to be named because they were discussing private information.

Nvidia's effective monopoly over the most powerful chips has made U.S. export controls a key tool in Washington's effort to preserve its advantage over China in the AI race. American AI labs such as OpenAI and Anthropic are locked in a training and pricing battle with Chinese developers, including Moonshot AI, which makes the Kimi family of models.

Moonshot was looking to lease capacity powered by Nvidia's Blackwell systems, advanced chips currently subject to those export controls which effectively ban their import into China, three of the sources told CNBC.

In July, the White House said the Chinese AI lab had accessed Nvidia's Blackwell chips via a facility in Thailand.

U.S. export controls restrict the shipment of Nvidia's most advanced chips to China, but remote access via overseas data centers offers a loophole. Current law does not give regulators broad authority to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

Moonshot, Nvidia, the White House, the U.S. Department of Commerce, China's embassy in the U.K. and its Ministry of Foreign Affairs did not respond to a request for comment.

Chinese developers have made notable capability gains in the past six months and greater access to Nvidia chips could put even more pressure on their American rivals.

To lease Nvidia computing power in Europe, a Chinese AI company would typically rent capacity from a non-Chinese neocloud or data center operator that can purchase advanced chips.

Moonshot was interested in leasing capacity on clusters for inference, which refers to serving models to end users, in Europe, two of the sources said. One of the sources said they were also aware of interest in clusters for training.

Talks for arrangements that would see Moonshot rent access to clusters of 8,000 or more Blackwell GPUs hosted in the region have taken place, one source said. They described the Chinese AI lab as "very aggressive" in its pursuit of more computing power.

One of the sources told CNBC that talks on securing Moonshot compute in Europe had taken place since the White House said it had done so via Thailand. Moonshot did not comment at the time.

Founded in 2023, Beijing-based Moonshot AI is one of China's leading model builders. It released a new model in July that Moonshot said closed the gap with leading systems built by Anthropic and OpenAI.

The model saw significant uptake as a cheaper alternative to U.S. offerings. Chinese AI models have surged in global adoption in 2026, amid capability gains and lower costs than U.S. rivals.

Around the time Kimi K3 was released, Moonshot said demand had stretched its capacity, and it temporarily paused new subscriptions to prioritize compute for current members. "We're adding capacity as fast as we can and will reopen new subscription spots in batches," the company said in a LinkedIn post.

Leasing AI compute is a booming industry, with customers, or offtakers, typically taking capacity for a fee and paying an upfront cost to secure the deal, which is a percentage of the lifetime contract value.

One source said it had been proposed that Moonshot pay 30% to 50% more upfront than a non-Chinese offtaker would.

Arrangements with Chinese customers can be more lucrative upfront, as neoclouds and financing firms look to hedge against a potential future ban of remote access agreements.

In Washington, lawmakers have proposed the Remote Access Security Act (RASA) to expand U.S. export controls to include the cloud-based access of critical hardware and software. It passed the House of Representatives in January but has not passed the Senate.

The Financial Times reported on Tuesday that the chip giant told cloud providers selling access to their chips that they must sell at least 51% of services to non-Chinese customers.

Asked about the reports earlier this week, an Nvidia spokesperson said: "America cannot win the AI race unless America's AI infrastructure wins worldwide — if the world's clouds rely on foreign-made chips, the model and application layers will be foreign-built as well."

Nvidia said in August that Washington had licensed some sales of its H200 AI chips, an older generation, but that "such sales were restricted" by the Chinese government, which has sought to boost domestic chipmakers by limiting access to Nvidia chips.
