#2812. How the midterms could fuel the K-shaped AI trade

The U.S. midterms could widen the AI divide: tighter data center rules may reward tech giants with capacity in hand and squeeze debt-funded companies trying to catch up.

Democratic victories in key gubernatorial races raised the prospect of tougher scrutiny for data center projects, adding to investor concerns about how quickly new capacity can be built and financed.

In Texas, Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has proposed a statewide moratorium on new data center permits, although projects already under construction would reportedly be exempt.

As the markets digested the results on Wednesday, Microsoft and Amazon each surged more than 6%, while AI infrastructure provider CoreWeave plunged around 10%. Chipmakers Micron and Nvidia fell 7% and 3.17%, respectively.

Differences in borrowing costs are playing a key role in the split, James Sullivan, managing director and co-head of global fundamental research at JPMorgan, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Thursday.

Sullivan said borrowing spreads have widened significantly for neocloud companies, leaving their cost of additional capital substantially higher than that of hyperscalers that benefit from more diversified customers and revenue streams.

"The fact that you're seeing this bifurcation in equity performance is largely driven by the view that existing capacity is significantly more valuable now because of incremental supply constraints," he said.

The midterm results could reinforce that view if they make new projects more vulnerable to permitting delays.

BMI warned that a sharp slowdown in data center construction and AI investment could produce a greater-than-expected decline in overall investment, The Business Times reported, citing the firm's post-midterm analysis.

The potential impact is uneven across the construction pipeline. Much of the data center capacity expected to come online in 2027 is already under construction, limiting the near-term impact of new restrictions.

Projects planned for 2028 face greater exposure to permitting delays and regulatory uncertainty, investment bank Nomura said in a note Wednesday about the midterm results.

Tighter regulations on data center construction could become a more significant headwind to AI investment from 2028, the firm said.

Shares of Nvidia-backed neocloud CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren fell at least 30% in October, as tightening credit markets raised questions about how AI infrastructure projects would be financed. CoreWeave's five-year credit default swap spread has surged past 1,100 basis points, reflecting rising costs for investors seeking protection against a potential default.

The distinction may be how well companies can absorb delays and fund the capacity they do build. Hyperscalers have broader cloud businesses and more internally generated cash flow, while lower-rated neoclouds face greater exposure to borrowing costs, energy prices and refinancing conditions, JPMorgan Asset Management said in an Oct. 20 research note.

The asset manager said financially stronger hyperscalers could also secure cheaper capacity or acquire assets if weaker operators scale back.

#2810. Wendy's rejects $1.4 billion takeover proposal from Nelson Peltz’s Trian

Wendy's on Thursday said that it is rejecting a $1.4 billion takeover offer from a group of investors led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Fund Management.

The group offered $7.35 per share in cash on Oct. 28 to take the struggling burger chain private. In addition to Trian, it included investment firms BlueFive Capital and Sixth Street Partners, plus Flynn Group, one of Wendy's largest franchisees.

In February, Wendy's board formed a committee of independent directors to assess any potential bid from Trian in response to a regulatory filing from the firm. The committee found that the proposal "substantially undervalues" Wendy's and does not reflect potential gains from the chain's ongoing turnaround strategy, the company said in a press release on Thursday.

"Our confidence rests firmly in [CEO] Bob Wright's proven track record of seasoned industry leadership, including restaurant business turnarounds," Art Winkleblack, Wendy's board chair and a member of the independent committee, said in a statement.

Trian declined to comment on Wendy's decision.

To stave off a hostile takeover, Wendy's has adopted a shareholder rights plan — or poison pill — with a one-year duration, as of Wednesday, the company said. Wendy's added that the investor group led by Trian rejected an offer from Wendy's for a standstill agreement that would have paused additional share purchases, adding to Wendy's decision to use a poison pill.

The rejection is the latest in the long-running saga between Peltz and the burger chain, which dates back to an activist campaign he led more than two decades ago. A Peltz firm has even owned Wendy's before. In 2008, his company Triarc, which also owned Arby's, bought Wendy's in a $2.3 billion deal. It later sold Arby's, turning Wendy's into a standalone company again.

In 2024, Wendy's named Peltz as chairman emeritus after he spent 17 years on the company's board. Trian executive Peter May and Peltz's son, Bradley, still sit on Wendy's board.

Wendy's also reported its third-quarter results on Thursday. Its same-store sales declined for the seventh straight quarter. However, its quarterly earnings and revenue topped Wall Street's expectations.

Shares of Wendy's fell less than 1% in premarket trading. The takeover offer had lifted the stock, which has lost about 60% of its value over the last three years.

Wendy's is not the only restaurant chain that has found itself in the crosshairs of activist investors recently. Applebee's owner Dine Brands recently adopted a poison pill to block a takeover bid from investment firms Arkhouse Management and Cobblers Cove Capital.

#2809. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2807. Here's what the Democratic election rout could mean for the U.S. economy

The Democratic midterm election sweep could result in changes in economic policies related to trade, spending priorities and artificial intelligence. But the biggest forces shaping the economy aren't going to change just because the party in power did.

Affordability pressures, heavy levels of government spending and growth that is dependent on the artificial-intelligence tailwind are likely to persist, economists say. And all of those factors pose risks to the economy.

"It's in a very kind of fragile place, and I don't know that it would take a lot to push the economy off the rails, and recession becomes a much greater risk," said Mark Zandi, chief economist at Moody's Analytics. "That isn't the case at the moment, but I don't think that's gone away."

Zandi said he had been worried before the election that the economy was under threat from inflation, an underwhelming labor market and geopolitical dangers, and remains concerned now.

Democrats romped to upset wins in not only House and Senate races but also governor and other state races. But economic and foreign policy is largely set in the executive branch, and President Donald Trump remains far apart from his Democratic adversaries, though he said Wednesday he anticipates working with them.

"I don't expect any change in economic policy. The Iran war, the tariffs and trade policy, the heavy-handed immigration policy, all are done under executive order. They're not legislatively driven," Zandi said.

"The president thought they were good policies before the election. I don't know why he'd change his mind after the election," he added. "So I think they will continue to be policies that restrain growth and add to inflationary pressures and interest rates."

Zandi sees the AI hyperscaler build-out as a key to economic growth, but also a source of potential peril.

"The risks are decidedly to the downside because the growth is so dependent on AI, and the AI growth effects are so dependent on the equity market, and the equity market is so dependent on the valuations," he said. "It just feels like kind of a very fragile place, and the labor market is not well functioning."

Local opposition to data centers was a fairly significant issue in the election, particularly for Democrats, according to CNBC polling, so that could be a further economic headwind, though the issue ranked below cost of living, corruption and several others.

The election happened amid a dichotomy in economic views.

Topline numbers such as gross domestic product, consumer spending and the unemployment rate tell the story of an economy growing briskly while consumers spend and most Americans who want jobs are at work. GDP accelerated at a 3.4% annualized pace in the third quarter while consumer spending grew 3.8%. The unemployment rate is at 4.2% while layoffs are around their lowest level since 1969.

However, rising prices have overshadowed those otherwise solid metrics.

The inflation rate in September as judged by the consumer price index was 3.7% — up sharply from 2.3% in April 2025 when Trump announced an aggressive round of so-called reciprocal tariffs against U.S. trading partners. The Iran war has only aggravated those conditions. CPI was at just 2.4% when the U.S. and Israel launched attacks on Iran in late February, a war that has caused gas prices to soar.

And wages aren't keeping up with rising costs. Wage growth when adjusted for inflation has turned negative, down 0.7% from a year ago in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

One area where the two sides of the political divide could come together is tariffs.

Congressional Democrats haven't been reflexively opposed to tariff efforts aimed at leveling the playing field. But the Trump tariffs have been criticized for their sweeping nature.

Gary Cohn, who served as director of the National Economic Council during Trump's first term, said Wednesday that he sees room for movement on trade issues.

"There's become a much more universal view on tariffs and what they're good for," Cohn said during a CNBC interview. "Protecting U.S. workers and protecting manufacturing, protecting jobs in the United States, specifically from China, more than anything, is good and has been good policy."

While Cohn noted that "shutting down the borders between Canada and Mexico and the United States has probably not been so good for policy," he thinks "there's a compromise that could be reached where we actually have tariffs put through the legislative system and they become law. They wouldn't become executive orders that can be rescinded the minute the next president walks off the podium."

Democrats would also likely look to roll back spending cuts to SNAP food stamp benefits and Medicaid that were approved by the Republican-controlled Congress under the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, Morgan Stanley analysts said in a client note. But other policies that Democrats oppose would be harder to reverse.

"We think policy uncertainty since the beginning of President Trump's second term has been tied to executive, not legislative, action," the firm said. "Policies on tariffs, geopolitics, trade, ... deregulation, and export controls are all likely to persist, meaning we should expect a certain level of policy uncertainty to continue."

High levels of government debt and budget deficits also are likely to persist.

The national debt currently stands at $40.5 trillion, up more than $4 trillion since Trump took office in January 2025. Net interest on the debt totaled $1.05 trillion in fiscal 2026, higher than spending on any other category except Social Security and Medicare.

The U.S. ended the year with a deficit just shy of $2 trillion, or about 6.1% of GDP. Morgan Stanley expects that level to persist even with some Democratic officials preaching fiscal discipline.

While there are concerns that the change in power could result in standoffs over the debt ceiling, "Democrats are likely to leverage [an impasse] to win policy concessions," Goldman Sachs chief economist Jan Hatzius said in a note.

"It is not yet clear what Democrats will seek in return, but limiting the war with Iran, tariff rollbacks, the reversal of spending cuts, and expanded health benefits appear to be high on the list of priorities," he said.

Despite the various headwinds and political uncertainty, most of Wall Street expects the growth in the economy to continue — if not without hiccups and lingering issues over the cost of living.

"My sense is that the economic expansion will continue to roll on, featuring above trend growth near 2½ percent," said Joseph Brusuelas, chief economist at RSM.

Brusuelas also sees the opportunity to "reset" the trade agreement between U.S., Mexico and Canada after a year and a half of tension from the Trump tariffs and reprisals.

"We only have a 15% probability of recession over the next 12 months," he said. "The outcome of the election was immaterial to that estimate."

#2806. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2805. The Jazz Peli-can’t snap out of losing streak, their 6th straight

In the darkest, inner circle of the inferno, the Jazz had fallen into, all Jazz fans wanted was a sign of life that this team was still alive and kicking.

Missing Jusuf Nurkic and Jaren Jackson Jr. with an uncertain timetable, a 1-7 start would be detrimental. They've battled against top dogs like the Spurs, Blazers and Grizzlies, meaning this was one of the few chances to bounce back as the worst team in the West. Utah had already taken down the Pelicans in their home opener, marking their first and only win on the season.

New Orleans had been on their own soul-searching journey in their 2026-27 campaign. They lost their first four games on the calendar, three of them coming up short in the clutch. But now, their record stands at 3-4; they handed Memphis their first loss of the year while taking down the Mavs and Kings on the side.

But unfortunately, that well-sought victory was not today. It was certainly the most alive the Jazz have looked all week, but ultimately they still fell into the same lapses that have plagued them all season. The Pelicans stole this one out of the Jazz's hands, 120-113.

The pace was all over the place in the first quarter. There was even a 2-minute scoring drought for both teams midway through the early going, an early indicator that Utah was going to start yet another game ice-cold from the floor. There are times when you just have to sigh at the Jazz's attempt to run an offense — using traditional big men as playmaking hubs, force-feeding players when they don't have the hot hand, and just the lack of team synergy that leads to a possession ending with a shot-chuck at the buzzer. Fortunately, at the hand of Keyonte George, the Jazz answered back to erase an 8-point deficit. They switched away from the three-point line to score 18 of their first 20 points inside the paint.

Then, it was Saddiq Bey's second quarter. He scored eight consecutive points for New Orleans to give the Pelicans a crescive 9-point lead. Utah missed seven consecutive shots to open up that quarter. It was another ugly one; the lackadaisical passes destroyed any rhythm the Jazz could build. Really, one of the bigger silver linings was holding Zion scoreless in the first half. 2-20 from three. 3-8 from the charity stripe. 13 turnovers. A big energy shift was needed.

And a shift it was. The dynamic went nearly completely in Utah's favor, turning around what was a tight deficit into a 10-point advantage. George jumpstarted the engine of the offense, pushing his game total to 29. Led by Mo Bamba's intimidating presence off the bench, the Jazz dominated he glass. Utah out-rebounded New Orleans 38 to 31 up until that point, dropping the Pelicans' second-chance points to 5 in the third quarter.

But I'd be lying if I said the Jazz could sit back and ride out a confident double-digit victory. New Orleans instantly opened on a 16-2 run to erase Utah's advantage. Hardy called a timeout about three possessions too late, fuelling the fire for the Pelicans. The two teams battled neck and neck over the next few minutes. A Keyonte floater. Zion free throws. Keyonte and-1. Derik Queen drive. Jaxson Hayes two. The Jazz held a nailbiting 102-102 lead with the final play, then:

The cruel joke played on the Jazz was that George let go of the ball 0.1 seconds too late. We're headed to OT.

That seemed to light a fire in him, as Keyonte nailed the first two buckets in the overtime period off tough three-point looks. The Pelicans responded with six unanswered points; the Jazz stood no chance against the physicality of Zion and Bey. There were some serious moments in this stretch that questioned whether Will Hardy's job was on the line (which seems to be the consensus).

The Jazz are firmly positioned in their last spot in the Western Conference, falling well into the lottery delegation zone; their pick was traded to the Grizzlies in the Jackson Jr. trade. While the losses are looming, the competetive nature of the fourth quarter is a fight we needed. Whether the rest will come is still under debate.

It was a rough two-game stretch for Keyonte, resulting in consecutive blowouts, but he had success tonight by dealing most of his damage inside, using his speed to attack the paint and finish with contact at the rim. He was highly disruptive on the defensive end too, chipping in four steals and an unexpected block.

It's nearly an undeniable fact that Keyonte has had to be 'the man' nearly every single night among his peers' shooting droughts. He's taken a noticable leap forward as a lead scorer and a focal point of the offense when others have missed time with injury. He looks much more confident creating his own shot and initiating the offense compared to last year.

To float in the Western Conference, this team desperately needs a second initiator/scorer. Wins won't come off Keyonte alone.

There was no other way to describe Ace on the scoring end in this one other than an unfortunate shooting night. While he played relatively well inside the arc, he spent all game searching for his rhythm from deep, having missed all 8 of his 9 of his three-point attempts. He got the looks he wanted, but couldn't take advantage. Ace finished with 15 points, 5 boards and a block on 6-for-14 shooting.

Though it's not all doom and gloom. Beyond the box score, Ace's physical presence showed in his off-ball activity. The team used him frequently to absorb switches on the perimeter and cut off driving angles for the monster of muscle, otherwise known as Zion Williamson.

A point of growth in his game has been managing his eagerness to jump at ball fakes. Tonight, he displayed a more grounded, disciplined stance and relied on his standing reach to contest shots without overcommitting. The motor is awesome to watch, no matter how he's playing on the other end of the floor.

Utah drops to 1-7 with this loss and lines up their next matchup in Dallas on Friday night.

#2804. ’Nole of the Week: Lucy Porter on her journey from Australia to FSU

The FSView has chosen to highlight Florida State sophomore swimmer Lucy Porter as its latest 'Nole of the Week. Originally from Perth, Australia, Porter has left her mark on the Seminoles' swimming and diving program while balancing life as a student-athlete.

Porter made an immediate impact after arriving midyear in her freshman season, qualifying for the 2026 NCAA D1 Women's Swimming and Diving Championships in the 200-yard breaststroke as a spring enrollee. Her personal-best time of 2:08.77, set at Georgia's Bulldog Invitational Last Chance Meet, made her one of FSU's top performers in the event.

Porter spoke with the FSView about moving from Australia to Tallahassee, her experience competing at the national level, and balancing swimming with her studies.

FSView Q: Can you tell me a little about your background growing up in Perth, Australia, and how you first got involved in swimming?

Porter: Everyone in Perth grows up swimming. I took swimming lessons as early as three and a bunch of my friends were swimming competitively in high school so I decided to try it and I really loved it.

Q: What led you to move to the United States and pick Florida State's swimming program?

Porter: I knew I wanted to experience something different from home and moving halfway across the world seemed like a good way to do that. I think what drew me to FSU was probably the team atmosphere. I fit in really well with the girls. It was a place where I could challenge myself academically and, in the pool, while enjoying my experience.

Q: What was the biggest adjustment moving from Australia to Tallahassee for you?

Porter: Learning that Americans have a very different vocabulary for things that were universally understood. I am still trying to get used to measuring everything in yards.

Q: You qualified for the 2026 NCAA Championships in the 200-yard breaststroke during your freshman season. What did that accomplishment mean to you?

Porter: I wasn't entirely sure what to expect coming into college swimming. Being able to contribute to the team and compete at that level has been really rewarding and motivated me to keep improving.

Q: You recorded a personal-best time of 2:08.77 in the event. Can you walk me through that performance and what you think contributed to it?

Porter: The support of my coaches and teammates. Being surrounded by such an amazing group of people who just want to see you succeed and push each other really motivates you.

Q: Looking back at your first season at FSU, what moment are you most proud of, and why?

Porter: I think scoring points at Atlantic Coast Conference's and qualifying for NCAA's has been two of my biggest accomplishments so far.

Q: How do you balance the demands of swimming with your academics as a biochemistry major?

Porter: I think it's less about finding a perfect balance and knowing what needs your attention at specific times. Some weeks swimming takes priority and other weeks academics do. You have to adapt.

Q: What is something you do to wind down after a day of swimming and school?

Porter: I call my parents or friends from home to see what they are doing to get my mind off of things.

Q: What is your favorite thing about your swimming program?

Porter: Getting to be surrounded by such amazing teammates and coaches. Everyone has their own individual goals and everyone has a shared understanding of how much work goes into the sport.

Q: What is your opinion on Buc-ee's when you stopped on the way to Atlanta?

Porter: I was so surprised how big it was, we really have nothing like that in Australia.

Porter and the Seminoles' next scheduled meet is on Nov. 6 at 11 a.m. in Columbia, South Carolina, following the USA Short Course World Championship Trials Oct. 28 to 31 in Westmont, Illinois.

Follow @FSView on Instagram to vote for next week's 'Nole of the Week and submit your questions for the next featured athlete.

Abbey Elalouf is a Staff Writer for the FSView & Florida Flambeau, the student-run, independent online news service for the FSU community. Email our staff at contact@fsview.com.

Kansas is still waiting to see if starting quarterback Isaiah Marshall will play this weekend.

#2798. Dalton Knecht 'came in ready to go' in Knicks debut: 'It was great'

Wednesday's game was the perfect chance for head coach Mike Brown to have Dalton Knecht make his Knicks debut.

After being waived by the Los Angeles Lakers, New York signed Knecht to a two-way contract roughly two weeks ago -- the 25-year-old had been playing with the Westchester Knicks in the NBA G League to learn the team's systems and get consistent playing time.

With the Knicks facing the 1-6 Chicago Bulls at Madison Square Garden, the team decided to make Knecht active. So when the small forward checked in for Andre Drummond with 3:20 left in the second quarter and New York up, 70-39, he was ready.

"I wasn't surprised, I just came in ready to go," Knecht said. "The coaches said you're active so that just means be ready."

Knecht got off to a great start less than a minute into his debut by following a three-point miss by Landry Shamet, grabbing the offensive rebound, and slamming it home for a putback dunk that got the MSG crowd buzzing.

"It was great. Honestly I didn't hear much just because I'm so locked in, but it was good," Knecht said about his nifty play. "Pretty much my first play was a putback dunk so it's cool, it's great."

Including his high-flying highlight, Knecht had 10 points on 4-for-6 shooting from the field, including two threes, and grabbed seven boards in just 16 minutes. He also had an assist and a block, looking good and reminding everybody why he was the 17th overall pick in the 2024 NBA Draft.

"I think I just go out there and impact the game on all three levels," Knecht said. "And I just wanna show on the other side of the ball, getting the ball pressure and stuff like that and going in and grabbing defensive rebounds."

It's hard to glean too much from Knecht's performance with it coming in a blowout against the hapless Bulls, but it's still good to see the talent is there. After all, in his rookie season, Knecht averaged 9.1 points and 2.8 rebounds in 19.2 minutes per game before taking a step back last season.

It's unclear how Brown plans to utilize Knecht this season, but as he gets more comfortable with his new team and teammates, he could be a good option for a team always looking for bench depth.

No matter what, Knecht is staying ready to make the most out of his fresh start.

"Be ready so you don't have to get ready," he said. "Just watching on the bench and talking to my teammates is really helpful and it was easy to get in there and play confident. All the guys out there gave me a good look."

#2797. NFL ranking of top 50 trade prospects is full of potential Bengals targets

The Cincinnati Bengals are 5-2 as they prepare for their Week 9 game in Madrid, Spain, against the 3-5 Atlanta Falcons. They're riding a two-game win streak, though the wins were ugly at times, and are very much in the mix to make a run at the AFC North title to host a playoff game for the first time since 2023.

Things aren't all sunshine and rainbows, though. The Bengals are dealing with some injuries, and many analysts believe they're aggressively pursuing an acquisition to help the team make a run. Considering they already traded the No. 10 overall selection in this year's draft to the Giants for Dexter Lawrence, a rare mid-season trade from the Bengals would likely blow the collective minds of fans across the globe.

Here are some of the players the Bengals could benefit from, in order from top to bottom on the list:

Taylor is one of the best running backs in the league and has been for some time. Since he was drafted in 2020, Taylor has more scrimmage yards than all RBs except for Derrick Henry. He is the type of explosive player who could change an offense. After firing GM Chris Ballard, the Colts should be considering all options, including a full tear-down and rebuild. Taylor signed a two-year extension with the Colts in August. Indianapolis would take on $10 million in dead money in 2027 if it traded Taylor, so the contract is not preventive.

This would have made sense a couple of weeks ago, but there are issues in the secondary that can't be ignored. Still, the Bengals do need to figure out how to fix their running game, because they'll need it in December and January.

Downs is in the final year of his rookie deal, and he has developed into one of the better route runners in the league. He is predominantly a slot player, aligning inside on more than 74 percent of his career offensive snaps. The Colts signed receiver Alec Pierce to a four-year, $114 million extension in the offseason. Pierce has played 49 snaps this season while battling injuries. Downs is good, cheap and on an expiring deal. He would be a potentially valuable trade piece if interim GM Ed Dodds is tasked with bolstering the draft cupboard.

Do the Bengals really need a wide receiver? No. But Downs could fit in nicely. He's a true slot receiver, and he would immediately be the Bengals' WR3, which is a spot currently held by TE1 Mike Gesicki. With some injuries at wide receiver, including to Andrei Iosivas, and the relative inexperience of everyone else, adding another talented receiver wouldn't hurt.

Though Hooker is under contract through 2028, he does not have any fully guaranteed money on his deal after this season. He is a versatile safety who can fulfill multiple responsibilities — post safety, split-field safety, nickel, dime, underneath zone and man-to-man on tight ends and slot receivers. He is a strong tackler who can impact in the running game, and he has the instincts in zone coverage to feel routes and bait quarterbacks into throws. The Titans would take on $8.6 million in dead money in 2027 if they traded Hooker.

How crazy would it be for Hooking to pick Joe Burrow off three times in Week 8 just to join him a couple of weeks later? The Bengals have dealt with injuries at safety. Bryan Cook has missed a couple of games, as has Kyle Dugger, and Jordan Battle has been hit or miss (mostly miss). A trade for Hooker could shore up the secondary.

Robertson signed a two-year, $16 million deal with the Commanders in free agency. He has been one of the few bright spots from Peters' offseason. Robertson does not have any fully guaranteed money on his deal in 2027. The Commanders would take on $3 million in dead money in 2027 if they trade him before the deadline. Robertson is a capable man coverage corner who could help a number of teams in the secondary. He can play inside and outside.

Here's really where the Bengals need some help, especially if DJ Turner is forced to miss some time. The cornerback position was pretty much an afterthought this offseason, and now they're potentially going to pay the piper for it. If the Bengals do make a move, I predict it will be for a corner.

Woolen won a Super Bowl with the Seahawks last season. He then hit free agency and, rather surprisingly, a market did not materialize for him. Woolen ended up signing a one-year deal with the Eagles. Although he has given up two touchdowns, Woolen has played well in coverage down-to-down for Philadelphia. He was in tight coverage on the Parker Washington one-handed touchdown in the Eagles' loss to the Jaguars in Week 5. The combination of size and speed will always make Woolen an intriguing piece.

Another possibility at corner. He could join Boye Mafe, whom he won a Super Bowl with in Seattle. He could fill in for a starter on the outside should the need arise. That way the Bengals wouldn't be forced to rely on Josh Newton, who reportedly spent much of the offseason in the doghouse.

Jackson is in the final year of a two-year free-agent deal he signed in 2025. He has missed the past two games with a hamstring injury. Jackson is one of the most compelling defensive backs on this list. He is on an expiring deal. He is relatively cheap, so he is an option for teams tight against the cap. And he is an experienced veteran who can acclimate quickly to a variety of schemes, though a zone-heavy scheme would be the best fit. Jackson has 23 career interceptions, tied for fifth-most among active players.

Same thing here. The Bengals could definitely use some help in the cornerback room. Even if Turner's heel injury allows him to play this weekend, will it limit him? He's required to be able to stay hip to hip with some of the best athletes in the world. If his foot hurts, will he be able to?

The Jets signed Wright to a one-year deal in free agency, and he has yet to play a defensive snap this season for coach Aaron Glenn. Wright should be a trade candidate even if the Jets win games over the next three weeks. He had five picks playing for the Chicago Bears in 2025. Wright lacks some burst and change-of-direction ability. But his ball production last season was very impressive. At 6-foot-4, he has receiver-like skills in 50-50 situations.

The Bengals were actually listed as one of the best fits for Wright, who had a good 2025 season but is struggling to get on the field this year. He could be had for little and potentially make a major impact for Al Golden's secondary.

Pollard, who signed with the Titans in free agency in 2024, is on an expiring contract. He has been getting the bulk of the work in the Tennessee backfield over Spears. Pollard is a veteran doubles hitter with some vision. He is reliable in pass protection. Pollard has the potential to be a supplemental piece in a running back rotation.

Pollard carried the ball 21 times for 117 yards and two touchdowns against the Bengals in Week 8. He might not be as flashy as Taylor or Saquon Barkley, but he would probably help. At least more than Tahj Brooks.

There were obviously others, but these were just some that caught my eye.

What trade targets from the list (and not on the list) are you most interested in for the Bengals before the Nov. 10 deadline?

#2796. Karl and Laimer on target as Bayern Munich cruise past Atlético Madrid

Looking to get back to winning ways in Europe after drawing 1-1 with Stuttgart last time out, Atlético Madrid made a lively start as Bayern visited the Metropolitano, with talisman Julian Alvarez curling an effort narrowly wide of Manuel Neuer's far post inside three minutes.

Bayern then quickly took control, but despite enjoying over 70 per cent of the ball in the opening 20 minutes, they failed to register a shot, while Atléti midfielder Pablo Barrios saw a deflected effort fly just over.

Lennart Karl threatened midway through the half, firing over from the edge of the box, while Kane put an effort just wide following a delivery from Konrad Laimer.

On the half-hour mark, the hosts should have taken the lead, with Alexander Sørloth handed a chance he will not want to see again, with the Norwegian unable to sort his feet out following a ball to the back stick from Marc Pubill.

And after both sides failed to manage a shot on target before the break, Joshua Kimmich finally tested Jan Oblak shortly after the restart, before Karl broke the deadlock in the 54th minute.

Robin Le Normand appeared to have the ball under control near the penalty spot after blocking an effort from Tom Bischof, but the youngster then stole possession from behind the Spanish defender and fired the visitors into the lead.

Koke, Ademola Lookman and Lee Kang-in would come on for the hosts five minutes later, and Los Colchoneros began to find their feet after the changes, with Marcos Llorente forcing Neuer into his first save of the evening in the 67th minute.

Goalscorer Karl was replaced by Nathaniel Brown shortly afterwards, before Kane went close to doubling Bayern's advantage, only for the flag to be raised after the England captain lifted an effort narrowly over after rounding the onrushing Oblak.

Bayern would double their lead in the 74th minute, when Laimer hammered in off the crossbar from a tight angle following a beautifully clipped ball from Kane.

In the ascendancy and carrying a two-goal lead, the record German champions were well on track to pick up their first Champions League win since the opening week triumph against Bodø/Glimt.

Bayern did think they had a third in the 82nd minute, when Dayot Upamecano headed home from a corner, before the effort was ruled out for a foul, and left-back Brown then had a chance to extend the lead, only to be denied by Oblak on the angle.

Seeing out the win, Bayern take three points back to Munich with them and turn their European form back around after successive draws, while attention now turns to Saturday's trip to Mainz.

#2792. Joe Mazzulla has faith Queta, Robinson will find their footing with Boston Celtics

With a few notable exceptions, the Boston Celtics big men have not had the best start to the 2026-27 NBA regular season. And given Mitchell Robinson won a title with the New York Knicks this summer and Neemias Queta is seeing his role change with the arrival of Robinson, both have something they can point to explain if not excuse it.

But Celtics head coach Joe Mazzulla is not too worried about that dynamic, instead trusting his centers to sort out their roles sooner than later. "The fives are the most important position that we have on both ends of the floor because of their ability to navigate our defense and the offensive's ... way they play directly has a positive impact on the other guys," said Mazzulla on Wednesday (Nov. 4) night. "And so there's a relationship aspect there that's really important. Everyone on our team knows that position is extremely important."

"They have to be one of the smartest positions, and they do a great job of that. I think what's not being valued is (Mitchell Robinson's) ability to help everyone else," he added. "His individual rebounding might be down a little bit, but what's not down is how he's positively impacting the other guys. (How he is) getting cleaner shots for guys, keeping matchups so that teams can't cross-match and they have to stay on him because of his size and getting there."

"There's a lot of stuff that's not measured that he's doing good," said Mazzulla. "The easiest measurable thing is the one thing that he's been great at his whole career, which is rebounding."

"And some of that (is that) he has to get better at it. But he's doing stuff for the other guys that's making it look easier for them, and that's a product of him. And you can't measure that, so that's on us to be able to show that stuff in film and measure that stuff."

#2791. Game Recap: Suns fall to 1-5 after 132-119 loss to Trail Blazers

The Phoenix Suns began their seven-game homestand by hosting the team that obliterated them on opening night. And while the final 132-119 score wasn't nearly as embarrassing as that initial beatdown, the result was another decisive victory for Portland, which improved to 6-1 on the season and a perfect 5-0 on the road. Meanwhile, the Suns dropped to 1-5.

Devin Booker put together a solid performance, scoring 33 points, with much of his damage coming at the free-throw line, where he went a perfect 13-of-13. However, he finished with only two assists against two turnovers and struggled from beyond the arc, shooting 2-of-8. Jalen Green added 22 points, 12 of which came in the fourth quarter, although he also shot 2-of-8 from deep. Miles Bridges contributed 18 points and seven rebounds, while Dillon Brooks finished with 15 points and six rebounds.

For Portland, Donovan Clingan dominated the interior, recording 24 points and 10 rebounds on an efficient 9-of-12 shooting. Damian Lillard added 25 points, while Deni Avdija chipped in 21.

It was one of those nights when all the little things added up, and unfortunately for Phoenix, they added up to another loss. Portland won the battle in the paint, outscoring Phoenix 56-46. The Blazers controlled the second-chance points battle 17-11, dominated fast-break scoring 19-9, and received a significant advantage from their bench, which outscored the Suns' reserves 42-25. Then there was the three-point shooting. Portland connected on 14 threes at a 35.9% clip, while Phoenix managed only 10 makes on 27.8% shooting.

Across the board, the Blazers consistently found ways to win the margins that matter. When you lose that many battles within a game, it's awfully difficult to win the war.

It was a much more stable start to the game than when these teams first met on October 21. If you recall, and I'm sure you do, the Suns trailed 21–0 in that one. This time around, it took only a minute for Devin Booker to knock down an 18-foot jumper and get the scoring started for Phoenix.

The first challenge of the evening came with 8:56 remaining in the opening quarter, when Deni Avdija appeared to charge into Dillon Brooks, but a blocking foul was called on Brooks. The Suns successfully challenged the call, negating what would have been Brooks' second foul early in the game.

Phoenix looked considerably more connected on both ends of the floor throughout the first half of the opening quarter. Outside of Khaman Maluach, every starter had scored at least four points within the first six minutes, a welcome sight for a team that has struggled to find any semblance of offensive consistency.

Defensively, there were still some issues navigating the pick-and-roll, particularly with Donovan Clingan finding opportunities to score on the interior. But outside of those breakdowns, the Suns looked much more connected, communicating and rotating with a level of cohesion that had been noticeably absent in their previous meeting with Portland.

Phoenix put together a 9–2 run after the second unit entered the game, overcoming an initially choppy stretch marked by careless turnovers. Their effort on the glass was clearly a point of emphasis, and they were executing well, outrebounding Portland 13–10 in the opening quarter.

Booker came out on a heater, shooting 4-of-6 from the field for 12 first-quarter points, which is always an encouraging sign. Dillon Brooks added eight points, two rebounds, and a steal as the Suns shot 56.5% from the field while holding the Blazers to 3-of-11 from beyond the arc. The result was a 34–29 Phoenix lead heading into the second quarter.

The Trail Blazers opened the second quarter on an 11-5 run, fueled once again by Donovan Clingan, who continued attacking the interior and creating mismatches for Phoenix. Damian Lillard did his part as well, helping Portland put together an 8-0 run that further shifted the momentum.

By the midway point of the quarter, the pressure Portland was putting on Phoenix had become increasingly difficult to overcome. The Blazers were shooting 8-of-9 from the field while simultaneously drawing multiple fouls on the Suns. The offensive avalanche Phoenix had managed to avoid in the first quarter finally arrived in the second.

Portland outscored Phoenix 43-25 in the quarter, shooting 14-of-19 from the field, a ridiculous 74%, while connecting on 6-of-11 attempts from beyond the arc.

Phoenix shot 1-of-7 from deep and had 5 turnovers to the Blazers 0. Defensively, however, they reverted to the same troubling habits we've seen throughout the early portion of the season, collapsing into the paint whenever Portland attacked and leaving shooters wide open on the perimeter. And the Blazers made them pay. Jrue Holiday went 3-of-4 from beyond the arc for nine points in the quarter, Lillard added nine of his own, and Clingan chipped in eight.

Meanwhile, Booker scored seven points in the period, bringing his total to 19 at halftime. Phoenix continued to hold the advantage on the glass, but that becomes a moot point when the opposing team seemingly can't miss a shot. After experiencing an entire game's worth of shooting variance in the season opener against Portland, the Suns were treated to a quarter's worth this time around.

The Suns opened the third quarter with an offensive push, outscoring Portland 12–6 behind eight points from Devin Booker, who went 2-of-2 from the field, including a three-pointer, and 3-of-3 from the free-throw line. Portland was considerably sloppier than it had been in the second quarter, committing three turnovers as Phoenix trimmed its deficit to seven points.

In what has become a nightly tradition, Dillon Brooks found himself in a shouting match, this time with Damian Lillard, following a turnover in which Brooks stepped out of bounds in transition while attempting a three-pointer. No technicals were assessed during the exchange, but the drama wasn't finished. On Portland's ensuing possession, Brooks attempted to box out Donovan Clingan, who grabbed him by the jersey and pulled him to the floor. Officials reviewed the play for a potential hostile act, ultimately assessing Clingan a technical foul.

Jrue Holiday continued to be a menace, as the Suns repeatedly left him wide open on the perimeter, and he continued to make them pay, knocking down another pair of threes in the third quarter.

Moments later, Deni Avdija found himself under review for a potential hostile act after appearing to deliver a flying knee to Dillon Brooks' groin area. Officials ultimately ruled the contact incidental.

All those whistles started to become an issue in the third quarter, as Devin Booker, Dillon Brooks, and Jalen Green each found themselves with four fouls and four and a half minutes still remaining in the period.

Booker eventually headed to the locker room after driving to the basket and taking a shot to the head from Robert Williams. What made the sequence particularly frustrating was the inconsistency of the officiating. After spending much of the quarter calling every ticky-tack bit of contact imaginable, the officials somehow swallowed their whistles when Booker got smacked in the head. No foul. Nothing.

The lengthy third quarter mercifully came to an end, with the Suns getting outscored 32-26. Chalk up seven more fouls against Phoenix, which struggled offensively, shooting 7-of-20 from the field and a miserable 3-of-12 from two-point range. And the rebounding? That became a problem once again, as Portland dominated the glass 13–6 while scoring 18 of its 32 points in the paint.

Booker continued to lead the charge for Phoenix, pouring in 14 points in the quarter, but he finished the period in the locker room after taking that shot to the head. The Suns trailed 104-85 entering the fourth.

Devin Booker returned with 10 minutes remaining in the fourth quarter and the Suns trailing by 18 points.

With Robert Williams on the floor, Phoenix simply didn't have anyone capable of matching his size and physicality. Duop Reath was getting absolutely destroyed on the interior, as Williams grabbed four offensive rebounds in the first two minutes of the quarter alone.

We also saw Jalen Green become considerably more aggressive in the fourth, attacking the basket with a level of purpose that had been missing earlier in the game. After scoring 12 points through the first three quarters, Green shifted into attack mode, repeatedly getting downhill and creating opportunities at the free-throw line. And this time, he actually made his free throws. Miles Bridges was more engaged in the fourth quarter as well, although much like Green's performance, it felt like too little, too late.

Phoenix kept its offense humming, and there were moments when it felt like the Suns were on the verge of making a meaningful dent in Portland's lead. One sustained run could have put them within striking distance with a couple of minutes remaining. But every time Phoenix threatened to make things interesting, Portland had an answer. There was always a response. Always a reaction to the action.

With 2:51 remaining, Devin Booker fouled out for the second consecutive game, and at that point, the Suns emptied their bench. Ball game.

We are right back here tomorrow night as the Suns host the Orlando Magic, who are currently 2-5 and last played in a loss against the Wizards. We shall see you then!

#2790. Cowboys news: The lack of snaps for George Pickens explained

If the Cowboys are going to find themselves in any kind of position to win a bad NFC East division, their defense has got to improve. Multiple players have spoken about the state of the unit, and the solutions don't seem to be that difficult, but they have to put it all together on the field.

One of the big topics around Dallas lately has been wide receiver George Pickens and his playing time, or lack thereof. There's been speculation as to why this is, but it was recently cleared up by head coach Brian Schottenheimer.

The reduction of snaps for Pickens has been very noticeable over the last few weeks. Cowboys head coach Brian Schottenheimer breaks down why that's been the case.

"Brian Schottenheimer told me that George Pickens snaps have been limited by his injuries," Clarence Hill of DLLS Cowboys said. "He has been taken off the field because blocking in the run game."

Schottenheimer added that it "would be poor coaching by me" to keep Pickens off the field for any reason.

That checks out because Pickens was added to the injury report in Week 7 due to a shoulder issue and he remained on it through last week.

The Cowboys defense has had trouble with consistency in 2026. According to defensive tackle Quinnen Williams, the solution is quite simple.

In going back and watching the film, one thing stood out to the defenders on what did not happen: execution.

"The biggest thing is execute," defensive tackle Quinnen Williams said. "We got to execute. We haven't been executing this whole season. We executed for three quarters and didn't execute that last quarter, and the film says it all. We have to execute and stack days."

Its been a tough season on the injury front for the Cowboys secondary. When they face the Indianapolis Colts this Sunday, it could be slim pickings in terms of who is available in that area.

It looks like those injuries could be a factor again. The Cowboys had 11 players who didn't practice Wednesday or were limited due to injury. That included two starting defensive backs in corner Caleb Downs and safety P.J. Locke. Both suffered concussions in Sunday's 33-30 overtime loss to the Arizona Cardinals. Schottenheimer said both Downs and Locke were still in concussion protocol as of Wednesday. Both were seen on the field during practice participating in workouts with helmets, but not drills.

Cowboys owner Jerry Jones said earlier this week that he didn't see any correlation between how much a team spends on a head coach and winning. That could be part of the reason why Dallas hasn't won in such a long time.

Or, as a coaching agent told NFL Network's Jane Slater when asked how aggressive Jones had been in looking for a veteran coach after the 2024 season: "Jerry won't pay $13M and up, and that's the starting point."

Instead, Dallas promoted Schottenheimer, its own offensive coordinator and a longtime assistant. The Cowboys were the only team to interview him that year. Schottenheimer might prove to be a good coach despite his 10-14-1 start, but should the most valuable team on the planet be led by a guy who has to grow into the job?

Dallas has had another shaky season defensively so far. With that said, linebacker Shemar James says the unit is eager to get things on track.

"I feel there's a sense of urgency every week, whether we win or lose, because every week is a dogfight," James added. "I mean, we're in a National Football League, so there's not going to be a week that we have circled like, 'Oh, we're going to beat these guys.'

"You can't take [a game] off. Every week, we have a sense of urgency to go out there and execute, and it just didn't get done this weekend. And that's why we have another week to go out there and prove to people that we're the defense that we know we can be."

#2788. Commanders' defense has historic night in Week 8

Despite Javon Kinlaw and Daron Payne not being in the game, the Washington Commanders defense had a historic night against the Philadelphia Eagles on Sunday Night Football with a season-high in pressures and one of the best performances using the blitz of any defense in the NFL this season.

Washington held Philadelphia to just 10 points, the Eagles' second-fewest points in a game this season. That was also the first time this season the Commanders have held an opponent under 20 points. Their defense finished the game with a total of five sacks and held quarterback Tanner McKee to just 212 passing yards. They also contained running back Saquon Barkley, who only had 30 rushing yards.

The defense's performance allowed Washington to run away with this game in the second half as the Eagles did not score after the first quarter. In fact, Philadelphia did not have a drive that lasted more than 60 yards until the end of the game, when they had a 34-yard completion as time expired.

Washington's defense will need to be just as dominant this coming Sunday against the Los Angeles Rams. The Rams are dealing with injury issues of their own, especially on defense, which should open things up for the Commanders offense. However, Washington's secondary will still have to contend with All-Pro receiver Puka Nacua, who has 19 receptions for 148 yards and two touchdowns in the past two games.

#2787. Laimer praises Bayern's belief after Atlético win and makes bold Karl prediction

Bayern Munich secured an impressive victory in the Spanish capital on Tuesday evening, with second-half goals from Lennart Karl and Konrad Laimer earning the Bavarians all three points.

Speaking to Amazon Prime after the match, Laimer was delighted with Bayern's performance, particularly given the questions surrounding the squad in recent weeks.

"We went into the game knowing what we're capable of. There has been a lot written about us and everything surrounding the team, but we have big confidence in our squad and the players we have," the Austrian said.

"I think we showed that over the full 90 minutes today: the way we can play football and the way we want to play.

"Ultimately, I think we could even have scored a few more goals. It was a thoroughly deserved win against a good team."

Laimer also reserved special praise for 18-year-old Lennart Karl, who opened the scoring in the 54th minute after winning possession inside the Atléti penalty area.

"I'm really happy for the boy. He stays calm, he wants to improve, he works hard and today he was rewarded for it.

"He showed that real fighting spirit. He tracks back, wins the ball back and then somehow manages to smash it into the net.

"He's young and still has a lot to learn. He still needs to take another step forward, but he's on a fantastic path. I'm absolutely delighted for him, and I think he has a great career ahead of him."

Laimer then discussed his stunner in the second half, which came after an excellent pass from Harry Kane, revealing that his teammates had joked about how long he took to shoot.

"The boys asked me, 'Why did you take so long to shoot?' I thought I'd take a bit more time this time before doing something too quickly, as I sometimes do, only for it not to come off properly.

"Ultimately, Harry played an outstanding pass. I controlled it, looked up to see if there was anything else I could do, and then simply finished it with a clean, composed strike.

"Sometimes you can tell as soon as the ball leaves your foot whether it's going to be a good one or not. And I immediately thought, 'Oh, yes, that feels really good!'"

After getting back to winning ways in Europe, Bayern now turn their attention to Saturday's trip to Mainz, where they will be looking to go into the international break with three points under their belt.

#2786. Knicks enjoy history-tying 84-point first half in dominant win

The Knicks broke out of their early-game slump in historic fashion.

They erupted for 84 points in the first half of their 135-105 rout of the Bulls on Wednesday night at Madison Square Garden. It tied a franchise record set on Nov. 18, 1988, against the 76ers.

But the Knicks actually lost that game, 137-135.

Wednesday, on the other hand, represented the Knicks' best and by far most complete showing of the season.

Jalen Brunson celebrates after hitting a 3-pointer during the Knicks' 135-105 blowout win over the Bulls on Nov. 4, 2026 at Madison Square Garden. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

The 84 points were also the most the Knicks have ever scored in any half at the current Madison Square Garden.

"I really liked how we started out," coach Mike Brown said. "Spacing the floor well, touching the paint, and then re-spacing too. Our re-spacing was really quick and really decisive. We had some great, great looks early on. And everybody played well offensively.

"Our pace was pretty good, it's something we've been talking about. Our offensive rebounding, for shooting a high percentage, was pretty good. Just a lot of good things."

Entering Wednesday, the Knicks were averaging just 51.6 points in first halves — the third-worst mark in the NBA and better than only the Bucks and Nets. They were shooting a paltry 39.2 percent from the field in first halves — worst in the NBA.

OG Anunoby goes up for a layup during the Knicks' win over the Bulls. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

It led to constant deficits they had to fight back from.

But they shot 62.5 percent (25-for-40) from the field and 50 percent (10-for-20) from 3-point range during Wednesday's first half and led by 32 at the break.

Jalen Brunson had 26 of those 84 first-half points, going a red-hot 8-for-11 from the field and 4-for-6 from 3-point range. OG Anunoby added 18 first-half points.

"Obviously, making 3s, but I think making our two-pointers are really important as well," Brunson said. "… When you have it going, it feels good. Just trying to play off that and use your gravity to make plays. I feel like it really started on the defensive side of the ball, we were able to get stops and get out in transition."

It also helped that the Knicks took a staggering 26 free throws in the first half — making 24 of them. Anunoby was 11-for-12 from the line in the first half, relentlessly attacking the rim.

The Bulls took just six first-half free throws, making five.

The historic start allowed for plenty of late-game rest at a time the Knicks have talked about dealing with physical fatigue given their short offseason. All five starters sat the entire fourth quarter.

A nice luxury thanks to an electric start.

"It was a really good half," Brown said, "in terms of doing what we believe we should be doing offensively as our staples. Everybody was really good in those areas."

#2784. Seahawks QB Drew Lock will look to dominate the Cardinals once again

The Seattle Seahawks announced that starting quarterback Sam Darnold is dealing with quad and rib injuries. It'll be Darnold's second stint on the sidelines this season due to injury. Backup Drew Lock will start against the Arizona Cardinals in Week 9 on Sunday.

Interestingly enough, Lock made his only other start of the season against the Cardinals in Week 2. That occurred after Darnold was injured in the regular-season opener versus the New England Patriots. Lock totally dominated the Cardinals, and he'll look to do it again.

Lock was 19-of-26 in Week 2 versus the Cardinals for 235 yards, three touchdowns, and zero interceptions. He averaged an aggressive 9.0 yards per attempt and his quarterback rating was a near-perfect 139.1.

The Seahawks blew out the Cardinals 31-7. Lock began the game by throwing an explosive 82-yard touchdown to Jaxon Smith-Njigba. He later threw two additional TDs to JSN. Chemistry between Lock and Seattle's go-to receiver won't be an issue versus the Cardinals on Sunday.

Lock looked extremely comfortable operating Brian Fleury's offense in that game. The Cardinals defense hasn't gotten better since then, and they are rumored to trade veterans like Budda Baker and Josh Sweat before the deadline. Lock should toy with the Cardinals in Week 9.

#2783. Diving deeper into Cavs 124-116 loss to Thunder: Cavaliers are too reliant on James Harden

A sloppy start cost the Cleveland Cavaliers in their 124-116 loss to the Oklahoma City Thunder. They've now dropped five straight games to OKC.

It was another day in the office for the reigning back-to-back MVP. SGA got to his spots on the floor, no matter how many defenders they threw his way. This allowed him to score over 40 points for the second time this week against the Cavs.

There was simply nothing Cleveland could do to slow down Gilgeous-Alexander, or really, even make it challenging.

Ajay Mitchell provided timely baskets whenever it seemed like the Cavs were getting any kind of momentum. He served as the offensive engine when SGA was off the court, and as the counterpunch when he was.

27 points on 10-of-17 shooting, 6 assists, 3 rebounds, 1 steal

Mitchell's stat line is a little deceiving. He was an incredibly efficient scorer, but he didn't make a constant impact throughout all four quarters.

The Cavs needed something to wake them up in the first half, but Mitchell couldn't do it. He was a non-factor, as he had just six points in a half the Cavs only had 44.

Something flipped in the second half, particularly down the stretch. Mitchell put up 14 points on 5-of-8 shooting with four assists in the final frame alone. Unfortunately for Cleveland, that all came after the game was effectively decided.

Anything bought from the links helps support Fear the Sword. You can buy the Browns throwback shirt HERE. You can also shop all of Homage's Cleveland gear HERE.

9 points, 0 assists, 1 rebound, 2 turnovers, 6 fouls

Thomas looked like a rookie for one of the first times this season. He struggled to make an impact on the scoreboard and was picked on defensively — particularly by the reigning MVP when he got an opportunity to do so. This led to Thomas fouling out in just 18 minutes.

12 points on 5-for-10 shooting, 8 rebounds

The Cavs need someone to provide tertiary ball-handling, rebounding, and who thinks the game at a high level. Hezonja has consistently supplied that and did so again in Oklahoma City.

I'm not sure why Hezonja doesn't get more minutes than he does, or see the floor in closing situations — especially in a game Jarrett Allen left with an injury. In this one, head coach Kenny Atkinson went with Tyrese Proctor (until he left with an injury) and then with Craig Porter Jr. Having a third guard makes sense for a team that struggles with ball pressure, but can't Hezonja help with that in addition to supplying extra rebounding and shooting? I just don't get it.

Proctor performed well in his second career start. He provided some off-ball shooting and held his own defensively. That's what you want to see from a second-year player who's thrown into the starting lineup.

An injury to Allen forced Enaruna into his first-ever rotation minutes, and he performed admirably. His size and energy on the defensive end were much needed on an evening the Cavs were severely lacking wing defenders due to injuries — even if he wasn't necessarily perfect on that end.

The offensive numbers weren't necessarily impressive. Enaruna went just 2-for-7 from the field, which included going 0-for-3 from distance. However, a couple of those threes went halfway down before popping back out.

Getting serviceable minutes from two-way players is more necessary than ever. Pace, offensive rebounding, and where defenders are picking up their offensive assignments are higher than ever. This has created more injuries. Throw in the second-apron restrictions, and it's almost a necessity for teams to have two-way players (whose salaries don't count against the cap) that can provide fill-in rotation minutes when needed.

This performance doesn't mean Enaruna will be a mainstay in the rotation or anything like that, but it did show that he can provide playable minutes when called upon.

Mitchell was awesome down the stretch, it just came too little too late.

"Dissapointed in our first quarter. Three quarers [were] really solid play, and you have one quarter where you're not up to par. Like I said, the turnoves, the live-ball turnovers, they just get the other team going, and you know, it kind of snow balls."

The Cavs have struggled to stop the bleeding so far this year. There's been one quarter, or extended stretch of play in each of their losses where they let go of the rope. That happened in the first quarter here, when they were beaten 37-20. Cleveland outscored Oklahoma City for each of the next three quarters, but that doesn't matter much when you have a start like this.

Only 20 of Cleveland's 93 shots came at the rim (16th percentile).

The Cavs' three-point volume and accuracy allowed them to have a slightly above league-average offensive output in this game. However, it's nearly impossible to have a truly effective offense if you aren't getting to the rim well. If you aren't getting to the basket, you likely aren't getting to the line either, which was an issue for the Cavs.

This game turned around when Harden did. There was a stretch from the end of the second quarter to the start of the third where Harden looked like the best version of himself for one of the few times this season.

Harden was up to his old tricks during that run. He was able to establish the three-point shot midway through the second quarter, which opened up the rest of his game. The threat of that shot caused defenders to bite too hard on step-backs and made them a step slow when he drove inside. This allowed Harden to be an effective playmaker at all three levels, which isn't something he's been able to do on command so far this campaign. He scored or assisted on 16 points during the Cavs' 18-3 run at the start of the third quarter that allowed Cleveland to make this a moderately competitive game.

The Cavs' success this year hinges on Harden's, which is why things have been so rocky at the start. Harden has posted his career-worst free-throw rate and has struggled to hit anything inside 14 feet. This has handicapped his effectiveness and left him mostly an outside shooter.

Cleveland desperately needs the version of Harden we got a glimpse of here if they want to succeed against high-level teams. Or at the very least, they need that iteration if they're going to be this isolation-heavy with both guards.

The entire offense is predicated on the guards drawing extra defenders to them. If they can do that, the kick-out threes open up, as do windows to get their bigs involved. Conversely, when they aren't getting inside, the ball and player movement can stagnate. This leads to turnovers and settling for contested jump shots — as we've seen during Cleveland's worst stretches.

Relying this heavily on a 37-year-old isn't ideal, but it's where the Cavs find themselves. And they will continue to be at this spot unless they drastically alter their offensive process.

#2781. Bears' Jeffery Simmons will be ready for Good, Better, Best next time

All-Pro defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons is good at a lot of things, but the "Good, Better, Best" chant was not one of them after the Chicago Bears' 34-17 win against the Seattle Seahawks.

Simmons had not even been with the Bears for a week before Monday night's game against Seattle and head coach Ben Johnson's "Good, Better, Best" postgame chant is unlike anything any other head coach in the NFL does. During the footage of his "Good, Better, Best" after Monday's win, it was clear Simmons did not know the words yet. But he has been studying film on YouTube and promised to be ready for Chicago's next game.

"I definitely been on YouTube trying to figure that, make sure I'm good for next game," Simmons said. "Next game, especially I'm in the camera like that, I know the words. I'll be ready."

Luckily, Simmons has his college teammate, edge rusher Montez Sweat, to help him learn the words. They did a lot of winning together at Mississippi State. Simmons also got to experience a winning culture in his first three years in the NFL during his time with the Tennessee Titans. However, the Titans have not had a winning season since 2021, so this is the first time Simmons has gotten to be part of a winning culture in five years.

"When you winning, great things happen and when you're a part of something like that after a game, you're doing something good and to feel the energy, feel the excitement from everybody in the locker room after a game," Simmons said. "It's great to be a part of."

Chicago is currently heavy favorites over the Tampa Bay Buccaneers for their next game on Sunday Night Football. So, barring anything catastrophic, Simmons will get to say the "Good, Better, Best" chant along with the rest of the Bears in the locker room after the game.

#2780. Four Takeaways from the Raptors’ 128-109 loss to Nuggets

The Toronto Raptors continue an up-and-down start to the season without Kawhi Leonard after losing 128-109 to the Denver Nuggets on Wednesday.

With the loss, Toronto falls to 4-4 on the season and 2-4 without Leonard.

Toronto is a poor three-point shooting team. Entering the contest, the Raptors averaged 31.5 per cent from beyond the arc, which ranks 27th in the NBA.

While the perimeter shooting couldn't sustain itself (Toronto finished at 35 per cent), the opening quarter featured a Raptors team that felt dangerous from three. Toronto went an impressive 6-for-12 from the arc. For context, the Raptors averaged 11.5 triples per game.

The opening 12 minutes were a breath of fresh air, especially after the team's abysmal 8-for-29 performance against the Los Angeles Clippers. This season, the league average from three is currently at 34.8 per cent. Toronto has shot better than 34.8 per cent three times in eight games.

Unfortunately, the Raptors shot 6-for-22 the rest of the way.

It should be made clear: tonight's loss was not on Immanuel Quickley.

But it was pointed out on the TSN broadcast that Quickley needs to have a consistent impact through all four quarters. Quickley was brilliant in the opening 12 minutes, finishing with 10 points and six assists while shooting 50 per cent from three. He also added two steals.

By the end of the game, Quickly finished with 13 points and seven assists. The point guard also shot 25 per cent from the perimeter and didn't record another steal.

While we suggested that the Raptors could overwhelm Nikola Jokic and the Nuggets, specifically by leveraging their strengths on the offensive glass, it turns out that the game goes well beyond the battle of the boards. The future Hall of Famer finished with 30 points, 10 rebounds and 11 assists while pummelling the Raptors into submission.

Jokic emerged from halftime ready to shift into another gear. While in the Denver air, Toronto didn't stand a chance. Jokic had already safely recorded his triple-double by the end of the third quarter. In the same period, he tied the entire Raptors team in points with 15.

Referencing the Colorado air might also be doing Jokic a disservice. The big man was ultimately responsible for 26 of the Nuggets' 33 points in the first quarter.

The Nuggets had limited the Raptors to seven fast-break points with just over three minutes remaining in the third quarter. Stopping Toronto in transition was likely a focal point for Denver head coach David Adelman and his staff. The Raptors entered the game second in transition points at 19.7 per game.

For the Raptors to salvage their four-game road trip, they'll need to either get back to what they do best on the breaks or find ways to score in the half-court. Their next opponent, the Milwaukee Bucks, is 11th in fastbreak points allowed per game at 13.3
